Unstable cohomology of 𝖦𝖫 2 ​ n ​ ( ℤ ) \mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z}) and the odd commutative graph complex
Francis Brown, Simone Hu, Erik Panzer

Abstract
We study a closed differential form on the symmetric space of positive definite matrices, which is defined using the Pfaffian and is 𝖦𝖫 2 ​ n ​ ( ℤ ) \mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z}) invariant up to a sign. It gives rise to an infinite family of unstable classes in the compactly-supported cohomology of the locally symmetric space for 𝖦𝖫 2 ​ n ​ ( ℤ ) \mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z}) with coefficients in the orientation bundle. Furthermore, by applying the Pfaffian forms to the dual Laplacian of graphs, and integrating them over the space of edge lengths, we construct an infinite family of cocycles for the odd commutative graph complex. By explicit computation, we show that the first such cocycle gives a non-trivial class in H − 6 ​ ( 𝖦𝖢 3 ) H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3}) .



1
Introduction

The differential forms studied in this paper are constructed out of what we call the invariant Pfaffian form. It is defined on the space
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
of positive definite matrices
X
of even rank
2n
by the formula

\phi^{2n}_{X}=\frac{\Pf(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X)}{\sqrt{\det(X)}}\ ,

where
\Pf
denotes the Pfaffian of a skew-symmetric matrix.
It is a closed smooth differential form of degree
2n
with many good properties. In particular, it transforms by

\phi^{2n}_{P^{\intercal}XP}=\sgn\left(\det(P)\right)\phi^{2n}_{X}

(1.1)

for any
P\in\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
. It therefore defines a differential form on the locally symmetric space
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
with coefficients in the orientation bundle
\mathcal{O}
, which is the rank one real vector bundle induced by the determinant representation of
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
. We call such a form an orientation form.
Importantly,
\phi^{2n}
is also invariant under the action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}
on
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
by scalar multiplication, and therefore descends to the quotient
\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{2n}
.

By taking exterior products with the invariant differential forms

\beta^{4k+1}_{X}=\tr\left((X^{-1}\mathrm{d}X)^{4k+1}\right)

(1.2)

for
k\geq 1
, we obtain many further orientation forms on
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
. For example, we show that the volume form (a non-vanishing orientation form of top degree)
on the space of projective classes of positive definite symmetric matrices
is proportional to the product
\beta^{5}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}
. The invariant Pfaffian therefore gives a factorisation of the volume form.

In this paper, we use these orientation forms for two different purposes. On the one hand, we show that those of
compact type
give rise to infinitely many non-zero classes in the compactly-supported cohomology of
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{O}
. We show, furthermore, that they are primitive with respect to a Hopf algebra structure on
\bigoplus_{n}H^{\bullet}_{c}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})
recently introduced in
[
1
]
, and deduce that the latter contains the symmetric algebra generated by the orientation forms of compact type.

On the other hand, we use the Pfaffian orientation forms which are
not of compact type
to construct cocycles on the odd commutative graph complex by interpreting them as orientation forms on a moduli space of metric graphs. By integrating over cells indexed by graphs, we assign transcendental invariants to elements in the odd graph complex and compute them explicitly for a closed linear combination of graphs with
6
loops and
12
edges. This proves that the first of our cocycles defines a non-trivial class in
H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
.

We emphasize that the orientation forms we use to construct cohomology classes for
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
(which are of ‘compact type’), and for the odd graph complex (which are of ‘non-compact type’), are not the same, with exactly one exception (namely
\phi^{2}
).

1.1
Unstable cohomology of
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})

The Pfaffian forms can be used to write down explicit representatives for the Poincaré duals of the cohomology classes for
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
generated by the invariant forms
\beta
(of non-compact type) in the unstable range. More precisely, let

\Omega_{nc}^{\bullet}(2n-1)=\ExtA^{\bullet}\bigg(\bigoplus_{1\leq k<n-1}\mathbb{Q}\,\beta^{4k+1}\bigg)

denote the graded exterior algebra generated by formal symbols
\beta^{4k+1}
in degree
4k+1
corresponding to the invariant differential forms (
1.2
). The subscript
nc
stands for ‘non-compact type’. It was proven in
[
23
]
that

\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}(2n-1)\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{m}/\mathsf{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\quad\hbox{ is injective for all }m\geq 2n-1\

(1.3)

having been announced in
[
48
]
(see also
[
35
]
).
Together with results of Matsushima and Garland this implies Borel’s theorem (see
[
11
]
and references therein) which states that the stable cohomology of
\mathsf{GL}(\mathbb{Z})
is generated by the graded exterior algebra with one element in degree
4k+1
, for each
k\geq 1
.

Now define a bigraded vector space
\Omega^{\bullet}_{c}[\phi](2n)
of orientation forms, which we call of ‘compact type’, which is
generated by the symbols
\phi^{2}
and

\beta^{4i_{1}+1}\wedge\beta^{4i_{2}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4i_{k}+1}\wedge\phi^{2n}

(1.4)

for all
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}
where
i_{k}=n-1.
The grading is by genus and degree minus genus, where the generator (
1.4
) has degree
(4i_{1}+1)+\ldots+(4i_{k}+1)+(2n+1)
and genus
2n
, and
\phi^{2}
has degree
3
and genus
2
.

As a first application of Pfaffian forms, we show that they can be interpreted as compactly supported cohomology classes: we define classes denoted

\displaystyle[(\phi^{2})_{c}]

\displaystyle\in H_{c}^{3}(\mathcal{P}_{2}/\mathsf{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})

and

\displaystyle[(\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n})_{c}]

\displaystyle\in H^{6n-2}_{c}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})

for all
n>1
,

and we write
(\phi^{2})_{c}
,
(\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n})_{c}
for any compactly supported forms (representatives) in such a class. The key point of our construction is that while the orientation forms themselves do
not
have compact support, they can be interpreted as canonical
relative
forms on a compactification of
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
. In particular, integrals like

\int_{\sigma}\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}

converge even for locally finite,
\mathcal{O}
-oriented chains
\sigma
on
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
.

All the cohomology classes thus obtained are non-trivial and independent:

Theorem 1.1
.

For all
n>1
, we obtain a canonical injective map of graded vector spaces

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet}_{c}[\phi](2n)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}_{c}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})

(1.5)

\displaystyle\beta^{4i_{1}+1}\wedge\beta^{4i_{2}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle[\beta^{4i_{1}+1}\wedge\beta^{4i_{2}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge(\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n})_{c}]

The images of these classes pair non-trivially, under Poincaré duality, with the image of (
1.3
) when
m=2n
. For
n=1
, the class
[(\phi^{2})_{c}]
generates
H_{c}^{3}(\mathcal{P}_{2}/\mathsf{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})\cong\mathbb{R}
.

Note that the compactly supported twisted cohomology of
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
is Poincaré dual to the ordinary cohomology of
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
via

H^{k}(\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\cong H^{k}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\cong\left(H_{c}^{d_{2n}-k}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})\right)^{\vee}

where
d_{m}=m(m+1)/2

and furthermore appears as a summand in the cohomology of the special linear group by an application of Shapiro’s lemma which implies that:

H^{k}(\mathsf{SL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{Q})\cong H^{k}(\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{k}(\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\det)

where
\det
denotes the determinant representation.

The proof of theorem
1.1
involves studying the asymptotic behaviour of the forms
\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}
and proving that they extend to an algebro-geometric incarnation of the Borel-Serre compactification
[
23
]
, and vanish along the boundary.

1.1.1
Four spaces of differential forms

The relationship between canonical forms, the Pfaffian invariant form, and different cohomology groups associated to the locally symmetric space of
\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
may be summarised as follows.
Let
g>1
be odd, and consider the four graded vector spaces:

(i)

\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}(g)=\ExtA^{\bullet}\left(\mathbb{Q}\beta^{5}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{Q}\beta^{2g-5}\right)

(ii)

\Omega^{\bullet}_{c}(g)=\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}(g)\wedge\beta^{2g-1}

(iii)

\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}[\phi](g+1)=\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}(g)\wedge\phi^{g+1}

(iv)

\Omega^{\bullet}_{c}[\phi](g+1)=\Omega^{\bullet}_{c}(g)\wedge\phi^{g+1}

The first space,
\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}(g)
has the structure of a graded algebra, but the others are to be viewed merely as graded vector spaces. The subscript
c
stands for ‘compact type’, and
nc
for ‘non-compact type’.
When
n=g
is odd, the locally symmetric space
\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
is orientable and we have embeddings of graded vector spaces
[
23
]
:

\Omega_{nc}^{\bullet}(g)\hookrightarrow H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\qquad\ ,\qquad\Omega_{c}^{\bullet}(g)\hookrightarrow H_{c}^{\bullet+1}(\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\ .

The two spaces
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})
and
H_{c}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})
are related by Poincaré duality, which is manifested by the Hodge star operator which relates
\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}
to
\Omega^{\bullet}_{c}
.

When
2n=g+1
is even, the orbifold
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
is not orientable and all spaces (i)–(iv) are implicated. We have a pair of injections of graded vector spaces

\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}(2n-1)\hookrightarrow H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\qquad,\qquad\Omega^{\bullet}_{c}[\phi](2n)\hookrightarrow H_{c}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})

which are related by Poincaré duality. The first injection is a consequence of (
1.3
), the second is the statement of
Theorem

1.1
. In addition, we have maps

\Omega^{\bullet}_{c}(2n-1)\hookrightarrow H_{c}^{\bullet+2}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\qquad,\qquad\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}[\phi](2n)\mapsto 0\in H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})

where the first map is the inflation morphism of
[
14
]
, and was shown to be injective in
[
23
]
. The fact that
\Omega_{nc}^{\bullet}[\phi](2n)
maps to zero in
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})
follows from the fact
[
60
]
that the cohomology class of
[\phi^{2n}]
in
H^{2n}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})
vanishes.

1.1.2
Symmetric algebra on Pfaffian invariant forms of compact type

It was recently shown in
[
1
]
that the bigraded
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space

H^{\det}=\bigoplus_{k,n\geq 0}H_{k}(\mathsf{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathrm{St}_{n}(\mathbb{Q})\otimes\det)

where
\mathrm{St}_{n}(\mathbb{Q})
denotes the Steinberg module and
\det
the determinant representation, has the structure of a bigraded commutative Hopf algebra. Its graded linear dual
H_{\det}=(H^{\det})^{\vee}
is a cocommutative bigraded Hopf algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
. Since the dualising module for
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
is
\mathrm{St}_{2n}(\mathbb{Q})\otimes\det
(
[
10
, §11.4]
, see, e.g.
[
34
, §7.2]
,
[
55
]
), we obtain an isomorphism

H_{\det}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H^{\bullet}_{c}\left(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O}\right)

since the local system
\mathcal{O}
is given by the representation
\det\otimes\mathbb{R}
in the even rank case.

k-2n

\mathsf{GL}_{8}(\mathbb{Z})

\mathsf{GL}_{6}(\mathbb{Z})

\mathsf{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})

\mathsf{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

1

[\phi^{2}]

6

[\beta^{5}\wedge\phi^{4}]

7

\pagecolor{emph}[\phi^{2}]\cdot[\beta^{5}\wedge\phi^{4}]

10

[\beta^{9}\wedge\phi^{6}]

11

\pagecolor{emph}[\phi^{2}]\cdot[\beta^{9}\wedge\phi^{6}]

12

\pagecolor{emph}[\beta^{5}\wedge\phi^{4}]^{2}

[\beta^{13}\wedge\phi^{8}]

15

[\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}\wedge\phi^{6}]

\pagecolor{emph}[\phi^{2}]\cdot[\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}\wedge\phi^{6}]

Table 1:
Cohomology classes in
H_{c}^{k}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})
obtained from
Theorem

1.2
, for small
n
. By Poincaré duality, which reverses the degrees in each column, one deduces classes in the cohomology of
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
. The highlighted classes denote non-trivial products with respect to the Hopf algebra structure of
[
1
]
.

By showing that the Pfaffian forms of compact type are primitive for the commutative coproduct, we deduce from the Milnor-Moore and Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorems that there exists a
large amount of new unstable cohomology for
\mathsf{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
for even
n
.

Theorem 1.2
.

There is an embedding of bigraded vector spaces

\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}\left(\Omega_{c}[\phi]\right)\otimes\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H^{\bullet}_{c}\left(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O}\right)

(1.6)

from the symmetric algebra on the bigraded vector space
\Omega_{c}[\phi]=\bigoplus_{n}\Omega_{c}[\phi](2n)
into the compactly supported cohomology of the locally symmetric space for
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
with twisted coefficients. The former is isomorphic (as a bigraded vector space) to the tensor product of the free commutative polynomial ring on its generators in even degrees, with the graded exterior algebra on those in odd degrees.

Dually, there is a surjective map of bigraded vector spaces

\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}H^{d_{2n}-\bullet}\left(\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R}\right)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(\Omega_{c}[\phi])^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{R}\qquad\text{where}\ d_{2n}=\binom{2n+1}{2}\ .

(1.7)

See
table

1
for an illustration.
We note that
Theorem

1.2
explains all homology classes of
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
in the range
2n\leq 6
where it has been computed in all degrees
[
34
]
, and consistent with
[
58
]
. Note that there is some freedom in the choice of map (
1.6
): it can be any choice of splitting of the length grading which arises in the Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorem. If one chooses the map (
1.6
) to be given by (graded) symmetrised products of length
n
with respect to the Hopf algebra structure on
H_{\det}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
(with the appropriate prefactor
1/n!
) then it may be promoted to a map of bigraded coalgebras. The corresponding dual map (
1.7
) is then a map of bigraded algebras.

In particular, it follows that the graded Lie bracket (antisymmetrised product) of
[\phi^{2}]
and
[\beta^{5}\wedge\phi^{4}]
necessarily vanishes in
H^{7}_{c}(\mathcal{P}_{6}/\mathsf{GL}_{6}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})
since it has rank 1, i.e., they commute.
It is an interesting open question, therefore, whether the Lie algebra generated by the classes in
\Omega_{c}[\phi]
is abelian or not: we do not know of any non-trivial Lie brackets at present.

1.2
Cochains in the odd commutative graph complex

The commutative graph complexes
\mathcal{GC}_{N}
introduced by Kontsevich
[
43
]
have numerous applications in deformation theory
[
65
]
, geometry
[
25
]
, and topology.
Thanks to isomorphisms
\mathcal{GC}_{N}\cong\mathcal{GC}_{N+2}
, there are essentially two cases:
N
even and
N
odd.

In this paper we consider the odd graph complex
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
.
1
1

1

Geometrically,
H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})
can be realized as equivariant relative homology of the simplicial closure of outer space twisted by the determinant representation
[
29
, Proposition 27]
, or as twisted homology of the moduli space of metric graphs
[
6
, Example 5.9]
(see also
[
42
, bottom of p. 6]
).

A connected combinatorial graph
G
with
n
vertices and
m
edges has
loop number

\ell=m-n+1
and
degree

k=m-3\ell
.
As a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space,
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
is spanned by isomorphism classes of such graphs, equipped with an orientation. As a chain complex, its boundary
\partial G=\sum_{e}G/e
contracts edges, which is homogeneous of degrees
(0,-1)
for the bigrading by
(\ell,k)
.
The dual cochain complex
\mathsf{GC}_{3}\cong\Hom(\mathcal{GC}_{3},\mathbb{Q})
has a coboundary
\delta
and a compatible (graded) Lie bracket.
The cohomology
H^{k}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})=\prod_{\ell}\gr_{\ell}H^{k}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
is known by
[
66
, Lemma 1.4]
to vanish outside the region

-\ell\leq k\leq-3,

(1.8)

with the sole exception of the class
\gr_{2}H^{-3}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})=\mathbb{Q}[D_{3}]
spanned by the theta graph, which we denote
D_{3}
. It provides the first class in the cohomology group
H^{-3}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
at the upper bound
k=-3
. The group
H^{-3}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
has an additional algebraic structure and is also called the
algebra of 3-graphs

[
33
]
and has been studied extensively due to its role in the enumeration of Vassiliev knot invariants. In lower degrees, however, only very few classes are known, as summarized in
Table

2
, even though there must exist many cohomology classes for large
\ell

[
12
]
.

Table 2:

Dimensions of the cohomology
\gr_{\ell}H^{k}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
, bigraded by degree
k
(columns) and loop number
\ell
(rows), known from computer calculations
[
3
,
40
,
24
]
.
Empty cells indicate trivial zeroes (no graphs), whereas

0

indicates vanishing in the range
k<-\ell
known from (
1.8
).
The column
k=-3
highlights the algebra of 3-graphs; e.g.
\gr_{2}H^{-3}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\cong\mathbb{Q}
is spanned by the theta graph.

Generalizing a key idea in
[
22
]
, we construct linear functionals
I(\omega)\colon\mathcal{GC}_{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, in other words cochains (elements of
\mathsf{GC}_{3}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathbb{R}
), by integrating the pullback of differential forms on
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
to a moduli space of metric graphs via the tropical Torelli map
[
15
]
.

Concretely, to a graph
G
we associate the space
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
parametrizing positive edge lengths. For any basis
\mathcal{C}
of the cycle space
H_{1}(G)
, the dual graph Laplacian
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
is a positive definite symmetric
\ell\times\ell
matrix whose entries are linear in the edge lengths.
Pulling back under
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
, we obtain smooth differential forms

\omega_{G}\wedge\phi_{G}=\omega_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}\wedge\phi^{\ell}_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}

on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
, for any polynomial
\omega
in the forms (
1.2
). In fact, these pullbacks are projectively invariant and descend to smooth forms on the open simplex
\sigma_{G}=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}/\mathbb{R}_{+}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
.

For example, the theta graph has
n=2
vertices and
m=3
edges, hence
\ell=2
loops and degree
k=-3
. The dual Laplacian for the cycle basis
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},C_{2})
as indicated in

D_{3}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcD3cyc}}}\qquad\text{is}\qquad\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}+x_{3}&x_{3}\\
x_{3}&x_{2}+x_{3}\end{pmatrix}

in terms of the edge length variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
. Computing the invariant Pfaffian form
\phi^{2}
for this family of positive definite matrices provides the projective 2-form

\phi_{D_{3}}=-\frac{x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{3}-x_{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{3}+x_{3}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{(x_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}x_{3}+x_{1}x_{3})^{3/2}}.

Theorem 1.3
.

For any homogeneous polynomial
\omega
of degree
m-\ell-1
in the canonical forms (
1.2
), and any oriented graph
G\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
with loop number
\ell
, the integral

I_{G}(\omega)=\frac{1}{(-2\pi)^{\ell/2}}\int_{\sigma_{G}}\omega_{G}\wedge\phi_{G}

(1.9)

is absolutely convergent and defines a linear function
I(\omega)\colon\mathcal{GC}_{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},G\mapsto I_{G}(\omega)
.

The convergence follows from the asymptotic behaviour of the forms at the boundary of a suitable compactification
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
of the simplex
\sigma_{G}
. To define
I_{G}(\omega)
, we also have to specify the orientation of
\sigma_{G}
and fix the sign of
\phi_{G}
, since the latter depends implicitly on the cycle basis
\mathcal{C}
. These ambiguities are resolved by the notion of orientation of a graph
G
in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
, which by
[
29
]
amounts to a choice of generator of
\det\mathbb{Z}^{m}\otimes\det H_{1}(G)
where, for a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module
M
of finite rank
n
,
\det(M)
denotes the rank one
\mathbb{Z}
-module
\ExtA^{n}M
. The sign in (
1.1
) is therefore crucial for our construction.

We thus obtain canonical integrals, and hence numbers, associated to oriented graphs in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
. In the simplest case,
\omega=1
so we integrate only the bare Pfaffian form. For example,
\phi_{D_{3}}
defined above integrates to
I_{D_{3}}(1)=1
for the theta graph. Our normalization of
I_{G}(\omega)
by powers of
-2\pi
ensures that
I_{G}(1)\in\{-1,0,1\}
for all graphs, since we show that
I_{G}(1)=\pm 1
if and only if
G\cong\pm D_{2k+1}
is isomorphic to a dipole graph
D_{2k+1}
with even loop number
2k
.
These are the graphs on
2
vertices with
2k+1
edges:

D_{3}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD3}}},\qquad D_{5}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD5}}},\qquad D_{7}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD7}}},\qquad\ldots

For non-trivial
\omega
, the integrals (
1.9
) are much more complicated. An example for an integral of the 11-form
\beta^{5}\wedge\phi^{6}
is (this graph is labelled
G_{234}
in
appendix

A
)

G=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc234}}}\quad\mapsto\quad\begin{aligned} I_{G}(\beta^{5})&=-80+\tfrac{140}{9}\pi^{2}-\tfrac{4}{3}\pi^{4}+210\zeta(3)-20\pi^{2}\ln 2\\
&\quad+\tfrac{20}{3}\left[(\ln 2)^{4}+24\operatorname{Li}_{4}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\right]-\tfrac{40}{9}\left[\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})\right]\end{aligned}

where
\mathcal{C}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{2}(\mathrm{i})
denotes Catalan’s constant and
\operatorname{Li}_{s}(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}z^{k}/k^{s}
is the polylogarithm function.

1.2.1
Stokes’ relations and cocycles

In the context of graphs, the relevant algebra of ‘canonical’ forms is
\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
,
the exterior algebra generated by formal symbols
\beta^{4k+1}
, one for each positive integer
k
. The above integrals of orientation forms constitute a linear map

I\colon\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\Hom(\mathcal{GC}_{3},\mathbb{R}).

Since
\Hom(\mathcal{GC}_{3},\mathbb{R})\cong\mathsf{GC}_{3}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathbb{R}
, we can apply the coboundary
\delta
and the Lie bracket of
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
to the cochains
I(\omega)
. Stokes’ theorem for the manifold with boundary
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
relates the coboundary of such cochains to Lie brackets. To state this result, we declare

\Delta\beta^{4k+1}=1\otimes\beta^{4k+1}+\beta^{4k+1}\otimes 1

to endow
\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
with the structure of a graded cocommutative Hopf algebra. This coproduct encodes the restrictions of canonical forms to the boundary faces at infinity of
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
.
We use Sweedler’s notation
\sum_{(\omega)}\omega^{\prime}\otimes\omega^{\prime\prime}
to denote the reduced coproduct, such that

\Delta\omega=1\otimes\omega+\omega\otimes 1+\sum_{(\omega)}\omega^{\prime}\otimes\omega^{\prime\prime}.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}
denote the following sum of all even loop number dipoles:

\mathfrak{m}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{D_{2k+1}}{2\cdot(2k+1)!}.

Write
\lvert\omega\rvert
for the degree of
\omega
. Then, for any canonical form
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
, we have the relation

0=\delta I(\omega)+[I(\omega),\mathfrak{m}]+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{|\omega^{\prime}|}[I(\omega^{\prime\prime}),I(\omega^{\prime})].

(1.10)

The dipole sum
\mathfrak{m}
arises as the integral
I(1)
of the bare Pfaffian form in the trivial piece
1\otimes\omega+\omega\otimes 1
of the coproduct
\Delta\omega
.
Specializing to
\omega=1
, Stokes’ relation is equivalent to the Maurer-Cartan equation

\delta\mathfrak{m}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}]=0

which was discovered combinatorially in
[
40
]
. We thus obtain a geometric explanation for the origin of the corresponding twisted differential
\delta+[\cdot,\mathfrak{m}]
on
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
, tracing it back to the structure of the boundary of the compactifications
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
.

Specializing to primitive canonical forms
\omega=\beta^{4i+1}
, the Stokes’ relation (
1.10
) shows that the corresponding cocycle is closed with respect to the twisted differential:

\delta I(\beta^{4i+1})=-[I(\beta^{4i+1}),\mathfrak{m}].

To obtain
bona fide
cocycles for the original differential
\delta
in
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
, let
\gr_{\ell}I(\omega)
denote the restriction of the cochain
I(\omega)
from
\mathcal{GC}_{3}=\bigoplus_{\ell}\gr_{\ell}\mathcal{GC}_{3}
to the part in loop number
\ell
.

Lemma 1.5
.

Write
\lceil x\rceil
for the smallest integer above or equal to
x
. For a canonical form
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{d}
of degree
d
, the integrals
\gr_{\ell}I(\omega)=0
vanish in all loop orders
\ell<2+2\lceil d/4\rceil
.

For example, this result shows that
I_{G}(1)=0
for graphs with one loop, and
I_{G}(\beta^{5})=0
for graphs with
\ell<6
loops. Since the Lie bracket respects the grading by
\ell
, Stokes’ relations simplify drastically in the minimal loop order where
I(\omega)
might be non-zero.

Corollary 1.6
.

For every canonical form
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{d}
of degree
d
, the cochain
I(\omega)
restricts in loop number
2+2\lceil d/4\rceil
to a cocycle
\gr_{2+2\lceil d/4\rceil}I(\omega)\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}
with respect to
\delta
.

We have thus explicitly defined infinitely many classes in the cohomology of
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
, one for each monomial in the forms
\beta^{4i+1}
. They lie in degrees
-6\leq k\leq-3
related to the degree
d
of the form
\omega
by
k\equiv d+1\mod 4
. We do not know in general which of these classes are zero, but we give examples of zero- and non-zero cases.

The simplest such cocycle is
\gr_{2}I(1)
, that is, the integral of the Pfaffian form
\phi^{2}
, in degree
k=-3
. By
I_{D_{3}}(1)=1
, the corresponding cohomology class

[\gr_{2}I(1)]\in H^{-3}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})

is non-trivial and dual to the class
[D_{3}]\in H_{-3}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})
of the theta graph in homology.

For primitive
\omega=\beta^{4i+1}
, the cocycles lie in degree
k=-6
and define classes

\big[\gr_{4+2i}I(\beta^{4i+1})\big]\in H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\otimes\mathbb{R}.

We computed a cycle
X\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
whose class spans
\gr_{6}H_{-6}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})\cong\mathbb{Q}
. It consists of a linear combination of 20 graphs with 12 edges, 6 loops, and 7 vertices each (
fig.

4
). Calculating explicitly each of the corresponding integrals
I_{G}(\beta^{5})
, we found that
I_{X}(\beta^{5})\neq 0
:

Theorem 1.7
.

The class
[\gr_{6}I(\beta^{5})]\in H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\otimes\mathbb{R}
is non-zero.

Since
\gr_{6}H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\cong\mathbb{Q}
is known to have dimension one (see
table

2
), it is spanned by this class. We have thus explained the first non-trivial cohomology group of
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
in degrees below
-3
in terms of the non-compactly supported orientation form
\beta^{5}\wedge\phi^{6}
. Explicitly, we can represent this graph cohomology class using only two graphs:
2
2

2

Some integrals were calculated numerically, see
appendix

A
. The identification of the numerical factor
2\pi^{2}\log 2-13\zeta(3)\approx 4.112
is thus strictly confirmed only to 40 digits of precision, which we consider ample for this purpose. To establish the non-vanishing (
Theorem

1.7
), two digits would have sufficed.

\left[\gr_{6}I(\beta^{5})\right]=10\cdot\Big(2\pi^{2}\ln 2-13\zeta(3)\Big)\cdot\left[2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc288}}}+3\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc287}}}\right].

This class is in the image of the Lie bracket
[H^{-3},H^{-3}]\subseteq H^{-6}
on the cohomology of
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
. It is proportional to the bracket
[[K_{4}],[K_{4}]]=-2[[Y_{3}],[D_{3}]]
of the complete graph
K_{4}
with itself, as well as the bracket of the triangular prism
Y_{3}
with the theta graph
D_{3}
.

The first product,
\omega=\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}
, yields a cocycle
\gr_{10}I(\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9})
supported in
10
loops and degree
k=-5
. This cocycle is exact, since the cohomology in this bidegree is trivial (see
table

2
). However, we will explain how this exactness can be exploited to adjust the cochain
\gr_{12}I(\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9})
so as to obtain a cocycle in bidegree
(\ell,k)=(12,-9)
. While we cannot presently infer if this class is non-trivial, this kind of mechanism suggests that out of the cochains
I(\omega)
, one can construct a large number of cocycles (going beyond the restrictions of
I(\omega)
to graphs of smallest loop order as in
lemma

1.5
). We hope that those will prove useful to construct further classes in
H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
.

1.3
Comments

The orientation forms
\Omega_{c}[\phi]
of compact type, and those of non-compact type
\Omega_{nc}[\phi]
, play opposite roles in our construction of cohomology classes for
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
on the one hand, and for the odd graph complex
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
on the other hand:

•

Compact type forms
\omega\wedge\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}\in\Omega_{c}[\phi](2n)
provide lots of classes in
H^{\bullet}_{c}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})
by
Theorem

1.2
, but the corresponding integrals
\gr_{2n}I(\omega\wedge\beta^{4n-3})
in
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
are all zero by
lemma

1.5
.

•

Non-compact type forms
\omega\wedge\phi^{2n}\in\Omega_{nc}[\phi](2n)
map to zero in
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})
by
[
60
,
7
]
, but their integrals
\gr_{2n}I(\omega)
can give rise to non-trivial classes in
H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
, see
Theorem

1.7
.

This picture is starkly different from the case of the
even
graph complex
\mathsf{GC}_{2}
.
There, an infinite family of explicit non-zero classes in
H^{0}(\mathsf{GC}_{2})
was constructed using integrals over vertex positions in
[
56
]
, and independently as integrals
\int_{\sigma_{G}}\omega_{G}
over edge lengths in
[
22
,
21
]
.
In the latter construction, the integrands
\omega
used for graphs with
\ell=2i+1
loops are the primitive forms of
compact
type
\beta^{4i+1}\in\Omega_{c}(2i+1)
. These cocycles pair non-trivially with the wheel cycles
[
21
]
and conjecturally generate the whole of
H^{0}(\mathsf{GC}_{2})\otimes\mathbb{R}
as a Lie algebra. One expects that the entirety of
H^{0}(\mathsf{GC}_{2})
is pulled back from the compactly supported cohomology of
\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{Z})
, for
\ell
odd
[
20
]
.

The even and odd cases also seem to differ in the kind of numbers that emerge from the integrals. As the example above shows, integrals of graphs in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
can produce polylogarithms at 4th roots of unity and mix different weights (see
appendix

A
). In contrast, all canonical integrals computed to date for
\mathcal{GC}_{2}
are multiple zeta values, of homogeneous weight. However, in the odd case, we observed cancellations such that the canonical integral of the
cycle

X\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
is much simpler, with homogeneous weight 3 and no 4th roots (see
Theorem

6.18
).

2
The Pfaffian form on positive definite matrices

We denote
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell(\ell+1)/2}
the subset of symmetric
\ell\times\ell
matrices
X=X^{\intercal}
which are positive definite. This space is homeomorphic to the space of right cosets of the general linear group
\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
by its compact subgroup
\mathsf{O}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
of orthogonal matrices, via

\mathsf{O}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})\backslash\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathcal{P}_{\ell},\qquad\mathsf{O}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})g\mapsto g^{\intercal}g.

Thus
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
is equipped with a transitive right action
X\mapsto g^{\intercal}Xg
by
\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
.
The
primitive canonical forms

\beta^{4k+1}\in\Omega^{4k+1}(\mathcal{P}_{\ell})
studied in
[
22
, §4]
are smooth differential forms on
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
of degree
4k+1
defined for every integer
k\geq 0
by

\beta^{4k+1}\coloneqq\tr\left((X^{-1}\mathrm{d}X)^{4k+1}\right).

(2.1)

They are closed and invariant under
\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
. In fact, by the Hopf-Koszul-Samelson theorem
they generate the entire subalgebra of invariant forms, which is the graded exterior algebra

\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{\ell})^{\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})}=\ExtA^{\bullet}\left(\mathbb{R}\beta^{1}\oplus\mathbb{R}\beta^{5}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{R}\beta^{4\lfloor\frac{\ell-1}{2}\rfloor+1}\right).

Except for
\beta^{1}=\mathrm{d}\log\det X
, these forms are also projective, by which we mean the vanishing
\mathcal{E}\lrcorner\beta^{4k+1}=0
of the interior product with the Euler vector field

\mathcal{E}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq\ell}X_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{ij}}

(2.2)

for all
k\geq 1
. This vector field is tangent to the orbits of the centre
\mathbb{R}^{\times}\subset\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
, which consists of multiples
\lambda I
of the identity and acts by positive scalings
X\mapsto\lambda^{2}X
. The forms
\beta^{4k+1}
with
k\geq 1
therefore descend to (i.e. are pullbacks of) forms on the quotient

L\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{\ell}/\mathbb{R}^{\times}\cong\mathsf{SL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})/\mathsf{SO}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}).

(2.3)

We call this quotient “the
link
of
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
”. It follows that its invariant forms are

\Omega^{\bullet}(L\mathcal{P}_{\ell})^{\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})}=\ExtA^{\bullet}\left(\mathbb{R}\beta^{5}\oplus\mathbb{R}\beta^{9}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{R}\beta^{4\lfloor\frac{\ell-1}{2}\rfloor+1}\right).

(2.4)

The purpose of this section is to enlarge this picture by allowing forms which are invariant under
X\mapsto g^{\intercal}Xg
if
\det g>0
, but change sign if
\det g<0
.

2.1
The invariant Pfaffian

The Pfaffian of a skew-symmetric
2n\times 2n
matrix
M
with entries in a commutative ring
R
is the following homogeneous polynomial of degree
n
in the entries of
M
:

\Pf(M)=\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{2n}}\sgn\pi\cdot M_{\pi(1)\pi(2)}\cdots M_{\pi(2n-1)\pi(2n)}

(2.5)

where the sum is over the group
\mathfrak{S}_{2n}
of permutations of
\{1,\ldots,2n\}
.
For a square matrix of odd dimension
(2n+1)\times(2n+1)
, we set
\Pf(M)=0
.

We will use several well-known properties of the Pfaffian:

(P1)

\det(M)=\Pf(M)^{2}
,

(P2)

\Pf(\lambda M)=\lambda^{n}\Pf(M)
for any
\lambda\in R
,

(P3)

\Pf(P^{\intercal}MP)=\det(P)\Pf(M)
for any
2n\times 2n
matrix
P
with entries in
R
,

(P4)

\Pf(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})=\Pf(M_{1})\Pf(M_{2})
, where
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}M_{1}&0\\
0&M_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
denotes a block diagonal matrix.

Since positive definite matrices
X\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}
are invertible, we can consider on
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
the matrix

\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X.

Its entries are smooth
2
-forms, so they commute. This matrix is also skew-symmetric (which follows from the symmetry
X=X^{\intercal}
). Therefore, its Pfaffian is defined.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
denote the space of positive definite symmetric
n\times n
matrices
X
with real entries. We define a smooth differential form
\phi^{n}\in\Omega^{n}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
of degree
n
on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, called the
Pfaffian form
, as follows: If
n
is odd, we set
\phi^{n}=0
. For
n
even, set

\phi^{n}_{X}=\phi_{X}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det{X}}}\Pf\left(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right).

(2.6)

Here
\sqrt{\det X}
denotes the positive square root of
\det X>0
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The Pfaffian form satisfies the following properties:

(i)

\phi_{X}=0
if
X
is odd-dimensional.

(ii)

\phi_{X}\wedge\phi_{X}=0
.

(iii)

\phi_{P^{\intercal}XP}=\sgn(\det{P})\cdot\phi_{X}
for any matrix
P\in\mathsf{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
with real entries.

(iv)

\phi_{X^{-1}}=\phi_{X}
.

(v)

\phi_{X_{1}\oplus X_{2}}=\phi_{X_{1}}\wedge\phi_{X_{2}}=\phi_{X_{2}}\wedge\phi_{X_{1}}
.

(vi)

\phi_{X}=\phi_{\lambda X}
is basic for the scaling action
X\mapsto\lambda X
of
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

(vii)

\mathrm{d}\phi=0
.

Proof.

Most of these properties follow directly from those of the Pfaffian.

(i)

By definition.

(ii)

As the Pfaffian squares to the determinant
(P1)
,

\phi_{X}\wedge\phi_{X}=(\det{X})^{-1}\det\left(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right)=\det\left(X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right).

Let
M=X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X
which has commuting entries.
Since
\det M
is a polynomial in the traces of powers of
M
, it suffices to show that
\tr(M^{k})=0
for all
k
.
This is
[
22
, Lemma 4.3]
with
\tr(M^{k})=\beta_{X}^{2k}
.

(iii)

Such an invertible real matrix
P
is in particular constant (
\mathrm{d}P=0
). Therefore

\mathrm{d}(P^{\intercal}XP)\cdot(P^{\intercal}XP)^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}(P^{\intercal}XP)=P^{\intercal}\left(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right)P

and so by applying
(P3)
we obtain

\phi_{P^{\intercal}XP}=\frac{\det{P}}{\sqrt{(\det{P})^{2}\det{X}}}\Pf\left(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right)=\sgn(\det{P})\cdot\phi_{X}.

(iv)

Notice that
0=\mathrm{d}(X\cdot X^{-1})=\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}+X\cdot\mathrm{d}X^{-1}
and thus

\mathrm{d}X^{-1}\cdot X\cdot\mathrm{d}X^{-1}=X^{-1}\left(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right)X^{-1}.

As
X
is symmetric and
\sqrt{(\det{X})^{-1}}=(\det{X})^{-1}\sqrt{\det{X}}
,
(P3)
implies that

\phi_{X^{-1}}=\frac{\det{X^{-1}}}{\sqrt{\det{X^{-1}}}}\Pf\left(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right)=\phi_{X}.

(v)

It follows immediately from
(P4)
that

\phi_{X_{1}\oplus X_{2}}=\frac{\Pf(\mathrm{d}X_{1}\cdot X_{1}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X_{1}\oplus\mathrm{d}X_{2}\cdot X_{2}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X_{2})}{\sqrt{\det{X_{1}}\det{X_{2}}}}=\phi_{X_{1}}\wedge\phi_{X_{2}}.

(vi)

Let
\Phi_{X}=\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X
. Then for any
\lambda>0
, we have

\Phi_{\lambda X}=(X\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\lambda\,\mathrm{d}X)\lambda^{-1}X^{-1}(X\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\lambda\,\mathrm{d}X)=\lambda\,\Phi_{X}+\mathrm{d}\lambda\cdot\mathrm{d}X+\mathrm{d}X\cdot\mathrm{d}\lambda\ .

Since
\mathrm{d}X
is a matrix of one forms,
\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=-\mathrm{d}\lambda\,\mathrm{d}X
and hence
\Phi_{\lambda X}=\lambda\,\Phi_{X}
. It follows from property
(P2)
that
\phi_{\lambda X}=(\lambda^{n}/\sqrt{\lambda^{2n}})\phi_{X}=\phi_{X}
.

The absence of
\mathrm{d}\lambda
(thus
\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\phi_{\lambda X}=0
) means that the form
\phi_{X}
on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is horizontal for the projection
\mathcal{P}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{n}/\mathbb{R}_{+}
with the vertical vector field
\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}X_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{ij}}
.

(vii)

To show that the Pfaffian form is closed, we use

\mathrm{d}\phi_{X}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det{X}}}\left[\mathrm{d}\Pf\left(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right)-\Pf\left(\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\right)\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}\det{X}}{2\det{X}}\right]

and prove an analogue to Jacobi’s formula.
Let
M^{ij}
be the matrix
M
with both rows and columns
i,j
deleted and let
\theta(i-j)=1
if
i>j
and
0
otherwise.
The Pfaffian expands along row
i
(analogous to the cofactor expansion of the determinant) as

\Pf(M)=\sum_{j\neq i}(-1)^{i+j+1+\theta(i-j)}M_{ij}\Pf(M^{ij}).

(2.7)

In terms of the Pfaffian adjugate matrix
M^{\prime}
defined by

M^{\prime}_{ij}=\begin{cases}(-1)^{i+j+\theta(i-j)}\Pf(M^{ij})&\text{if $i\neq j$ and}\\
0&\text{if $i=j$,}\end{cases}

the expansion shows that the diagonal entries of
M^{\prime}\cdot M
are
\Pf(M)
. Off-diagonal entries
\sum_{j}M^{\prime}_{ij}M_{jk}
vanish (consider replacing the
i
th row and column of
M
with a copy of the
k
th). Hence we get a multiple of the identity matrix:

M^{\prime}\cdot M=\Pf(M)\cdot I_{n}.

It follows already from (
2.7
) that

\partial(\Pf{M})/\partial M_{ij}=M^{\prime}_{ji}

and thus

\mathrm{d}\Pf(M)=\sum_{i<j}M^{\prime}_{ji}\mathrm{d}M_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\tr(M^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{d}M).

Letting
M=\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X
and using
\mathrm{d}X^{-1}=-X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}
(see above), we note

\mathrm{d}M=-\mathrm{d}X\cdot\mathrm{d}X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X=M\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X

and therefore

\mathrm{d}\Pf(M)=\tr(M^{\prime}\cdot M\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X)/2=\Pf(M)\wedge\tr(X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X)/2.

With Jacobi’s formula
\tr(X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X)=(\mathrm{d}\det{X})/(\det X)
, this proves
\mathrm{d}\phi_{X}=0
.

\blacksquare

The multiples
g=\lambda I_{n}\in\mathsf{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
of the identity matrix constitute the central subgroup
\mathbb{R}^{\times}\subset\mathsf{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
. They act as scalings on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, defining a map

\mathcal{P}_{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{n},\qquad(X,g)\mapsto\lambda^{2}X.

Part
(vi)
above says that under this scaling action, the pullback of the Pfaffian is independent of
\lambda
(i.e. no
\mathrm{d}\lambda
differential). Hence
\phi
descends to a form on the quotient

L\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{n}/\mathbb{R}^{\times}\quad\subset\quad\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{n(n+1)/2}\big).

Example 2.3
.

The
2\times 2
symmetric, positive definite matrices are

\mathcal{P}_{2}=\left\{X=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
b&c\end{pmatrix}\colon a,c>0\ \text{and}\ b^{2}<ac\right\}.

In terms of
\Omega_{3}=-a\cdot\mathrm{d}b\wedge\mathrm{d}c+b\cdot\mathrm{d}a\wedge\mathrm{d}c-c\cdot\mathrm{d}a\wedge\mathrm{d}b
, we have

\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X=\frac{1}{ac-b^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-\Omega_{3}\\
\Omega_{3}&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and thus}\quad\phi^{2}_{X}=\frac{-\Omega_{3}}{(ac-b^{2})^{3/2}}.

Notice that
\det X=ac-b^{2}
in the denominator of
\phi^{2}
becomes the elementary symmetric polynomial
x_{1}x_{2}+x_{1}x_{3}+x_{2}x_{3}=\det X
in the coordinates
(a,b,c)=(x_{1}+x_{3},x_{3},x_{2}+x_{3})
, and
\Omega_{3}=x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{3}-x_{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{3}+x_{3}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{2}
. This formula generalizes to higher
n
, if we restrict to the subspace of matrices where all off-diagonal entries are equal:

Lemma 2.4
.

For any
i\geq 1
, consider the
(2i+1)
-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{P}_{2i}
consisting of matrices that can be written as the sum of a diagonal matrix with positive entries and a matrix with all entries equal and positive. This subspace is parametrized by

\Lambda\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2i+1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2i},\qquad(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2i+1})\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&&\\[-4.0pt]
&\ddots&\\[-4.0pt]
&&x_{2i}\\
\end{pmatrix}+x_{2i+1}\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&1\\[-3.0pt]
\vdots&&\vdots\\
1&\cdots&1\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Set
\Omega_{2i+1}=\sum_{a=1}^{2i+1}x_{a}(-1)^{a-1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{a-1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{a+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{2i+1}
and furthermore denote the
2i
-th elementary symmetric polynomial as
S_{2i}(x)=\sum_{a=1}^{2i+1}\prod_{b\neq a}x_{b}
.

Then the restriction of the Pfaffian form
\phi^{2i}
to this subspace equals

\Lambda^{\ast}\phi^{2i}=(-1)^{i}\frac{(2i)!}{2^{i}\cdot i!}\frac{(x_{1}\cdots x_{2i+1})^{i-1}}{[S_{2i}(x)]^{i+1/2}}\Omega_{2i+1}.

(2.8)

Proof.

The determinant and inverse of the matrix
\Lambda_{a,b}=x_{2i+1}+x_{a}\delta_{a,b}
are

\det\Lambda=S_{2i}=\sum_{a=1}^{2i+1}\prod_{b\neq a}x_{b}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\Lambda^{-1}\right)_{a,b}=\frac{1}{S_{2i}}\begin{cases}\partial S_{2i}/\partial{x_{a}}&\text{if $a=b$,}\\[3.0pt]
-\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}\cdots x_{2i+1}}{x_{a}x_{b}}&\text{if $a\neq b$}\end{cases}

and from there one computes that

\left(\mathrm{d}\Lambda\cdot\Lambda^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}\Lambda\right)_{a,b}=-\frac{x_{1}\cdots x_{2i+1}}{S_{2i}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{a}}{x_{a}}\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{b}}{x_{b}}-\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{a}}{x_{a}}\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{2i+1}}{x_{2i+1}}+\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{b}}{x_{b}}\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{2i+1}}{x_{2i+1}}\right].

Since
\phi
is projective, we only need to establish (
2.8
) in the dense affine chart
x_{2i+1}=1
. So apart from the factor
(-x_{1}\cdots x_{2i+1}/S_{2i})^{i}
due to the homogeneity property
(P2)
of the Pfaffian, we only need to apply (
2.5
) to obtain

\Pf\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{a}}{x_{a}}\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{b}}{x_{b}}\right)_{1\leq a,b\leq 2i}=\frac{1}{2^{i}\cdot i!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{2i}}(\sgn\pi)\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{\pi(1)}}{x_{\pi(1)}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{\pi(2i)}}{x_{\pi(2i)}}

and note that all
(2i)!
summands are the same, namely
(\mathrm{d}x_{1})/x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge(\mathrm{d}x_{2i})/x_{2i}
.

\blacksquare

Corollary 2.5
.

For even
n
, the differential form
\phi^{n}
on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
does not vanish anywhere.

Proof.

By
lemma

2.4
,
\phi^{n}
is non-vanishing on a non-empty subset of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Using part (iii) of
lemma

2.2
, the claim follows from transitivity of the
\mathsf{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
-action on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

\blacksquare

2.2
Asymptotics

We now consider the behaviour of the invariant Pfaffian
\phi_{X}^{n}
when the matrix
X
becomes singular.
Specifically, we study degenerations parametrized by block matrices

X=\begin{pmatrix}zA&zB\\
zB^{\intercal}&C\end{pmatrix}

(2.9)

with positive definite
A\in\mathcal{P}_{n_{1}}
,
C\in\mathcal{P}_{n_{2}}
, and some
n_{1}\times n_{2}
matrix
B
with real entries. For
0<z
sufficiently small, such a matrix
X
is positive definite.
3
3

3

The matrix
X
is positive definite if and only if both
zA
and
C-zB^{\intercal}A^{-1}B
are positive definite, hence for
0<z<z_{0}(A,B,C)
where
z_{0}
denotes the smallest positive zero of
\det(C-zB^{\intercal}A^{-1}B)
.

Therefore,
\phi^{n}_{X}
is a smooth form on an open domain in
(A,C,B,z)\in\mathcal{P}_{n_{1}}\times\mathcal{P}_{n_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}n_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}
. We may study its behaviour as
z\rightarrow 0
.

Example 2.6
.

For
n_{1}=n_{2}=1
, the blocks are scalar (
1\times 1
matrices), and the form from
example

2.3
becomes, in the chart (
2.9
) where
a=zA
,
b=zB
,
c=zC
:

\phi^{2}_{X}=\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{\sqrt{z}}\wedge\frac{C(A\mathrm{d}B-B\mathrm{d}A)}{(AC-zB^{2})^{3/2}}+\sqrt{z}\cdot\frac{A\mathrm{d}B\wedge\mathrm{d}C-B\mathrm{d}A\wedge\mathrm{d}C+C\mathrm{d}A\wedge\mathrm{d}B}{(AC-zB^{2})^{3/2}}.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
n_{1},n_{2}>0
be integers with
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
even and let
X
as in (
2.9
). If
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
are even, then
\phi_{X}^{n}
extends to a holomorphic form in a neighbourhood of
\{z=0\}
, and its pullback to
\{z=0\}
is the product
\phi_{A}^{n_{1}}\wedge\phi_{C}^{n_{2}}
. If
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
are odd, then there exist forms
\alpha
and
\beta
, holomorphic in a neighbourhood of
\{z=0\}
, such that

\phi^{n}_{X}=\sqrt{z}\cdot\alpha+(\mathrm{d}\sqrt{z})\wedge\beta.

Proof.

Since
C
is invertible, the matrix
S=C-zB^{\intercal}A^{-1}B
is also invertible (for small
z
) and
z\mapsto S^{-1}
is a holomorphic function of
z
at
z=0
. The inverse of
X
can be written as

X^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}z^{-1}A^{-1}+A^{-1}BS^{-1}B^{\intercal}A^{-1}&-A^{-1}BS^{-1}\\
-S^{-1}B^{\intercal}A^{-1}&S^{-1}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

For matrices
T,U
and an ideal
I
, write
T\equiv U\mod I
to indicate that all entries of
T-U
lie in
I
. For example,
S\equiv C\mod(z)
and
S^{-1}\equiv C^{-1}\mod(z)
. Let
I=(z,\mathrm{d}z)
denote the ideal generated by
z
and
\mathrm{d}z
, inside the ring of differential forms that are holomorphic at
z=0
. Note that
I^{k}=(z^{k},z^{k-1}\mathrm{d}z)
. Multiplying the matrices, one finds

M\coloneqq(\mathrm{d}X)X^{-1}(\mathrm{d}X)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}z(\mathrm{d}A)A^{-1}(\mathrm{d}A)\mod I^{2}&0\mod I\\
0\mod I&(\mathrm{d}C)C^{-1}(\mathrm{d}C)\mod I\\
\end{pmatrix}.

The lowest order of
\Pf(M)
in
I
arises by taking as many factors of the summand in (
2.5
) as possible from the bottom-right block. If
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
are even, we can take
n_{2}/2
such factors from the bottom-right, and are then forced to take all remaining factors from the top-left. Using property
(P4)
of the Pfaffian, this shows that

\Pf(M)\equiv z^{n_{1}/2}\Pf(\mathrm{d}A\cdot A^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}A)\wedge\Pf(\mathrm{d}C\cdot C^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}C)\mod I^{n_{1}/2+1}.

Analogous reasoning shows that
\det X\equiv\det(zA)(\det C)\mod z^{n_{1}+1}
, thus
\sqrt{\det X}=z^{n_{1}/2}R
, where
R=\sqrt{(\det X)/z^{n_{1}}}\equiv\sqrt{(\det A)(\det C)}\mod z
is analytic and invertible at
z=0
. Therefore, dividing
\Pf(M)
by
\sqrt{\det X}
shows that

\phi^{n}_{X}\equiv\phi_{A}^{n_{1}}\wedge\phi_{C}^{n_{2}}\mod I.

For
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
odd, we can take at most
(n_{2}-1)/2
factors from the bottom-right block of
M
. Hence we must have at least
(n_{1}+1)/2
factors from elsewhere, so that
\Pf(M)\equiv 0\mod I^{(n_{1}+1)/2}
. Thus, there are holomorphic forms
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
such that

\Pf(M)=z^{(n_{1}+1)/2}\alpha^{\prime}+z^{(n_{1}-1)/2}\mathrm{d}z\wedge\beta^{\prime}.

Dividing by
\sqrt{\det X}=z^{n_{1}/2}R
as before, we are left with
\phi^{n}_{X}=\sqrt{z}\cdot\alpha+(\mathrm{d}\sqrt{z})\wedge\beta
where the forms
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}/R
and
\beta=2\beta^{\prime}/R
are holomorphic at
z=0
.

\blacksquare

For the asymptotics of canonical forms (
2.1
), we quote from
[
19
, Theorem 7.4]
:

Lemma 2.8
.

For integers
i,n_{1},n_{2}>0
and
X
as in (
2.9
), the form
\beta_{X}^{4i+1}
extends holomorphically over
z=0
, and its pullback to
\{z=0\}
is
\beta^{4i+1}_{A}+\beta^{4i+1}_{C}
.

2.3
Hopf algebra of forms

By the
algebra of canonical forms
we mean the graded
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra

\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}=\bigoplus_{d\geq 0}\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{d}=\ExtA^{\bullet}\left(\bigoplus_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{Q}\beta^{4k+1}\right)

with one generator
\beta^{4k+1}
of degree
4k+1
for each integer
k\geq 1
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
denote the space of positive definite, symmetric
n\times n
matrices
X
over
\mathbb{R}
. The algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
of smooth differential forms on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
receives a homomorphism

\rho_{n}\colon\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{n})\quad\text{defined by}\quad\beta^{4k+1}\mapsto\beta^{4k+1}_{X}\coloneqq\tr\left((X^{-1}\mathrm{d}X)^{4k+1}\right).

It is known (e.g.
[
22
, Proposition 4.5]
) that
\beta^{4k+1}_{X}
vanishes if
n<2k+1
. In particular, for
g>1
odd and all
n\leq g+1
,
\rho_{n}
factors through the sum
\Omega(g)=\Omega_{c}(g)\oplus\Omega_{nc}(g)
of the spaces defined in
section

1.1.1
. This sum can be identified with the quotient of
\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
by setting
\beta^{4k+1}
to zero for all
4k+1>2g-1
.

We write
\Delta\colon\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}\otimes\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
for the (graded) cocommutative coproduct for which every generator is primitive, that is,

\Delta\left(\beta^{4k+1}\right)=1\otimes\beta^{4k+1}+\beta^{4k+1}\otimes 1.

In Sweedler notation
\Delta(\omega)=\sum_{(\omega)}\omega_{(1)}\otimes\omega_{(2)}
, graded cocommutativity means that

\sum_{(\omega)}\omega_{(1)}\otimes\omega_{(2)}=\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{|\omega_{(1)}|\cdot|\omega_{(2)}|}\omega_{(2)}\otimes\omega_{(1)}

(2.10)

where
|\omega|
denotes the degree.

Under the representation on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
, this coproduct encodes the restriction onto subspaces of block-diagonal matrices. Namely, under the map

\iota_{m,n}\colon\mathcal{P}_{m}\times\mathcal{P}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{m+n},\qquad(A,B)\mapsto A\oplus B\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\\
\end{pmatrix},

(2.11)

the additivity of the trace implies that
\beta_{A\oplus B}^{4k+1}=\beta_{A}^{4k+1}+\beta_{B}^{4k+1}
. In other words, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{m+n}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{m+n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{m,n}^{*}}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}\otimes\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{m}\otimes\rho_{n}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{m})\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{m}\times\mathcal{P}_{n})}

Remark 2.9
.

The Pfaffian of a block-diagonal matrix factorizes as
\phi_{A\oplus B}=\phi_{A}\wedge\phi_{B}
(see
lemma

2.2
). To encode this property in the Hopf algebra formalism, one could add one generator
\phi^{2k}
in each positive even degree to
\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
, subject to the relations
\phi^{2k}\phi^{2l}=0
and
\beta^{4k+1}\phi^{2l}=\phi^{2l}\beta^{4k+1}
for all
k,l>0
. The realizations and coproduct extend via

\rho_{n}(\phi^{2k})=\begin{cases}\phi_{X}&\text{if $n=2k$ and}\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\\
\end{cases}\qquad\qquad\Delta\phi^{2k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\phi^{2i}\otimes\phi^{2(k-i)}.

These relations can also be phrased as
\Delta\phi=\phi\otimes\phi
for the generating series
\phi=\sum_{k>0}\phi^{2k}
in the degree completion of this Hopf algebra.

2.4
Order of the pole

For a positive definite symmetric
2n\times 2n
matrix
X
, it follows from (
2.6
) that
\phi_{X}
can be written in terms of some homogeneous polynomial
Q(X)
of degree
n(2n+1)
in the variables
X_{ij}
of the entries of
X
and their differentials
\mathrm{d}X_{ij}
, such that

\phi_{X}=\frac{Q(X)}{(\det{X})^{n+1/2}}

(2.12)

since each entry of the matrix
\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X
is a polynomial
2
-form of homogeneity degree
2n+1
, divided by
\det{X}
.
The exponent in the denominator of (
2.12
) is best possible, by
lemma

2.4
.
Similarly, from (
2.1
) it is clear that
\beta_{X}^{4k+1}
is a polynomial divided by
(\det{X})^{4k+1}
. In fact, the pole order is lower
[
22
, Theorem 5.2]
and one has:

\beta_{X}^{4k+1}=\frac{Q_{k}(X)}{(\det{X})^{k+1}}

(2.13)

for polynomial forms
Q_{k}(X)
homogeneous of degree
2n(k+1)
. By
[
22
, Proposition 4.5]
we know that
\beta_{X}^{4k+1}=0
when
k\geq n
, so the order of the pole of
\beta_{X}^{4k+1}
never exceeds
n
. This bound also persists for arbitrary polynomials in
\beta_{X}^{4k+1}
and the Pfaffian form:

Theorem 2.10
.

For symmetric
2n\times 2n
matrices
X
and any canonical form
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
, the pole of the form
\omega_{X}\wedge\phi_{X}
at
\det(X)=0
has order no more than
n+1/2
.
That is,

\omega_{X}\wedge\phi_{X}=\frac{Q_{\omega}(X)}{(\det X)^{n+1/2}}

for some polynomial form
Q_{\omega}(X)
in the matrix entries
X_{ij}
and their differentials
\mathrm{d}X_{ij}
.

Proof.

Following
[
22
, §5.2]
, we parametrize
X=U^{\intercal}DU
in terms of block matrices

U=\begin{pmatrix}I&u\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad D=\begin{pmatrix}B&0\\
0&b\end{pmatrix},\qquad b=\frac{\det(X)}{\det(B)},

where
I
denotes the
(2n-1)\times(2n-1)
identity matrix,
u
is a column vector of size
(2n-1)
, and the matrix
B
is
X
without the last row and column. For each
i\geq 0
, define

\nu_{i}=b^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}u^{\intercal}\cdot B\cdot\big(B^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}B\big)^{i}\cdot\mathrm{d}u.

These are
(i+2)
-forms in the entries of
X
. Indeed, since
u=B^{-1}(X_{j,2n})_{1\leq j<2n}
, we can express
\mathrm{d}u
in terms of
\mathrm{d}X_{j,2n}
and
\mathrm{d}(B^{-1})=-B^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}B\cdot B^{-1}
. Therefore, we can write

\nu_{i}=\frac{Q_{\nu_{i}}(X)}{(\det B)^{i+2}(\det X)}

for some polynomial
Q_{\nu_{i}}(X)
in the entries of
X
and their differentials. It was shown in
[
22
, §5.2]
that
\nu_{0}=\nu_{1}=0
and that a primitive canonical form can be expressed as

\beta^{4k+1}_{X}=\beta_{B}^{4k+1}+\text{words in}\ \Big\{\nu_{i},(\mathrm{d}b)/b\Big\}.

(2.14)

The form
\beta_{B}^{4k+1}
has no pole on
\det X=0
, and each
\nu_{i}
and
(\mathrm{d}b)/b
has a simple pole on
\det X=0
. Since the
\nu_{i}
have degree
\geq 4
(as
i\geq 2
), each word can have at most
k
of them. Also,
(\mathrm{d}b)/b
can appear at most once since
\mathrm{d}b\wedge\mathrm{d}b=0
. The bound (
2.13
) follows.

Now consider a product
\omega=\beta^{4k_{1}+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\beta^{4k_{m}+1}
and expand each primitive form via (
2.14
). Let
[\mathrm{d}^{r}u]\omega_{X}
denote the part of
\omega_{X}
that is homogeneous of degree
r
in the differentials
\mathrm{d}u
. Each
\nu_{i}
is quadratic in
\mathrm{d}u
, thus
\omega_{X}
has only even degrees in
\mathrm{d}u
and

[\mathrm{d}^{2r}u]\omega_{X}\in\frac{1}{b^{r}}\mathbb{Q}\Big[X_{ij},\mathrm{d}X_{ij},(\det B)^{-1},(\mathrm{d}b)/b\Big].

Turning to the Pfaffian, for
X=U^{\intercal}DU
we find
\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X=U^{\intercal}MU
and therefore
\phi_{X}=\Pf(M)/\sqrt{\det X}
for the skew-symmetric matrix

M=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\mathrm{d}B\cdot B^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}B+b^{-1}\left(B\cdot\mathrm{d}u\cdot\mathrm{d}u^{\intercal}\cdot B\right)&\mathrm{d}B\cdot\mathrm{d}u+\left(B\cdot\mathrm{d}u\right)b^{-1}\mathrm{d}b\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{d}u^{\intercal}\cdot\mathrm{d}B+b^{-1}\mathrm{d}b\left(\mathrm{d}u^{\intercal}\cdot B\right)&0\end{array}\right).

Expanding
\Pf(M)
via (
2.5
) as a polynomial in the entries of
M
, note that the last row of
M
has degree one in
\mathrm{d}u
. All remaining factors of
\Pf(M)
come from the top-left block, where
\mathrm{d}u
’s come in pairs and each pair is accompanied by a factor
b^{-1}
. Therefore,

[\mathrm{d}^{2r+1}u]\Pf(M)\in\frac{1}{b^{r}}\mathbb{Q}\Big[X_{ij},\mathrm{d}X_{ij},(\det B)^{-1},(\mathrm{d}b)/b\Big].

In total,
\mathrm{d}u
has only
2n-1
entries. Therefore, the maximal degree of
\omega_{X}\wedge\Pf(M)
in
\mathrm{d}u
, and thereby the highest possible power of
b^{-1}
, comes from terms of the form

([\mathrm{d}^{2n-2-2r}u]\omega_{X})\cdot([\mathrm{d}^{2r+1}u]\Pf(M))\in\frac{1}{b^{n-1}}\mathbb{Q}\Big[X_{ij},\mathrm{d}X_{ij},(\det B)^{-1},(\mathrm{d}b)/b\Big].

Since there can be at most one factor of
(\mathrm{d}b)/b
, the form
\omega_{X}\wedge\Pf(M)
has a pole of order at most
n
on
\det X=0
. Dividing by
\sqrt{\det X}
finishes the proof.

\blacksquare

Example 2.11
.

Consider the canonical form
\omega=\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}
. Then the form
\omega_{X}\wedge\phi^{2n}_{X}
on
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
has degree
2n^{2}+n-1
. The denominator in
Theorem

2.10
has degree
2n^{2}+n
, so by projectivity it follows that
\omega_{X}\wedge\phi^{2n}_{X}=c_{n}\eta^{2n}
is a multiple of the volume form (
3.1
) on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
, for some
c_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}
. We prove in
proposition

3.1
that
c_{n}\neq 0
.

3
Cohomology classes of Pfaffian invariant forms

Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
denote the space of positive-definite symmetric
n\times n
matrices
X
with real entries
X_{ij}=X_{ji}\in\mathbb{R}
.
The invariant Pfaffian forms
\phi^{2n}\in\Omega^{2n}(\mathcal{P}_{2n})
descend to differential forms on the locally symmetric space
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
with coefficients in the orientation bundle. We shall use them to represent compactly supported cohomology classes on
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
with coefficients in
\mathcal{O}
.

3.1
Volume form

We first need some preliminary results on the exterior products of the Pfaffian and canonical differential forms.
Let
\varepsilon\colon\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{\times}
be the sign of the determinant:

\varepsilon(g)=\det(g)\,|\det(g)|^{-1}\ .

Consider the volume form on
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
given by

\frac{1}{\det(X)^{\frac{2n+1}{2}}}\,\bigwedge_{i\leq j}\mathrm{d}X_{ij}\in\Omega^{n(2n+1)}(\mathcal{P}_{2n})

where the square root is positive and the exterior product is ordered lexicographically in
(i,j)
.
Contraction with the Euler vector field (
2.2
) produces a differential form

\eta^{2n}(X)=\mathcal{E}\lrcorner\left(\frac{1}{\det(X)^{\frac{2n+1}{2}}}\,\bigwedge_{i\leq j}\mathrm{d}X_{ij}\right)

(3.1)

which is closed, projectively invariant (i.e. passes to the quotient
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}
), and satisfies

\eta^{2n}(g^{\intercal}Xg)=\varepsilon(g)\eta^{2n}(X)\quad\text{for all}\quad g\in\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R}),X\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}\ .

(3.2)

Let
\mathcal{O}
denote the orientation bundle on the orbifold
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
. It is the rank one local system of
\mathbb{R}
vector spaces defined by the representation
\varepsilon\colon\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. It descends to a local system on the quotient
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
, where

L\mathcal{P}_{2n}=\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\times}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{2n}
.
We shall call an
orientation form
any differential form with coefficients in the orientation bundle. Such forms satisfy the transformation law
\omega(g^{\intercal}Xg)=\varepsilon(g)\omega(X)
for all
X\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}
and
g\in\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
. They are called ‘odd’ forms in the terminology of de Rham.

With this definition, (
3.2
) implies that
\eta^{2n}
defines a closed orientation form on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
. We first show that
\eta^{2n}

factorises into Pfaffian and canonical forms.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
.
There is a non-zero constant
c_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\beta^{5}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}=c_{n}\,\eta^{2n}.

In particular, the map
\omega\mapsto\omega\wedge\phi^{2n}
from
\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}(2n-1)
to the space of differential forms on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
satisfying (
3.2
), is injective.

Proof.

Consider the form
\delta^{2n}=\star\phi^{2n}
where
\star
is the Hodge star operator on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
relative to the orientation bundle
\mathcal{O}
on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
.
It is uniquely defined by the property that

\omega\wedge\delta^{2n}=\langle\omega,\phi^{2n}\rangle\,\eta^{2n}

(3.3)

for all smooth differential forms
\omega
on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
of degree
2n
, and has complementary degree
\dim L\mathcal{P}_{2n}-2n=d_{2n}-1-2n
. In the above,

\langle\ ,\ \rangle
is the pairing:

\Big(\textstyle{\ExtA^{m}}\,T^{*}L\mathcal{P}_{2n}\Big)\otimes\Big(\textstyle{\ExtA^{m}}\,T^{*}L\mathcal{P}_{2n}\Big)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

induced by taking exterior powers of any
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant inner product on the cotangent space
T^{*}L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
.
For any
g\in\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle g^{*}\omega\wedge g^{*}\delta^{2n}

\displaystyle=g^{*}(\langle\omega,\phi^{2n}\rangle)\,g^{*}\eta^{2n}

\displaystyle=\langle g^{*}\omega,g^{*}\phi^{2n}\rangle\,g^{*}\eta^{2n}

\displaystyle=\langle g^{*}\omega,\varepsilon(g)\phi^{2n}\rangle\,\varepsilon(g)\eta^{2n}

\displaystyle=\langle g^{*}\omega,\phi^{2n}\rangle\,\eta^{2n}

where the second equality follows from
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
-invariance of the inner product, the third from the transformation properties of
\phi^{2n}
and
\eta^{2n}
(
3.2
), and the fourth line from the fact that
\varepsilon^{2}=1
. Since this equation is true for all
\omega
, we
may replace
g^{*}\omega
with
\omega
to deduce that

\omega\wedge g^{*}\delta^{2n}=\langle\omega,\phi^{2n}\rangle\,\eta^{2n}

for all
\omega
, which on comparison with (
3.3
) implies that
g^{*}\delta^{2n}=\delta^{2n}
for all
g\in\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus
\delta^{2n}
is
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant.
Now the space of
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant differential forms on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
is known, as a consequence of the Hopf-Koszul-Samelson theorem, to be equal to the graded exterior algebra on the canonical forms
\beta^{5},\ldots,\beta^{4n-3}
. By comparing degrees, we conclude that

\delta^{2n}=\alpha_{n}\,\beta^{5}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}

for some
\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
. Substituting into (
3.3
) with
\omega=\phi^{2n}
, gives

\alpha_{n}\,\beta^{5}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}=\langle\phi^{2n},\phi^{2n}\rangle\,\eta^{2n}\ .

Note that the forms
\phi^{2n}
and
\eta^{2n}
are nowhere zero, by
corollary

2.5
and by definition, respectively.
Consequently,
\langle\phi^{2n},\phi^{2n}\rangle>0
and the right-hand side of the previous equation is non-zero. We deduce that
\alpha_{n}\neq 0
, and hence the equation in the proposition is true for the constant

c_{n}=\alpha_{n}^{-1}\langle\phi^{2n},\phi^{2n}\rangle\ .

Finally, note that the forms
\phi^{2n},\beta^{5},\ldots,\beta^{4n-3}
are defined integrally. In other words, at the tangent space at the identity matrix
I_{2n}
, they lie in the graded exterior algebra on the integral Lie algebra generated by the entries
\mathrm{d}X_{ij}
. It follows from this fact, and the definition of
\eta^{2n}
, that
c_{n}
is necessarily an integer.

The last statement concerning injectivity follows from the fact that, for any non-zero element
\omega
in the exterior algebra generated by
\beta^{5},\ldots,\beta^{4n-3}
, there exists an element
\omega^{\prime}
in the same algebra such that
\omega\wedge\omega^{\prime}=\beta^{5}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}
. Therefore
\omega\wedge\phi^{2n}
is a factor of
\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega\wedge\omega^{\prime}=c_{n}\eta^{2n}\neq 0
and therefore does not vanish.

\blacksquare

Example 3.2
.

We saw that
c_{1}=1
in
example

2.3
. With computer calculations we also found the values
c_{2}=-180
and
c_{3}=-5\times 10!
.

3.2
Twisted cohomology of
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})

For all
g>1
odd, let
\Omega^{\bullet}(g)
be the graded exterior algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
generated by the canonical forms
\beta^{5},\ldots,\beta^{2g-1}
. One has a direct sum decomposition into forms of compact and non-compact types:

\Omega(g)=\Omega_{c}(g)\oplus\Omega_{nc}(g)\ ,

where recall that
\Omega_{c}(g)\subset\Omega(g)
is the ideal spanned by
\beta^{2g-1}
, and
\Omega_{nc}(g)\subset\Omega(g)
is the subalgebra generated by
\beta^{5},\ldots,\beta^{2g-5}
.
By
proposition

3.1
the Hodge star operator for
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
relative to the orientation bundle
\mathcal{O}
induces a linear involution of vector spaces

\star\colon\Omega_{c}(2n-1)\wedge\phi^{2n}\ \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\ \Omega_{nc}(2n-1)\ ,

where the invariant forms on the right-hand side are interpreted as invariant differential forms on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}
.
By rescaling the star operator, we may assume that

\star\alpha\wedge\alpha=\beta^{5}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}

for any
\alpha\ \in\ \Omega_{c}(2n-1)\wedge\phi^{2n}
which is a simple monomial in
the generators.

Example 3.3
.

For
n=4
, the Hodge star operator interchanges the forms on
L\mathcal{P}_{8}
:

\displaystyle\beta^{13}\wedge\phi^{8}

\displaystyle\ \longleftrightarrow\ \,\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}

\displaystyle\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{13}\wedge\phi^{8}

\displaystyle\ \longleftrightarrow\ -\beta^{9}

\displaystyle\beta^{9}\wedge\beta^{13}\wedge\phi^{8}

\displaystyle\ \longleftrightarrow\ \,\beta^{5}

\displaystyle\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}\wedge\beta^{13}\wedge\phi^{8}

\displaystyle\ \longleftrightarrow\ \,1

where the forms
\alpha
on the left-hand columns are orientation forms (satisfy the transformation law (
3.2
)), and the forms
\star\alpha
on the right are
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant.

3.3
Vanishing of the Euler class in cohomology

Here we explain how the invariant Pfaffian can be interpreted as the Euler form of a vector bundle in the context of Chern-Weil theory, see e.g.
[
62
, §25]
. From this we deduce the vanishing of the class of the Pfaffian form in cohomology.

The space
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
parametrizes Euclidean metrics on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
: let
E\coloneqq\mathcal{P}_{2n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
denote the trivial rank
2n
vector bundle on
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
, equipped with the metric
(\cdot,\cdot)
defined by

(v,w)=v^{\intercal}Xw

for vectors
v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
in the fiber over
X\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}
. We furthermore endow
E
with a right action of
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
: lifting the action on the base
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
, we define

(X,v)\mapsto\big(g^{\intercal}Xg,g^{-1}v\big).

The metric
(\cdot,\cdot)
is invariant under this action, and so
E
descends to a Euclidean orbibundle
E/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
. Since the action also preserves the lattice
\mathcal{P}_{2n}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2n}
inside
E
, following
[
7
, §I.(b)]
there is a distinguished metric connection
\nabla
on
E
.
4
4

4

This connection, which we denote
\nabla
, is denoted
{}^{0}\nabla^{E}
in
[
7
]
.

In the global frame
\mathbf{e}=(\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{2n})
of
E
given by the unit (column) vectors
\mathbf{e}_{i}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2n}
, this connection reads
\nabla\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{e}\theta
, that is
\nabla\mathbf{e}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\theta_{ji}\mathbf{e}_{j}
, for the matrix of one-forms

\theta=\frac{1}{2}X^{-1}\mathrm{d}X.

Remark 3.4
.

This connection is the average
\nabla=(^{0}\nabla+{\iota^{*}}^{0}\nabla^{\vee})/2
of the
flat
connection
{}^{0}\nabla\mathbf{e}_{i}=0
on
E
, and the pullback of the flat connection
{}^{0}\nabla^{\vee}(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\vee})=0
on the dual bundle
E^{\vee}
, under the isomorphism
\iota\colon E\cong E^{\vee}
furnished by the metric
(\cdot,\cdot)
. In terms of the frame
\mathbf{e}^{\vee}
of
E^{\vee}
dual to
\mathbf{e}
, i.e.
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\vee}(\mathbf{e}_{j})=\delta_{ij}
, this isomorphism is
\iota(\mathbf{e}_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}X_{ij}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\vee}
.
It follows that
\nabla
is compatible with the metric
(\cdot,\cdot)
and with the
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
action; hence it descends to a metric connection on the orbibundle.

To compute the Euler form of the connection
\nabla
, we change from
\mathbf{e}
to an orthonormal frame
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
of
E
.
Recall that every matrix
X\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}
has a unique positive definite square root
\sqrt{X}\in\mathcal{P}_{2n}
such that
X=(\sqrt{X})^{2}
.
Setting
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=\mathbf{e}(\sqrt{X})^{-1}
gives an orthonormal frame,
(\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbf{e}^{\prime}_{j})=\delta_{ij}
. In this frame, the connection matrix
\theta^{\prime}
such that
\nabla\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}
reads

\theta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{X}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}\sqrt{X}-\mathrm{d}\sqrt{X}\cdot\sqrt{X}^{-1}\right).

This matrix is skew-symmetric (confirming that
\nabla
is compatible with the metric on
E
), and so is the associated curvature matrix

\Theta^{\prime}=\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}+\theta^{\prime}\wedge\theta^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{X}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\cdot X^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}X\cdot\sqrt{X}^{-1}.

Comparing with
definition

2.1
and using property (iii) from
lemma

2.2
, we conclude that the Pfaffian form is indeed the Euler form of the bundle
E
with the connection
\nabla
:

\phi^{2n}=(-4)^{n}\Pf(\Theta^{\prime}).

By the general theory of characteristic classes, this gives an alternative proof of the closedness
\mathrm{d}\phi^{2n}=0
. In cohomology, the differential form
\Pf(\Theta^{\prime})/(2\pi)^{n}
represents the topological Euler class of the vector bundle
E
, see
[
4
]
. This class is known to vanish rationally
[
60
]
, and an explicit primitive is constructed in
[
7
, Theorem 1.27]
.
5
5

5

In terms of the differential form denoted
\gamma(0)
in
[
7
, Theorem 1.27]
, we have
\phi^{2n}=(-8\pi)^{n}\mathrm{d}\gamma(0)
.

Example 3.5
.

For
n=2
, the link
L\mathcal{P}_{2}\cong\mathbb{H}=\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\colon\tau_{2}>0\}
is isomorphic to the upper half-plane with coordinate
\tau=\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}\tau_{2}
, via the
\mathsf{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-equivariant map
6
6

6

Here we let
P=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{smallmatrix}\big)\in\mathsf{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
act on
\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\mathbb{H}
from the left via
X\mapsto PXP^{\intercal}
and
\tau\mapsto\frac{A\tau+B}{C\tau+D}
.

\tau\colon\mathcal{P}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{H},\qquad X=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
b&c\\
\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\frac{b+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{ac-b^{2}}}{c}\ .

In this coordinate, the Pfaffian form (
example

2.3
) is the
\mathsf{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-invariant volume form

\phi^{2}_{X}=-2(\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tau_{2})/\tau_{2}^{2}
.
An explicit
\mathsf{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-invariant primitive is given by the real part of the almost holomorphic modular form
E_{2}^{*}(\tau)=E_{2}(\tau)-3/(\pi\tau_{2})
, where
E_{2}(\tau)=1-24\sum_{k,m=1}^{\infty}ke^{2\mathrm{i}\pi km\tau}
, see for example
[
5
, §11.2, p. 355]
:

\phi_{X}^{2}=\mathrm{d}\frac{2\pi}{3}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\Big(E_{2}^{*}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\Big).

Corollary 3.6
.

For all
n\geq 1
, the cohomology class

[\beta\wedge\phi^{2n}]\in H^{\bullet}(L\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})

vanishes for any form
\beta\in\Omega(2n-1)
.

Proof.

The case
\beta=1
implies the general case, since
[\beta\wedge\phi^{2n}]=[\beta]\wedge[\phi^{2n}]
. The fact that
\phi^{2n}
is trivial in cohomology follows from
[
7
,
60
]
.

\blacksquare

In particular, the cohomology classes of the odd graph complex constructed in this paper (
section

6.3
) are
not
pulled back from the locally symmetric space
\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z}).

3.4
Proof of theorem
1.1

The proof of the theorem requires a number of technical steps. We first define the notion of orientation forms on the moduli space of tropical abelian varieties, and its compactification (which is homeomorphic to the Borel-Serre compactification for
\mathsf{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
). We then show that the Pfaffian forms can be extended to the boundary. The final step involves a computation of the cup product in relative cohomology. The key point is that since the volume form
\eta^{2n}
is non-zero in cohomology, so too are its factors.

3.4.1
The moduli space of tropical abelian varieties

For background, see
[
14
,
23
]
.
Every positive definite quadratic form
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
has a finite set
M(Q)\subset\mathbb{Z}^{g}\setminus\{0\}
of
minimal vectors
, i.e. vectors
v
which minimize
v^{\intercal}Qv
over all non-zero integer vectors.
7
7

7

For example, if
Q_{aa}=1
and
Q_{ab}=-1/g
for
a\neq b
, then
M(Q)=\{\pm e_{1},\ldots,\pm e_{g},\pm(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{g})\}
for
e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{g}
the unit vectors. The interior of the cone
\widehat{\sigma}_{Q}
is the subspace of
\mathcal{P}_{g}
considered in
lemma

2.4
.
The matrices
vv^{\intercal}
generate a convex cone

\widehat{\sigma}_{Q}=\sum_{v\in M(Q)}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot vv^{\intercal}\quad\subset\quad\mathcal{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{rt}}

in the space
\mathcal{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{rt}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{g(g+1)/2}
of positive semi-definite symmetric matrices whose null space is defined over the rationals. We call
\sigma_{Q}=(\widehat{\sigma}_{Q}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}
the
link
of this cone.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf}}
be the category whose objects
[Q]
are equivalence classes of positive definite quadratic forms
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
, where two forms
Q,Q^{\prime}
are equivalent if
M(Q)=M(Q^{\prime})
. The morphisms in
\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf}}
are
[Q]\rightarrow[h^{\intercal}Qh]
for any
h\in\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
, and whenever
M(Q^{\prime})\subseteq M(Q)
there is a ‘face map’ morphism
[Q^{\prime}]\rightarrow[Q]
.
The link of the moduli space of tropical abelian varieties is the topological space defined by the colimit:

|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|=\varinjlim_{[Q]\in\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf}}}\sigma_{Q}\ .

Equivalently,
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|
may be defined as the quotient of
\mathcal{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{rt}}
by
\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
, where the former is equipped with the Satake topology (the finest topology such that
\sigma_{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{rt}}
is continuous for all
Q
). It has a closed boundary
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|\subset|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|
with open complement which we denote by

|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\circ,\mathrm{trop}}|\coloneqq|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|\ \setminus\ |\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|\cong L\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})\ ,

where
L\mathcal{P}_{g}=\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{g}
.
The space
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|
is the colimit of the links
\sigma_{Q}
at ‘infinity’, i.e., contained in
L\mathcal{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{rt}}\setminus L\mathcal{P}_{g}
, or equivalently, in the vanishing locus of the determinant.

Consider the functor

\mathcal{O}\colon\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{R}}

which assigns to every object
[Q]
of
\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf}}
the one-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{R}
, and whose values on morphisms are: the identity on face maps, and multiplication by
\det(h)^{g-1}
for
h\in\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
. It defines a rank one local system of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces over
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|
:

\varinjlim_{[Q]\in\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf}}}\left(\sigma_{Q}\times\mathcal{O}_{Q}\right)

whose restriction to the open
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\circ,\mathrm{trop}}|
is the orientation bundle on
L\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
.

3.4.2
Orientation forms on the bordification of
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|

The space
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
is obtained from
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|
as an iterated blow-up. A posteriori, it is homeomorphic to the quotient of the Borel-Serre compactification of
L\mathcal{P}_{g}
modulo the action of
\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
, but in order to study the asymptotic behaviour of the Pfaffian forms, we must use its description as an iterated blow-up.

For this, embed the link of each cone in a projective space:

\sigma_{Q}\ \subset\ \mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V))(\mathbb{R})

where
V
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space of dimension
g
,
Q
is a positive definite quadratic form on
V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{Q}(V)
is the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space of quadratic forms on
V
. Following
[
23
]
, we blow up a finite set
\mathcal{B}_{Q}
of nested chains of subspaces of the form
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V/K))\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V))
, which are defined by the set of quadratic forms with kernel
K
for a specific set of subspaces
K\subset V
determined by
\sigma_{Q}
, in increasing order of dimension. We obtain a scheme

\pi_{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}\colon\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V))\ .

One defines
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q}\subset\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}(\mathbb{R})
to be the closure, in the analytic topology, of the inverse image of the interior of
\sigma_{Q}
under the map
\pi_{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}
. The blow-down
\pi_{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}
induces a continuous map
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q}\rightarrow\sigma_{Q}
. The space
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q}
is a manifold with corners which has the combinatorial structure of a polyhedron.

One defines a category
\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf},\mathcal{B}}
generated by the sets of faces
F\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q}
of
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q}
, for all
[Q]
, whose morphisms are inclusions of faces, and maps induced by the action of
\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
. Finally, the space
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
is defined by

|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|=\varinjlim_{\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf},\mathcal{B}}}F\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}

It is the space obtained by gluing together all (quotients of) polyhedra
\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
along faces, modulo the action of
\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
.
It has a closed subspace,
