|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|\subset|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
which is obtained by gluing all faces whose images under the blow-down
\pi_{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}
lie at infinity, i.e., are contained in
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}|
. It is proven in
[
23
]
that the open complement is again the locally symmetric space:

|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|\ \setminus\ |\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|=|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\circ,\mathrm{trop}}|=L\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})\ .

Since each face
F\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
of
\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
is contractible, we may define an orientation bundle on
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
as before. In detail, consider the functor

\mathcal{O}\colon\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf},\mathcal{B}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{R}}

which assigns to every object the vector space
\mathbb{R}
, and sends all inclusions of faces to the identity map, and morphisms induced by
h\in\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
to multiplication by
\det(h)^{g-1}
. It defines a local system on
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
given by:

\varinjlim_{\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf},\mathcal{B}}}\left(F\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q}\times\mathcal{O}_{Q}\right)\longrightarrow|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|\ .

3.4.3
Differential forms with integrable square root singularities

In order to set up a de Rham cohomology theory for a space defined as a colimit of polyhedral cells
\tau

[
23
, §3.3]
(where
\tau
ranges over the set of
F\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
), we may use any complex of differential forms
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)
on
\tau
, equipped with natural restriction maps
i^{*}\colon\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial\tau)
where
i\colon\partial\tau\subset\tau
denotes the inclusion, with the following properties:

(i)

H^{n}(\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau))\cong H^{n}(\tau;\mathbb{R})
and
H^{n}(\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial\tau))\cong H^{n}(\partial\tau;\mathbb{R})
for all
n
.

(ii)

The map
i^{*}\colon\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial\tau)
is surjective
[
61
, §7]
.

One example is given by the complex of differential forms
[
23
]
which are smooth in a neighbourhood of
\tau
. However, Pfaffian forms do not have this property because of the appearance of square root singularities on the boundary, see
example

2.6
. These singularities are mild, in that the square roots appear only as
\sqrt{z}
or
(\mathrm{d}z)/\sqrt{z}=2\mathrm{d}(\sqrt{z})
. These forms are integrable at
z=0
, and in fact they become smooth after changing variables from
z
to
x=\sqrt{z}
. We formalize this larger complex of forms as follows.

We first define a complex of sheaves
\mathfrak{A}^{\bullet}
on
\tau
, for every such polyhedron
\tau
, whose sections are differential forms on
\tau
which may have integrable square root singularities on the boundary
\partial\tau
. In detail, every such
\tau
is a compact real manifold with corners with the property that it is embedded in the real points
P(\mathbb{R})
of a smooth scheme
P
over
\mathbb{R}
which we may assume to have the same dimension as
\tau
(more precisely,
P
is an iterated blow up of a projective space) and the Zariski closure of its facets is a normal crossing divisor. In particular, every point
p\in\tau
admits a neighbourhood
U_{p}\subset P(\mathbb{R})
with local coordinates
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
such that for some
0\leq r\leq n
,

\displaystyle\tau\cap U_{p}

\displaystyle=\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in U_{p}\colon z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}\geq 0\}\quad\text{and}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\partial\tau\cap U_{p}

\displaystyle=\tau\cap U_{p}\cap\left\{z_{1}\cdots z_{r}=0\right\}\ .

Define
\mathfrak{A}^{m}(\tau\cap U_{p})
to be the
\mathbb{R}
vector space of forms
\omega
of degree
m
which are smooth in
\sqrt{z_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{z_{r}},z_{r+1},\ldots,z_{n}
in an open neighbourhood of
\tau\cap U_{p}
inside
U_{p}
. Equivalently, consider the ramified map of degree
2^{r}
given by
\pi_{r}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}

which, in standard coordinates, maps
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto(x_{1}^{2},\ldots,x_{r}^{2},x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n})
. Let
\pi^{-1}_{r,+}(\tau\cap U_{p})\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
be the unique preimage of
\tau\cap U_{p}
in the region
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\geq 0
(i.e., given by non-negative square roots of
z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}
). It is homeomorphic to
\tau\cap U_{p}
via the continuous map
\pi_{r}
. Then

\mathfrak{A}^{m}(U_{p}\cap\tau)=\Omega^{m}(\pi^{-1}_{r,+}(\tau\cap U_{p}))
is the space of
m
-forms
\omega
such that
\pi_{r}^{*}\omega
is smooth on an open neighbourhood of
\pi^{-1}_{r,+}(\tau\cap U_{p})
. This is nothing other than the direct image under
\pi_{r}
of the sheaf of smooth forms on a certain open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Since, away from
z=0
, the function
\sqrt{z}
is smooth in
z
, the collection of
\mathfrak{A}^{\bullet}(\tau\cap U_{p})
defines a complex of sheaves on
\tau
.

We denote the algebra of global sections to be
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)=\Gamma(\tau,\mathfrak{A}^{\bullet})
. The restriction of an element of
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)
to the interior of
\tau
is a smooth form in the usual sense. For every face
i\colon F\tau\hookrightarrow\tau
, the space
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(F\tau)
is defined in the same way, and the restriction of differential forms (in the coordinates
x_{i}=\sqrt{z_{i}}
) induces natural maps
i^{*}\colon\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(F\tau)
.

More precisely, for
1\leq\mu\leq r
, the restriction
i_{\mu}^{*}(\omega)\in\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial_{\mu}\tau)
of a form
\omega\in\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)
to a facet
\partial_{\mu}\tau\cap U_{p}=\tau\cap U_{p}\cap\{z_{\mu}=0\}
of
\partial\tau
is defined locally on
U_{p}
by setting
\mathrm{d}\sqrt{z_{\mu}}=\mathrm{d}x_{\mu}
and
\sqrt{z_{\mu}}=x_{\mu}
in
\omega
to zero. In other words, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\pi_{r,+}^{-1}(\tau\cap U_{p}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mu}^{*}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\pi_{r,+}^{-1}(\partial_{\mu}\tau\cap U_{p}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau\cap U_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\mu}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial_{\mu}\tau\cap U_{p})}

where
\iota_{\mu}^{*}
in the top row is the ordinary restriction of smooth forms to the subspace
\iota\colon\{x_{\mu}=0\}=\pi_{r,+}^{-1}(\partial_{\mu}\tau\cap U_{p})\subset\pi_{r,+}^{-1}(\tau\cap U_{p})
. The induced map
i^{*}
in the coordinates
z_{i}=x_{i}^{2}
has the effect of formally setting
\sqrt{z_{\mu}}=0
and
(\mathrm{d}z_{\mu})/\sqrt{z_{\mu}}=0
. Somewhat abusively, we will call this map
i^{*}
also “restriction” (to the facet
\{z_{\mu}=0\}
).

Restrictions to faces
\{z_{\mu_{1}}=\cdots=z_{\mu_{c}}=0\}
of higher codimension are defined analogously by setting
\mathrm{d}\sqrt{z_{\mu_{k}}}
and
\sqrt{z_{\mu_{k}}}
to zero for all
1\leq k\leq c
. These restrictions are functorial: given two face inclusions
i\colon\sigma\subset\partial\tau
and
j\colon\nu\subset\partial\sigma
, the pullback under the composed inclusion
ij\colon\nu\subset\partial\tau
of
\nu
as a face of
\tau
is
(ij)^{*}=j^{*}i^{*}\colon\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\nu)
.

We also define algebras
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial\tau)
to be the projective limit over the
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(F\tau)
for all strict faces
F\tau
of
\tau
.

Lemma 3.7
.

The complexes
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)
and
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial\tau)
satisfy
(i)
and
(ii)
above.

Proof.

Property
(i)
follows since the complex of sheaves
\mathfrak{A}^{\bullet}
is acyclic by the usual ‘Poincaré lemma’ (due to Volterra) and therefore defines a resolution of the constant sheaf
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
on
\tau
.
The statement for
\partial\tau
is not actually necessary, but follows by induction on the dimension of cells and repeated application of
[
23
, Theorem 3.3]
to
\partial\tau
, which is the inductive limit over all faces of
\tau
.

For property
(ii)
we show that a section of
\mathsf{A}^{m}(\partial\tau)
on the boundary
\partial\tau
may be extended to
\tau
. Using a partition of unity subordinate to a cover by charts
U_{p}
as above (with
p\in\partial\tau
), it suffices to show the extendability of forms
\omega\in\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial\tau)
that have compact support contained in some
U_{p}
. So let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
be coordinates as in (
3.4
). For any non-empty subset
K\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}
, let
\pi_{K}\colon\tau\cap U_{p}\rightarrow\partial\tau\cap U_{p}
denote the orthogonal projection of
z
onto the face
\partial_{K}\tau=\tau\bigcap_{\mu\in K}\{z_{\mu}=0\}
. Recall that
\omega
is by definition a collection of forms
\omega_{K}\in\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial_{K}\tau)
such that
\omega_{K\cup\{\mu\}}=i^{*}_{\mu}\omega_{K}
for
\mu\notin K
, where we abuse notation
i_{\mu}^{*}
for the “restriction” to
\{z_{\mu}=0\}
irrespective of the domain. Let
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function with compact support and equal to
1
in a neighbourhood of zero. Now define a form on
\tau
with compact support by

\widetilde{\omega}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}\\
K\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|K|-1}(\pi_{K}^{*}\omega_{K})\prod_{\mu\in K}\psi(z_{\mu})\quad\in\quad\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau).

Since
i_{\mu}^{*}(\psi(z_{\mu})\pi_{K}^{*}\omega_{K})=\pi_{K\setminus\{\mu\}}^{*}\omega_{K}
for
\mu\in K
and
i^{*}_{\mu}(\pi^{*}_{K}\omega_{K})=\pi^{*}_{K}\omega_{K\cup\{\mu\}}
for
\mu\notin K
, all contributions to
i_{\mu}^{*}\widetilde{\omega}
from
K
not containing
\mu
cancel with the contribution from the summand
K\cup\{\mu\}
. The only term left over is
K=\{\mu\}
, proving
i_{\mu}^{*}\widetilde{\omega}=\omega_{\{\mu\}}
for all
\mu\leq r
. It follows that
i^{*}\widetilde{\omega}=\omega
, since
\omega_{K}=i^{*}_{K\setminus\{\mu\}}\omega_{\{\mu\}}=i^{*}_{K\setminus\{\mu\}}i^{*}_{\mu}\widetilde{\omega}=i^{*}_{K}\widetilde{\omega}
for any
\mu\in K
.

\blacksquare

For every face
F\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
of
\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
, we define
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(F\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}};\mathcal{O})
to be the complex of differential forms with integrable square root singularities as considered above. They are to be interpreted as taking values in the trivial bundle
\mathcal{O}_{Q}=\mathbb{R}
on
F\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
.

Definition 3.8
.

Define the complex of smooth orientation forms on
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
to be the projective limit:

\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}\left(|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O}\right)=\varprojlim_{\mathcal{D}_{g}^{\mathrm{perf},\mathcal{B}}}\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}\left(F\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q},\mathcal{O}\right)\ .

By taking the limit over the faces contained in
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
, we may similarly define smooth orientation forms on the boundary
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
. The relative de Rham complex
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O})
is defined to be the mapping cone of the restriction map
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O})\rightarrow\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O})
.

An element of
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O})
is a compatible collection of differential forms on every face of every polyhedron
\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
, allowing integrable square root singularities along its boundary. The compatibility means that they glue together consistently with respect to inclusions of faces and transform with the correct sign rule with respect to elements of
\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})
.

One may show, as in
[
23
, §3.4-3.5]
, that the complex
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O})
computes the cohomology of
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
with coefficients in
\mathcal{O}
, and we deduce that

H^{n}_{\mathrm{dR},c}(L\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})\cong H^{n}(\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O}))\

by interpreting relative (twisted) cohomology as compactly supported cohomology.
Closed elements in
\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O})
pair with twisted cellular chains on
|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|

in the usual manner via integration: the key point being that the square root singularity
(\mathrm{d}z)/\sqrt{z}
is integrable for
z\in[0,1]
.
8
8

8

In particular, after pulling back along
\pi_{r}
, the forms are smooth in the coordinates
x
and the usual Stokes’ theorem for smooth forms applies. In the coordinates
z
on
\tau
it takes the form
\int_{\sigma}\mathrm{d}\omega=\int_{\partial\sigma}i^{*}\omega
where
i^{*}\colon\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(\partial\tau)
is the “restriction” which locally sets
\sqrt{z_{i}}
and
(\mathrm{d}z_{i})/\sqrt{z_{i}}
to zero for
i\leq r
, and
\sigma
is any singular chain on
\tau
with boundary contained in
\partial\tau
.

3.4.4
Extending the Pfaffian invariant forms to the boundary

The Pfaffian invariant forms
\phi^{2n}
define closed orientation forms on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
.
We first show that they extend to the compactification.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
n\geq 1
. The collection
\{\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}\phi^{2n}\}
of the pullbacks of the Pfaffian invariant forms defines a closed orientation form

\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}\ \in\ \mathsf{A}^{\bullet}\left(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O}\right).

(3.5)

Furthermore, the restriction
9
9

9

namely, applying the restriction map
i^{*}
to facets of each polyhedron in the sense defined above
of the form
\widetilde{\phi}^{2}
to the boundary
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
, or of

\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}\wedge\widetilde{\beta}^{4n-3}\ ,\ \text{for}\ n>1

to the boundary
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
, vanishes.

Proof.

The proof follows
[
23
, Theorem 13.10]
. By an inductive argument, it will suffice in the first instance to consider a single blow-up

\pi\colon\mathbb{P}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V))

along a subspace
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V/K))
, where
V
is a vector space of dimension
2n
and
K\subset V
a non-trivial subspace. The exceptional divisor
\mathcal{E}
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V/K))\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V)/\mathcal{Q}(V/K))
.
Following
loc. cit.
Proposition 13.7, we verify that the form
\pi^{*}\phi^{2n}
has no poles along the exceptional divisor. To see this, note that since the orientation bundle is trivial on the contractible region
\det(M)>0
for
M\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V))(\mathbb{R})
, we may treat
\phi^{2n}
as an ordinary differential form for the purposes of the following argument.
Now consider the local coordinates for
\pi
given in
loc. cit.
, Proposition 9.10, relative to a choice of splitting

V\cong K\oplus C
.
The map
\pi^{*}
acts on matrix entries of matrices in block matrix form as follows:

\pi^{*}\begin{pmatrix}M_{1}&M_{12}\\
M^{\intercal}_{12}&M_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}zM_{1}&zM_{12}\\
zM^{\intercal}_{12}&M_{2}\end{pmatrix}\

where
M_{i}
is an
n_{i}\times n_{i}
square matrix for
i=1,2
, and
n_{1}+n_{2}=2n.

The exceptional locus
\mathcal{E}
is given by
z=0
. Taking
z>0
, and invoking
lemma

2.7
, we deduce that

\pi^{*}\phi^{2n}=\begin{cases}\phi^{n_{1}}(M_{1})\wedge\phi^{n_{2}}(M_{2})+O(z)\ ,&\quad\hbox{ if }\dim K=n_{1}\hbox{ is even}\\
\sqrt{z}\wedge\alpha+(\mathrm{d}\sqrt{z})\wedge\beta,&\quad\hbox{ if }\dim K=n_{1}\hbox{ is odd}\end{cases}

where
\alpha
,
\beta
are smooth. Thus,
\pi^{*}\phi^{2n}
is either smooth on
z=0
(for
n_{1}
even), or becomes smooth on the cover
z=x^{2}
(for
n_{1}
odd).

Let
X_{V/K}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V/K))(\mathbb{R})
denote the locus consisting of quadratic forms with positive determinant, and let

X\big|_{K}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V)/\mathcal{Q}(V/K))
denote the subspace of quadratic forms whose restriction to
K
is positive definite.
The computation above implies that the restriction of
\pi^{*}\phi^{2n}
to the set

X_{\mathcal{E}}\coloneqq X\big|_{K}\times X_{V/K}\quad\subset\quad\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V)/\mathcal{Q}(V/K))(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(V/K))(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R})

vanishes if
\dim K
is odd, and equals

\pi^{*}\phi^{2n}\big|_{X_{\mathcal{E}}}=\pi_{K}^{*}(\phi^{n_{1}})\wedge\phi^{n_{2}}

if
\dim K=n_{1}
is even,
where
\pi_{K}\colon X\big|_{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}(K))(\mathbb{R})
denotes restriction of quadratic forms to
K
(on block matrices of the form described above, it sends
M\mapsto M_{1}
). This completes the description of the behaviour of
\phi^{2n}
with respect to a single blow-up.

Recall that
\pi_{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}
is a composition of blow-ups.
By
[
23
, Remark 5.3]
it is enough to consider a sequence of blow ups of the type considered above in order to understand the behaviour of
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}
along exceptional divisors. By iterating,
we deduce that the restriction of
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}=\pi_{\mathcal{B}_{Q}}^{*}\phi^{2n}
to any face of
\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
either vanishes, or equals an exterior product of a certain number of forms
\phi^{2k}
for
k<n
. In particular, it is finite and
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}
extends to the boundary of
\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
. It defines a section of the trivial bundle
\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{O}_{Q}=\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathbb{R}
over
\sigma_{Q}^{\mathcal{B}}
and similarly for all its faces.
The functoriality of
\phi^{2n}
implies that the forms
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}
glue together consistently for all such
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{Q}
and are compatible with both face maps, and morphisms induced by
\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
with the transformation rule (
3.2
).
It follows from these calculations and
lemma

2.7
that
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}
defines an element of

\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}(|L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|;\mathcal{O})
.
The claim (
3.5
) follows.

For the last part, consider the restriction of the form
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}\times\widetilde{\beta}^{4n-3}
to
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
. The boundary facets of
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
are isomorphic to products of blow-ups
\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\times\ldots\times\sigma_{r}^{\mathcal{B}}
. By iterating, it suffices to consider the case
r=2
. By computing a single blow-up of
V
along
K
, as above, the restriction of this differential form satisfies

\left(\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}\times\widetilde{\beta}^{4n-3}\right)\Big|_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\times\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}=\left(\widetilde{\phi}^{2n_{1}}\wedge\widetilde{\beta}^{4n-3}\right)\Big|_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{1}}\wedge\widetilde{\phi}^{2n_{2}}\Big|_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}+\widetilde{\phi}^{2n_{1}}\Big|_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{1}}\wedge\left(\widetilde{\phi}^{2n_{2}}\wedge\widetilde{\beta}^{4n-3}\right)\Big|_{\sigma^{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}

if the dimension
\dim K=2n_{1}
is even, and vanishes if not (since if
\dim K
is odd, so too is
\dim(V/K)
). In the former case,
\widetilde{\beta}^{4n-3}
already vanishes on matrices of rank
2n_{1},2n_{2}\leq n-2
because it is of compact type
[
23
, Theorem 13.10]
. Therefore in all cases the restriction of
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}\times\widetilde{\beta}^{4n-3}
to faces vanishes. The case
\widetilde{\phi}^{2}
is similar.
\blacksquare

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
n\geq 2
. The orientation forms
\phi^{2n}\wedge\beta^{4n-3}
admit compactly supported representatives on
L\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
which we denote by

\left(\phi^{2n}\wedge\beta^{4n-3}\right)_{c}

The same holds for
\phi^{2}
on
L\mathcal{P}_{2}/\mathsf{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Use the fact that

H_{\mathrm{dR},c}^{\bullet}(L\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O})\cong H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet}(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,\mathcal{O})
. By
proposition

3.9
the forms
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}\wedge\widetilde{\beta}^{4n-3}
define relative cohomology classes on
|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
since they vanish along
|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
.

\blacksquare

3.4.5
Proof of theorem
1.1

The strategy follows that of
[
23
]
.
The relative cohomology class of the volume form
\eta^{2n}
is non-zero in the group

H^{d_{2n}-1}_{\mathrm{dR}}\Big(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,\mathcal{O}\Big)\cong H^{d_{2n}-1}_{c,\mathrm{dR}}\Big(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\circ,\mathrm{trop}}|,\mathcal{O}\Big)

(where
d_{2n}
defined in
Theorem

1.2
is the dimension of
\mathcal{P}_{2n}
), since it pairs non-trivially with the relative fundamental class.
Let
\omega\in\Omega_{c}(2n-1)
be of compact type and let
\beta=\star(\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega)
. By
proposition

3.1
, the form
\beta
is the image of an element of
\Omega(2n-1)
. By definition of the Hodge star operator, we have

\beta\wedge(\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega)=\lambda\,\eta^{2n}

(3.6)

where
\lambda=\langle\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega,\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega\rangle
is non-zero by the last part of
proposition

3.1
. The identity (
3.6
) remains true after pulling back to the blow-up
|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
and extending to the boundary, so we may view the pullbacks as elements in:

\widetilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}\left(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|\right)\quad,\quad\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}\in\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}\left(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,\mathcal{O}\right)

where the fact that
\widetilde{\beta}
extends to
|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|
was established in
[
23
]
, and the statement for
\widetilde{\phi}^{2n}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}
follows from
proposition

3.9
, as does the fact that

\widetilde{\eta}_{2n}\in\mathsf{A}^{\bullet}\left(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,\mathcal{O}\right).

The exterior product of forms induces a cup product in relative de Rham cohomology

H^{m}_{\mathrm{dR}}\Big(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|\Big)\otimes H^{\ell}_{\mathrm{dR}}\Big(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,\mathcal{O}\Big)\rightarrow H^{m+\ell}_{\mathrm{dR}}\Big(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,\mathcal{O}\Big)

Identity (
3.6
) implies the relation on cohomology classes:

\big[\widetilde{\beta}\big]\wedge\big[(\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega)_{c}\big]=\lambda\big[(\eta^{2n})_{c}\big]\ .

Since
\lambda[(\eta^{2n})_{c}]
is non-zero, this implies that both

\big[\widetilde{\beta}\big]\in H^{m}_{\mathrm{dR}}(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\big[(\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega)_{c}\big]\in H^{\ell}_{\mathrm{dR}}(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,|\partial L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\mathrm{trop},\mathcal{B}}|,\mathcal{O})

are non-zero. Identifying the latter group with
H_{c}^{\ell}(|L\mathcal{A}_{2n}^{\circ,\mathrm{trop}}|;\mathcal{O})
completes the proof.

Remark 3.11
.

This argument proves in passing that the natural map
\Omega^{\bullet}_{nc}(2n-1)\rightarrow H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})
is injective,
which was also proven in
[
23
]
. Whilst the existence of the classes (
1.5
) could be deduced from
loc. cit.
using Poincaré duality, their description using Pfaffian invariant forms is new.

3.5
Primitivity and proof of theorem
1.2

Block direct sum of matrices (
2.11
) defines a map

b_{m,n}\colon\mathcal{P}_{2m}/\mathsf{GL}_{2m}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2m+2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2m+2n}(\mathbb{Z})\ ,

which induces an isomorphism of orientation bundles
b^{*}_{m,n}\,\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}

where
\boxtimes
denotes the external tensor product. Since
b_{m,n}
is proper
10
10

10

Block direct sum defines a continuous map
\mathcal{A}_{g}^{\mathrm{trop}}\times\mathcal{A}_{h}^{\mathrm{trop}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{g+h}^{\mathrm{trop}}
, which restricts to
b_{m,n}
when
g=2m,h=2n
. Since the
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{trop}}_{r}
are compact, the former is a proper map with compact fibers. The fiber of a point in the interior
\mathcal{P}_{g+h}/\mathsf{GL}_{g+h}(\mathbb{Z})
of
\mathcal{A}_{g+h}^{\mathrm{trop}}
is contained in
\mathcal{P}_{g}/\mathsf{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathcal{P}_{h}/\mathsf{GL}_{h}(\mathbb{Z})
since
\det(X\oplus Y)\neq 0
implies
\det(X),\det(Y)\neq 0
. This implies that
b_{m,n}
has compact fibers.

it induces a morphism

b_{m,n}^{*}\colon H^{\bullet}_{c}\left(\mathcal{P}_{2m+2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2m+2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O}\right)\longrightarrow\\
H^{\bullet}_{c}\left(\mathcal{P}_{2m}/\mathsf{GL}_{2m}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O}\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}H^{\bullet}_{c}\left(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O}\right)\ .

Proposition 3.12
.

For all
m,n\geq 1
, and
\omega\in\Omega_{c}(2m+2n-1)
of compact type the class

b^{*}_{m,n}[(\phi^{2m+2n}\wedge\omega)_{c}]
vanishes in
H^{\bullet}_{c}\left(\mathcal{P}_{2m}/\mathsf{GL}_{2m}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O}\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}H^{\bullet}_{c}\left(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O}\right)
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case
\omega=\beta^{4m+4n-3}
.
The statement follows from the fact that, for any
2m\times 2m
matrix
X
and
2n\times 2n
matrix
Y
, the form

\left(\phi^{2m+2n}\wedge\beta^{4m+4n-3}\right)_{X\oplus Y}=\left(\phi^{2m}\wedge\beta^{4m+4n-3}\right)_{X}\wedge\phi^{2n}_{Y}+\phi^{2m}_{X}\wedge\left(\phi^{2n}\wedge\beta^{4m+4n-3}\right)_{Y}

vanishes since
\beta^{4m+4n-3}_{X}=\beta^{4m+4n-3}_{Y}=0
, see
[
22
, Proposition 4.5]
.

\blacksquare

By
[
1
]
, the bigraded vector space

H_{\det,\mathbb{R}}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H^{\bullet}_{c}\left(\mathcal{P}_{2n}/\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathcal{O}\right)

admits a structure of cocommutative bigraded Hopf algebra over
\mathbb{R}
.

Corollary 3.13
.

For all
\omega\in\Omega_{c}(2n-1)
, the class

[(\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega)_{c}]\in H_{\det,\mathbb{R}}

is primitive.

Proof.

By
[
1
, Definition 2.1]
the multiplication on
H^{\det}
is induced by block direct sum of matrices, and therefore the
(m,n-m)
th graded components of the coproduct

\Delta\colon H_{\det,\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow H_{\det,\mathbb{R}}\otimes H_{\det,\mathbb{R}}
dual to it are given by
b_{m,n-m}^{*}
. By
proposition

3.12
all components with
0<m<n
vanish, hence only the trivial pieces
b_{0,n}
and
b_{n,0}
remain such that
\Delta[(\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega)_{c}]=1\otimes[(\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega)_{c}]+[(\phi^{2n}\wedge\omega)_{c}]\otimes 1
.

\blacksquare

We deduce the existence of a large amount of new unstable cohomology for
\mathsf{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
for even
n
. Indeed, the Milnor-Moore theorem states that for a connected, cocommutative Hopf algebra
H
over a field of characteristic zero which is finite-dimensional in each degree, there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{U}(\Prim(H))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H
with the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie algebra of primitive elements. The Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorem states that
\mathcal{S}(\Prim(H))\cong\Gr\mathcal{U}(\Prim(H))
is an isomorphism of algebras, where
\Gr
is the grading associated to the filtration by length of tensors. By choosing a splitting of this grading, it follows that there exists an isomorphism of graded vector spaces
\mathcal{S}(\Prim(H))\cong\mathcal{U}(\Prim(H))
. There is a choice of splitting for which it is a morphism of coalgebras. It follows from
Theorem

1.1
and

corollary

3.13
that

\mathcal{S}\left(\Omega_{c}[\phi]\right)\otimes\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow H_{\det,\mathbb{R}}

is injective. The rest of
Theorem

1.2
follows by duality.

Remark 3.14
.

[
1
]
have previously shown for dimension reasons that the volume form classes
\eta^{2n}=\beta^{5}\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta^{4n-3}\wedge\phi^{2n}
are primitive. They are denoted by
t_{2n}
in
loc. cit.
since they are dual to the trivial classes in
H^{0}(\mathsf{GL}_{2n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})
.

4
The odd commutative graph complex

The odd graph complex as introduced by Kontsevich
[
42
, §2]
and studied further e.g. in
[
29
]
is a chain complex. We denote it by
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
. Its dual cochain complex is a differential graded Lie algebra, which we denote as
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
, following
[
65
]
. For further details and in particular the underlying operad of graphs, see also
[
50
, §4]
.

A graph
G
in this paper consists of finite sets of vertices
V(G)
, undirected edges
E(G)
, and half-edges
H(G)
. Each edge
e
consists of two distinct half-edges
H(e)=\{e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\}
, providing a partition
H(G)=\bigsqcup_{e}H(e)
. Each half-edge is incident to exactly one vertex, amounting to a map
H(G)\rightarrow V(G)
.
Thus, multiple edges may connect the same pair of vertices, and edges with both ends at the same vertex (self-loops) are allowed (
fig.

1
).

G=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcCone}}}

H(G)

\rightarrow

V(G)

b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}

\mapsto

x

a^{\prime},c,d

\mapsto

y

a,b

\mapsto

z

G/c=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcConeQuot}}}

H(G/c)

\rightarrow

V(G/c)

a^{\prime},b^{\prime},d,d^{\prime}

\mapsto

\ast

a,b

\mapsto

z

Figure 1:
A graph with its half-edges, incidences, and its quotient by edge
c
.

Definition 4.1
.

An
orientation
of a graph
G
is one of the two invertible elements in

\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}\otimes\bigotimes_{e\in E(G)}\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}\cong\mathbb{Z}.

An
oriented graph
is a pair
(G,o)
of a combinatorial graph and an orientation
o
of
G
.

To pick an orientation
o=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n}\otimes\bigotimes_{e}o_{e}
, we can specify a total order
v_{1}<\ldots<v_{n}
of the vertices and choose a direction
o_{e}=e^{\prime}\wedge e^{\prime\prime}
or
o_{e}=e^{\prime\prime}\wedge e^{\prime}
for each edge. In figures, we indicate orientations by labelling vertices with integers (implying the increasing order) and directing edges
v\rightarrow w
to mean that in
o_{e}
the half-edge at the tail (
v
) comes first and the head (
w
) last. Swapping two vertices, or reversing an edge, changes the sign of
o
.

An isomorphism
\varphi\colon G\cong G^{\prime}
is a pair of bijections
H(G)\leftrightarrow H(G^{\prime})
and
V(G)\leftrightarrow V(G^{\prime})
that preserves the edge partitions and incidence relations. It induces a bijection of edges
E(G)\leftrightarrow E(G^{\prime})
and identifies each orientation
o
of
G
with an orientation
\varphi_{*}(o)
of
G^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.2
.

As a vector space, the
odd commutative graph complex
is a quotient

\mathcal{GC}_{3}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{(G,o)}\mathbb{Q}(G,o)\bigg/{\sim}

spanned by all oriented graphs which are connected and have at least 3 half-edges at every vertex. The relations imposed are
(G,o)\sim(G^{\prime},\varphi_{*}(o))
for every isomorphism
\varphi\colon G\cong G^{\prime}
, and furthermore that
(G,o)\sim-(G,-o)
.

Any automorphism
\alpha\colon G\cong G
with
\alpha_{*}(o)=-o
forces
(G,o)\sim-(G,o)\sim 0
. We call such automorphisms
odd
. For example, swapping the two half-edges
e^{\prime}\leftrightarrow e^{\prime\prime}
of a self-loop constitutes an odd automorphism. Graphs with self-loops are therefore zero in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
.
We typically do not distinguish an oriented graph from its equivalence class in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
.

Example 4.3
.

The
m
-fold
multiedge
or
dipole
is the oriented graph consisting of two vertices, ordered
0<1
, and
m
edges in between—all directed from
0
to
1
. For example,

D_{1}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD1}}},\quad D_{2}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD2}}},\quad D_{3}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD3}}},\quad D_{4}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD4}}}.

Reversing all edges and swapping the vertex order
0\leftrightarrow 1
is an automorphism of
D_{m}
, which acts on its orientation as
o\mapsto(-1)^{m+1}o
. Therefore
D_{m}=0\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
for
m
even. Note that the graphs
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
do not belong to
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
(vertices with degree less than
3
).

The vector space
\mathcal{GC}_{3}=\bigoplus_{\ell,k}\gr_{\ell,k}\mathcal{GC}_{3}
is bigraded by the
loop number

\ell(G)
and the
degree

k=\left|G\right|
. For a connected graph with
n
vertices and
m
edges, they are

\ell(G)=m-n+1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left|G\right|=m-3\ell(G)=3n-2m-3.

The dimension of each piece
\gr_{\ell,k}\mathcal{GC}_{3}
is finite and equal to the number of isomorphism classes of graphs in this bidegree that do not have any odd automorphisms.
As each vertex has valence at least 3, necessarily
2m\geq 3n
and so
\gr_{\ell,k}\mathcal{GC}_{3}=0
if
k>-3
or
\ell<2
.

Definition 4.4
.

We denote the degree completion of
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
as

\mathsf{GC}_{3}=\prod_{\ell,k}\gr_{\ell,k}\mathcal{GC}_{3}.

Accounts on graph complexes typically discuss either the (homological) chain complex
\mathcal{GC}_{3}

or
the (cohomological) cochain complex
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
, but not both. In the following, we first review the relevant combinatorial structures in these two settings separately. Then in
section

4.3
we make explicit the identification
\mathsf{GC}_{3}\cong\Hom(\mathcal{GC}_{3},\mathbb{Q})
that relates them.

4.1
Homology

Given a graph
G
and a subgraph
\gamma\subset G
, the quotient
G/\gamma
is a graph with vertex set
V(G/\gamma)=(V(G)\setminus V(\gamma))\sqcup\{v_{*}\}
, and it is obtained from
G
by removing all edges in
\gamma
and identifying, in the remaining edges, all endpoints in
V(\gamma)
with a single vertex
v_{*}
.

If
\gamma=e
is a single edge with distinct endpoints
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, consider the half-edges
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
of
e
at
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, respectively. We get a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}

\displaystyle\otimes\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}

\displaystyle\cong\quad\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G/e)}

\displaystyle e^{\prime}\wedge e^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\otimes v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge w

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad v_{*}\wedge w

where
w=v_{3}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n}
for any order of the remaining vertices. Since all edges other than
e
are in both graphs, we conclude that orientations of
G
and
G/e
are in natural bijection with each other. Let
(G,o)/e=(G/e,o/e)
denote the
oriented
quotient graph obtained in this way from an
oriented
input graph
(G,o)
. Pictorially,
o/e
is obtained by first moving the tail (
v_{1}
) and head (
v_{2}
) of
e
to the beginning of the vertex order
v_{1}<v_{2}<\ldots<v_{n}
(which may introduce a sign), then replacing
v_{1}<v_{2}
by
v_{*}
, and keeping the directions of all edges other than
e
.

Definition 4.5
.

The
boundary

\partial\colon\mathcal{GC}_{3}\longrightarrow\mathcal{GC}_{3}
is the linear map such that, for every oriented graph
(G,o)
without self-loops,

\partial(G,o)=\sum_{e\in E(G)}(G,o)/e.

The boundary is homogeneous of bidegree
(0,-1)
in the grading by
(\ell,k)
, and it turns
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
into a chain complex (
\partial^{2}=0
). Thus the
graph homology

H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})=\frac{\ker\partial}{\im\partial}=\bigoplus_{\ell,k}\gr_{\ell}H_{k}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})

is bigraded by the loop number
\ell
and the degree
k
.

Example 4.6
.

For all
i\geq 1
, the dipole
D_{2i+1}
is closed (contracting any edge creates self-loops). For
i>1
, it is the boundary of a triangle graph with a simple edge
0\rightarrow 1
, a double edge
0\rightarrow 2
, and
(2i-1)
-fold multiedge
1\rightarrow 2
:

\partial\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcTri12k}}}\right)=D_{2i+1}.

The theta graph
D_{3}
has degree
k=-3
and is therefore not exact, since
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
is concentrated in degrees
\leq-3
(no graphs in degree
-2
implies no boundaries in degree
-3
).
Hence the theta class is non-zero in homology:

[D_{3}]\neq 0\in\gr_{2}H_{-3}(\mathcal{GC}_{3}).

For an arbitrary subgraph
\gamma\subset G
, an orientation
o
of
G
does not single out preferred orientations
o^{\prime}
and
o^{\prime\prime}
of
\gamma
and
G/\gamma
. But we can define a canonical
pair
, so that the product
(\gamma,o^{\prime})\otimes(G/\gamma,o^{\prime\prime})
is well-defined in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{GC}_{3}
. We use the isomorphism

\displaystyle\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}\quad

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\otimes\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G/\gamma)}

\displaystyle v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n}\quad

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad

\displaystyle v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\otimes v_{*}\wedge v_{n^{\prime}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n}

where
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n^{\prime}}
and
v_{n^{\prime}+1},\ldots,v_{n}
are the vertices in
V(\gamma)
and
V(G)\setminus V(\gamma)
, respectively, in any order. Thus if any
o
is given, move the vertices of
\gamma
to the beginning of the vertex order of
G
(which may produce a sign), and then
o^{\prime}
and
o^{\prime\prime}
are defined by the induced vertex orders just described, and by keeping all edge directions as in
o
.
In other words, if both
G
and the subgraph
\gamma
are oriented, we have an induced orientation on
G/\gamma
.
11
11

11

The quotient
G/e
by a single edge, discussed at the beginning of
section

4.1
, is the special case where we endow an edge
e
with the orientation of
D_{1}\cong e
(directing
e
from the first to the second vertex).

Example 4.7
.

Let
G
denote the oriented triangle graph from
example

4.6
and consider the subgraph
\gamma\cong D_{2i-1}
consisting of vertices
1
and
2
and the multiedge in between. Then

G=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcTri12kd}}}=(-1)^{2}\cdot\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcTri12kb}}},\quad\text{so}\quad G/\gamma=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcTri12kb}}}\Bigg/\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcTri12kc}}}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcTri12kq}}}=-D_{3}.

For an oriented graph
G
and a subgraph
\gamma\subset G
, the expression
\gamma\otimes G/\gamma
is therefore well-defined (independent of the orientation put on
\gamma
). We thus have a linear map

\mathcal{GC}_{3}\longrightarrow\mathcal{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{GC}_{3},\qquad G\mapsto\sum_{\gamma\subset G}\gamma\otimes G/\gamma

(4.1)

by summing only over subgraphs
\gamma
with the property that both
\gamma
and
G/\gamma
are in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
(i.e. are connected and have each vertex of valency 3 or more).
This map is similar, but different, to the coproduct of the core Hopf algebra of Feynman graphs
[
9
,
45
]
.
12
12

12

In the latter, graphs have no orientations, may have 2-valent vertices, may not have bridges, may be disconnected, and the quotient identifies only vertices within each connected component of
\gamma
.

4.2
Cohomology

Dual to the contraction of a subgraph, we can insert one oriented graph
G_{2}
into a vertex
v
of another oriented graph
G_{1}
. Let
H(v)
denote the half-edges of
G_{1}
at
v
. Any map
\rho\colon H(v)\rightarrow V(G_{2})
can be interpreted as a rule to re-attach the half-edges
h\in H(v)
to vertices
\rho(h)
in
G_{2}
. Afterwards, the vertex
v
is left isolated. Starting from the disjoint union
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
, re-attaching
H(v)
, and then deleting
v
, produces a graph denoted
G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2}
. Its vertex set is
V(G_{2})\sqcup V(G_{1})\setminus\{v\}
. The orientation of
G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2}
is determined by keeping all edge directions as in
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, and merging vertex orderings by putting the vertices from
G_{2}
before those of
G_{1}
; i.e. exploiting the isomorphism

\displaystyle\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G_{1})}

\displaystyle\otimes\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G_{2})}

\displaystyle\quad\cong\quad\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2})}

\displaystyle v\wedge w

\displaystyle\otimes w^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto\quad w^{\prime}\wedge w

where
w\in\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G_{1})\setminus\{v\}}
is any order of the remaining vertices from
G_{1}
.

The sum over all such insertions yields a bilinear operation
\circ\colon\mathsf{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathsf{GC}_{3}\rightarrow\mathsf{GC}_{3}
with

G_{1}\circ G_{2}=\sum_{v\in V(G_{1})}\ \sum_{\rho\colon H(v)\rightarrow V(G_{2})}G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2}.

This insertion is not associative, but it gives rise to the graded
13
13

13

with signs
[x,y]+(-1)^{\left|x\right|\cdot\left|y\right|}[y,x]=0
and
(-1)^{\left|x\right|\cdot\left|z\right|}[x,[y,z]]+(-1)^{\left|y\right|\cdot\left|x\right|}[y,[z,x]]+(-1)^{\left|z\right|\cdot\left|y\right|}[z,[x,y]]=0

Lie bracket
of
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
,

[G_{1},G_{2}]=G_{1}\circ G_{2}-(-1)^{\left|G_{1}\right|\cdot\left|G_{2}\right|}G_{2}\circ G_{1}.

In this combination, contributions from attachments
G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2}
where
\rho(h)=v^{\prime}
glues all half-edges
h\in H(v)
to the same vertex
v^{\prime}
of
G_{2}
, cancel with
G_{2}\circ_{\rho}G_{1}
where
\rho(h)=v
for all
h\in H(v^{\prime})
. So to compute brackets, we only keep
\rho
that take at least 2 values.

Example 4.8
.

Let
Y_{3}
denote the triangular prism graph with the following orientation:

Y_{3}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcPrism}}}.

The dipole
D_{3}
and
Y_{3}
both have odd degree
\left|Y_{3}\right|=\left|D_{3}\right|=-3
, so their bracket is the sum
[Y_{3},D_{3}]=Y_{3}\circ D_{3}+D_{3}\circ Y_{3}
.
Both graphs are vertex-transitive, so to compute
D_{3}\circ Y_{3}
(first 5 terms below) and
Y_{3}\circ D_{3}
(last 2 terms below), it suffices to insert only into vertex
0
, and then multiply by the number of vertices (
2
respectively
6
).
Explicitly, this gives
14
14

14

The terms from
D_{3}\circ Y_{3}
where the three edges of
D_{3}
are re-attached to vertices like
\{1,3,5\}
of the prism graph are missing because they have an odd automorphism and thus are
0
in
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
.

\displaystyle[Y_{3},D_{3}]

\displaystyle=24\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.65}]{graphs/gcLie288}}}+144\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.65}]{graphs/gcLie267}}}+72\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.65}]{graphs/gcLie197}}}

\displaystyle+36\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.65}]{graphs/gcLie242}}}+72\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.65}]{graphs/gcLie112}}}+12\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.65}]{graphs/gcLie255}}}+24\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.65}]{graphs/gcLie251}}}.

Definition 4.9
.

The
coboundary

\delta\colon\mathsf{GC}_{3}\longrightarrow\mathsf{GC}_{3}
is the Lie bracket with an edge
D_{1}
:
15
15

15

The Lie bracket defined above for
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
extends without any modifications to arbitrary graphs, so we can input
D_{1}\notin\mathsf{GC}_{3}
. One checks easily that when
G\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}
, the output will land in
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
as well.

\delta G=\frac{1}{2}\left[G,\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD1}}}\right].

Combinatorially,
\delta
splits each vertex
v
of
G
in turn into two vertices
\{0,1\}=V(D_{1})
, linked by an extra edge.
Flipping the role of
0
and
1
in a bipartition
H(v)=\rho^{-1}(0)\sqcup\rho^{-1}(1)
produces isomorphic attachments
G\circ_{\rho}D_{1}\cong G\circ_{1-\rho}D_{1}
, only one of which is counted due the prefactor
1/2
.
New vertices of degree 2 or less are never created by
\delta
(those appear twice but with opposite signs) and so it restricts to a well-defined map on
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
.

The coboundary is homogeneous of bidegree
(0,+1)
in the grading
(\ell,k)
, and it turns
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
into a cochain complex (
\delta^{2}=0
). Thus the
graph cohomology

H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})=\frac{\ker\delta}{\im\delta}=\prod_{\ell,k}\gr_{\ell}H^{k}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})

is bigraded by loop number (
\ell
) and degree (
k
). The triple
(\mathsf{GC}_{3},\delta,[\cdot,\cdot])
forms a differential graded Lie algebra
16
16

16

with our conventions, the graded Leibniz rule reads
\delta[x,y]=[x,\delta y]+(-1)^{\left|y\right|}[\delta x,y]
and so the graded Lie (super-)bracket descends to cohomology.

Example 4.10
.

Every connected 3-regular graph has degree
k=-3
and is therefore a cocycle (its coboundary has degree
-2
, where
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
is zero).
In small loop orders
2\leq\ell\leq 4
, the cohomology groups are supported in degree
k=-3
and have rank one, see
table

2
. They are generated by the classes of the theta graph
D_{3}
(
\ell=2
), the complete graph
K_{4}
(
\ell=3
), and the triangular prism
Y_{3}
(
\ell=4
). The Lie bracket of the classes of
D_{3}
and
Y_{3}
is a class in
\gr_{6}H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
, for which a representative is given in
example

4.8
.

4.3
Duality

From
definitions

4.2
and
4.4
it is clear that
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
is the linear dual of
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
. In order for the operations
\partial,\delta,G/\gamma,\circ
recalled above to be exact duals of each other, we identify
\mathsf{GC}_{3}\cong\Hom(\mathcal{GC}_{3},\mathbb{Q})
through the following pairing
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle\colon\mathsf{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{GC}_{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
.
17
17

17

In
[
65
,
50
]
, graphs have a fixed vertex set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
and cochains
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
are defined as
invariants
for vertex permutations and coinvariants for edge reversals. Instead, we write cochains like the chains
\mathcal{GC}_{3}

[
42
,
29
]
, i.e. as
coinvariants
(equivalence classes under oriented graph isomorphisms
\sim
) with respect to vertex permutations
and
edge reversals. The factor of
A=\Aut(G)
in
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
thus comes from identifying
(\mathsf{Gra}_{A})^{\vee}\cong(\mathsf{Gra}^{\vee})^{A}
with
(\mathsf{Gra}^{\vee})_{A}
, for
\mathsf{Gra}
the span of labelled graphs.

Definition 4.11
.

For any two oriented graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
, set

\left\langle G,G^{\prime}\right\rangle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\phantom{-}\lvert\Aut G\rvert&\text{if $G\sim G^{\prime}$ and $G\not\sim 0$,}\\
-\lvert\Aut G\rvert&\text{if $G\sim-G^{\prime}$ and $G\not\sim 0$,}\\
\phantom{-}0&\text{otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

(4.2)

In other words,
\left\langle G,G^{\prime}\right\rangle=\left\langle G^{\prime},G\right\rangle=\epsilon|\Aut G|=\epsilon|\Aut G^{\prime}|
where
\epsilon=\pm 1
if
G
and
G^{\prime}
are isomorphic and don’t have any odd automorphisms, and
\epsilon=0
otherwise.

Here we denote
\Aut G
the group of automorphisms of the combinatorial half-edge graph
\underline{G}
, ignoring the orientation
o
of
G=(\underline{G},o)
. Such an automorphism induces a permutation of the vertices. For a simple graph
\underline{G}
(no self-loops or multiedges), this permutation determines the automorphism. In general, there is a short exact sequence

1\rightarrow K_{G}\rightarrow\Aut(G)\rightarrow\Aut_{V}(G)\rightarrow 1

of groups where
\Aut_{V}(G)\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{V(G)}
is a subgroup of vertex permutations and the kernel
K_{G}
is generated by self-loop reversals and multiedge permutations. It has

\left|K_{G}\right|=\bigg(\prod_{v}d_{v}!\cdot 2^{d_{v}}\bigg)\prod_{v<w}(d_{vw}!)

elements, where
d_{v}
and
d_{vw}
denote the number of self-loops at
v
and the number of edges between two different vertices
v
and
w
, respectively.

Example 4.12
.

Dipole graphs
D_{m}
with
m
edges (
example

4.3
) have
|\Aut(D_{m})|=m!\cdot 2
automorphisms, where
m!=d_{01}!
and
2=|\Aut_{V}(D_{m})|=|\{\mathrm{id},0\leftrightarrow 1\}|
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Given two oriented graphs
G=(\underline{G},o)
and
G^{\prime}=(\underline{G}^{\prime},o^{\prime})
, write
\varphi\colon G\cong G^{\prime}
for isomorphisms of the (un-oriented) graphs
\underline{G}\cong\underline{G}^{\prime}
. Let
\sgn\varphi\coloneqq(\varphi_{*}o)/o^{\prime}\in\{1,-1\}
denote the relative sign between the orientations. Then the sum over all isomorphisms is

\left\langle G,G^{\prime}\right\rangle=\sum_{\varphi\colon G\cong G^{\prime}}\sgn\varphi.

Proof.

If
G
and
G^{\prime}
are not isomorphic, the sum is trivially zero. So assume there exists at least one isomorphism
\varphi
, hence
G\sim(\sgn\varphi)G^{\prime}
. The set of all isomorphisms is a torsor over
\Aut(G)
. Since
\sgn(\varphi\circ\alpha)=(\sgn\varphi)(\sgn\alpha)
, the sum thus equals

(\sgn\varphi)\sum_{\alpha\in\Aut(G)}\sgn\alpha=(\sgn\varphi)\left(|\Aut^{+}(G)|-|\Aut^{-}(G)|\right)

where
\Aut^{+}
and
\Aut^{-}
denote the even and odd automorphisms. If
G
has an odd automorphism
\beta
, then
\alpha\mapsto\alpha\circ\beta
gives a bijection
\Aut^{+}(G)\leftrightarrow\Aut^{-}(G)
and thus the sum vanishes—in agreement with
\left\langle G,G^{\prime}\right\rangle=0
since
G\sim 0
. If
G
has no odd automorphism, then
\Aut^{+}(G)=\Aut(G)
and so the sum reduces to the definition of
\left\langle G,G^{\prime}\right\rangle
.

\blacksquare

Theorem 4.14
.

For any three oriented graphs
G_{1},G_{2},G
, we have the identity

\left\langle G_{1}\circ G_{2},G\right\rangle=\sum_{\gamma\subset G}\left\langle G_{2},\gamma\right\rangle\left\langle G_{1},G/\gamma\right\rangle

where the sum runs over all subgraphs
\gamma
of
G
.

Proof.

Expanding both sides using
lemma

4.13
, the claimed identity has the form

\sum_{(v,\rho,\chi)\in L}\sgn\chi=\sum_{(\gamma,\varphi,\psi)\in R}(\sgn\varphi)(\sgn\psi).

The left-hand side sums over the set
L
of triples consisting of a vertex
v\in V(G_{1})
, a map
\rho\colon H(v)\rightarrow V(G_{2})
, and an isomorphism
\chi\colon G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2}\cong G
.
The right-hand side sums over the set
R
of triples consisting of a subgraph
\gamma\subset G
, an isomorphism
\varphi\colon G_{2}\cong\gamma
, and an isomorphism
\psi\colon G_{1}\cong G/\gamma
.

The sets
L
and
R
are in bijection:
G_{2}
is canonically a subgraph of
G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2}
, and the quotient is canonically isomorphic to
(G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2})/G_{2}\cong G_{1}
. So given
(v,\rho,\chi)
, we can set
\gamma=\chi(G_{2})
,
\varphi=\chi|_{G_{2}}
, and
\psi
the induced isomorphism
G_{1}\cong\chi(G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2})/\chi(G_{2})=G/\gamma
. Conversely, given
(\gamma,\varphi,\psi)
, we can recover the special vertex
v=\psi^{-1}(v_{*})
; the vertices in
G
incident to the half-edges at
v_{*}
in
G/\gamma
determine
\rho
via
\varphi
, and we can glue
\varphi
,
\psi
, and
\rho
into
\chi
. Under this correspondence,
\sgn\chi=(\sgn\varphi)(\sgn\psi)
by the definitions of the orientations on
G_{1}\circ_{\rho}G_{2}
(vertices of
G_{2}
come first) and
\gamma\otimes G/\gamma
(vertices of
\gamma
come first).

\blacksquare

Corollary 4.15
.

For any cochain
Q\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}
and any chain
G\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
, we have

\left\langle\delta Q,G\right\rangle=\left\langle Q,\partial G\right\rangle.

Proof.

Recall that
\lvert D_{1}\rvert=1
for
D_{1}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcD1}}}
. For oriented graphs
Q
and
G
,

\displaystyle\left\langle Q\circ D_{1},G\right\rangle=\sum_{e\in E(G)}\left\langle D_{1},e\right\rangle\left\langle Q,G/e\right\rangle=2\left\langle Q,\partial G\right\rangle

by
Theorem

4.14
.
Furthermore,
\left\langle D_{1}\circ Q,G\right\rangle=0
, as having a subgraph
\gamma\subset G
such that
G/\gamma\cong D_{1}
requires a 1-valent vertex in
G
.
By
definition

4.9
, the claim follows.

\blacksquare

Example 4.16
.

Consider the following graphs
Q
and
G
and their (co-)boundaries:

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcT122}}},

\displaystyle\delta Q

\displaystyle=2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcK4}}}+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcD2xK2}}},

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcK4}}},

\displaystyle\partial G

\displaystyle=6\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcT122}}}.

These graphs have
\lvert\Aut Q\rvert=8
and
\lvert\Aut G\rvert=24
automorphisms, so the identity reads

8\cdot 6=\left\langle Q,\partial G\right\rangle=\left\langle\delta Q,G\right\rangle=24\cdot 2.

Remark 4.17
.

The results of this subsection hold in all commutative graph complexes
\mathcal{GC}_{d}
. For even
d
, the notion of orientation and hence the induced orientations on
G_{1}\circ G_{2}
and
G/\gamma
change, but the proof of
Theorem

4.14
persists verbatim. The contribution of self-loops to
\Aut(G)
was discussed specifically to clarify the case of even
d
.

4.4
Orientations

In
definition

4.1
we view orientations as equivalence classes of ways to direct edges and order vertices. This convention from
[
65
]
is convenient for computer calculations and allows for simple indication of orientations in figures. To associate integrals of Pfaffian forms to graphs, however, we interpret orientations through canonical isomorphisms

\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}\otimes\bigotimes_{e\in E(G)}\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}\cong\det\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}\otimes\det H_{1}(G)

(4.3)

explained in
[
29
, Proposition 1]
. Recall that each edge
e\in E(G)
consists of two half-edges
H(e)=\{e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\}
. The 1-simplex corresponding to
e
is oriented as
e^{\prime}\wedge e^{\prime\prime}\in\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}
or
e^{\prime\prime}\wedge e^{\prime}=-e^{\prime}\wedge e^{\prime\prime}
by ordering these half-edges. The group of 1-chains is thus

C_{1}(G)=\bigoplus_{e\in E(G)}\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}.

This group is non-canonically isomorphic to
C_{1}(G)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}
, by choosing a direction (ordering of the two half-edges) for each edge to identify
\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

The
cycle space
is the subgroup
H_{1}(G)\subseteq C_{1}(G)
given by the kernel of the boundary map
\partial\colon C_{1}(G)\rightarrow C_{0}(G)=\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}
. If
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
denote the endpoints of the half-edges
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
of edge
e
, respectively, then
\partial(e^{\prime}\wedge e^{\prime\prime})=v^{\prime\prime}-v^{\prime}
(head minus tail). With the augmentation map defined by
\varepsilon(v)=1
for every vertex
v
, we have the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow H_{1}(G)\longrightarrow C_{1}(G)\overset{\partial}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}\overset{\varepsilon}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow 0

for every connected graph. It implies
\det C_{1}(G)\cong\det H_{1}(G)\otimes\det(\ker\varepsilon)
and
\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}\cong\det(\ker\varepsilon)
, where we fix the latter as

v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n}\mapsto(v_{2}-v_{1})\wedge(v_{3}-v_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge(v_{n}-v_{1})
.
The isomorphism (
4.3
) then arises from the canonical identification

\det C_{1}(G)\cong\det\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}\otimes\bigotimes_{e\in E(G)}\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}.

Concretely, we can describe the isomorphism (
4.3
) for a connected graph
G
as follows:
Suppose that generators
o
of the left- and
o^{\prime}
of the right-hand sides of (
4.3
) are given as

o=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n}\otimes\bigotimes_{e}o_{e}\qquad\text{and}\qquad o^{\prime}=e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{m}\otimes C_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge C_{\ell}.

Here
o_{e}\in\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}
are edge directions and
C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell}\in H_{1}(G)
are cycles forming a basis of the cycle space. For each
2\leq i\leq n
, let
P_{i}\in C_{1}(G)
denote a preimage of
v_{i}-v_{1}=\partial P_{i}
, e.g. the sum of edges of a directed path from
v_{1}
to
v_{i}
. Since any two such paths differ by a cycle, the element
C_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge C_{\ell}\wedge P_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge P_{n}\in\det C_{1}(G)
is independent of the choices of
P_{i}
. This generator of
\det C_{1}(G)
is equal to
(\det A)\cdot o_{e_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge o_{e_{m}}
, where

A=\begin{pmatrix}C_{1}&\cdots&C_{\ell}&P_{2}&\cdots&P_{n}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathsf{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{Z})

denotes the square
m\times m
matrix where 1-chains are written as column vectors, identifying
C_{1}(G)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{m}
such that the
i
th row corresponds to
o_{e_{i}}\in C_{1}(G)
.
Thus (
4.3
) is the map

o\mapsto(\det A)\cdot o^{\prime}.

Example 4.18
.

The orientation
o=v_{0}\wedge v_{1}\otimes\bigotimes_{e}o_{e}
of the dipoles
D_{2i+1}
from
example

4.3
is given by directing all edges from vertex
0
towards vertex
1
. That is, for every edge,
o_{e}=e^{\prime}\wedge e^{\prime\prime}
in terms of the half-edges
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
at vertex 0 and vertex 1, respectively.

Pick any order of the
m=2i+1
edges, set
C_{j}=o_{e_{j}}-o_{e_{m}}
for
1\leq j\leq\ell=2i
and let
o^{\prime}=e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{m}\otimes C_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge C_{\ell}
. Pick the path
P=o_{e_{m}}
from
v_{0}
to
v_{1}
. Then clearly

C_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge C_{\ell}\wedge P=o_{e_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge o_{e_{m}}

and thus
o^{\prime}\in\det\mathbb{Z}^{E(D_{2i+1})}\otimes\det H_{1}(D_{2i+1})
corresponds to
o
under (
4.3
).
For the theta graph, this cycle basis and the corresponding matrix (with
\det A=1
) look like

D_{3}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/gcD3cyc}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad A=\begin{pmatrix}C_{1}&C_{2}&P\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
-1&-1&1\end{pmatrix}.

5
Pfaffian forms on graphs

Throughout, let
G
denote a graph with
n
vertices and
m
edges,
E=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\}
. To each edge, we associate a coordinate
x_{e}
of the projective space
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})\cong\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{m-1}
. The positive part of this projective space is an open simplex, which we denote

\sigma_{G}=\sigma_{m}=\left\{[x_{e_{1}}:\cdots:x_{e_{m}}]\ \text{with all}\ x_{e}>0\right\}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E}).

Its closure
\overline{\sigma}_{G}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})
is a compact manifold with corners. Facets
\overline{\sigma}_{G}\cap\{x_{e}=0\}\cong\overline{\sigma}_{G/e}
can be identified with closed simplices of quotient graphs.
To fix the fundamental class

[\overline{\sigma}_{G}]\in H_{m-1}\Big(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E}),\bigcup_{e\in E}\{x_{e}=0\};\mathbb{Z}\Big),

we orient the simplex
\sigma_{G}
using the
(m-1)
-form on the positive orthant
\mathbb{R}^{E}_{+}
given by

\Omega_{m}=\sum_{e=1}^{m}(-1)^{e-1}x_{e}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{e-1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{e+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{m}.

(5.1)

This form pulls back as
\Omega_{m}\mapsto\lambda^{m}\Omega_{m}
under the action
x\mapsto\lambda x
of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
, and it is horizontal (
\iota_{V}\Omega_{m}=0
) for the Euler vector field
V=\sum_{e=1}^{m}x_{e}\partial_{e}
since
\Omega_{m}=\iota_{Z}(\bigwedge_{e=1}^{m}\mathrm{d}x_{e})
. Thus
\Omega_{m}
induces a well-defined orientation on
\sigma_{m}=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}/\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In the affine chart
x_{1}=1
of
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{m-1}
, the frame
\partial_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge\partial_{m}
of the coordinate vector fields is thus positively oriented on
\sigma_{m}
.

Every permutation
\alpha
of the edges acts on
\mathbb{R}^{E}
by a linear automorphism such that

\alpha^{*}\Omega_{m}=(\sgn\alpha)\Omega_{m}.

(5.2)

Therefore, in particular, the automorphisms of
G
act on the fundamental class
[\overline{\sigma}_{G}]
via the sign of the corresponding edge permutation.

5.1
The dual graph Laplacian

To define differential forms on a graph simplex
\sigma_{G}
, we map the simplex to the space of positive definite matrices (actually, a quotient of the latter), as follows.

The combinatorial structure of a graph can be encoded by the boundary map

\partial\colon C_{1}(G)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{E}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{V},\qquad e\mapsto\text{head}(e)-\text{tail}(e)

of the underlying simplicial complex, sending each edge
e\in E
to the difference of its endpoints.
18
18

18

The identification
C_{1}(G)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{E}
depends on a choice of direction of each edge, i.e. picking which of the endpoints is called head and tail (see
section

4.4
).
The kernel of this map is the cycle space
H_{1}(G)
of
G
.
This abelian group is free with rank
\ell=m-n+1
, the
loop number
. An isomorphism

H_{1}(G)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}

amounts to an integer
m\times\ell
matrix
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell})
whose columns
C_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{E}
constitute an ordered basis of the cycle space.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
denote the
m\times m
diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
.
The
dual Laplacian
19
19

19

In graph theory, the Laplacian matrix refers to a similar matrix, where
\mathcal{C}
is replaced by the edge-vertex incidence matrix. We hence refer to
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
as the
dual
Laplacian. This terminology is reversed in
[
22
]
.
matrix of a graph
G
, with respect to a basis
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell})
of the cycle space
H_{1}(G)
, is the symmetric
\ell\times\ell
matrix

\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}^{\intercal}\mathcal{D}\mathcal{C}.

More invariantly, the dual Laplacian is the symmetric bilinear form on
H_{1}(G)
defined by
(a,b)=\sum_{e=1}^{m}x_{e}a_{e}b_{e}
for
a,b\in H_{1}(G)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{E}
. In the basis
\mathcal{C}
, this form is represented by the matrix
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
. Any other basis
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
has the form
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}P
for some invertible matrix
P\in\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{Z})
, so the dual Laplacian matrix of a graph is defined up to conjugation:

\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}=P^{\intercal}\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}P.

(5.3)

The entries of the matrix
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
are linear functions of the variables
x\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
, thus the matrix can be interpreted as a function from
\mathbb{R}^{E}
to the space of symmetric matrices. If all coordinates
x_{e}
are positive, then the dual Laplacian form is positive definite. Therefore, a cycle basis
\mathcal{C}
gives rise to a smooth map

\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{E}_{+}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\ell}

to the space
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
of positive definite symmetric
\ell\times\ell
matrices. Under this map, a scaling
x\mapsto\lambda x
by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
amounts to
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\mapsto\lambda\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}=g^{\intercal}\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}g
where
g=\sqrt{\lambda}
is a multiple of the identity matrix. Thus, the above map descends to a smooth map

[\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}]\colon\sigma_{G}\longrightarrow L\mathcal{P}_{\ell}=\mathcal{P}_{\ell}/\mathbb{R}^{\times}

of the open simplex
\sigma_{G}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})
to the link of
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
. This link,
L\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, is the quotient of the symmetric space
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}=\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})/\mathsf{O}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
by the action of the central subgroup
\mathbb{R}^{\times}\subset\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
consisting of scalar multiples of the identity matrix.

Definition 5.2
.

The
Symanzik polynomial

\Psi_{G}
of a graph
G
is the homogeneous degree
\ell
polynomial in
(x_{e})_{e\in E}
given by the determinant of any dual Laplacian matrix:

\Psi_{G}\coloneqq\det\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}.

Since
\det P=\det P^{\intercal}=\pm 1
in (
5.3
), this determinant is indeed independent of the choice of cycle basis
\mathcal{C}
. By
[
8
, Proposition 2.2]
, if
G
is connected, then

\Psi_{G}=\sum_{T}\prod_{e\notin T}x_{e}

is a sum over all spanning trees
T
of
G
. Since every connected graph has a spanning tree,
\Psi_{G}
takes positive values on
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{E}
. In particular,
\Psi_{G}
is not the zero polynomial.

Remark 5.3
.

If
G
has several connected components
G=G_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup G_{r}
, then
H_{1}(G)=H_{1}(G_{1})\oplus\ldots\oplus H_{1}(G_{r})
. Thus
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
is block diagonal such that

\Psi_{G}=\Psi_{G_{1}}\!\cdots\Psi_{G_{r}}\neq 0
.

5.2
Differential forms associated to graphs

Pulling back invariant forms along the map
[\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}]\colon\sigma_{G}\longrightarrow L\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
induced by the dual Laplacian, we obtain differential forms on the graph simplex. We denote these pullbacks as
\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}\coloneqq[\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}]^{*}(\phi)
and
\beta_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}^{4i+1}\coloneqq[\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}]^{*}(\beta^{4i+1})
, for the Pfaffian and primitive canonical forms, respectively. Whenever
\ell
is odd, recall that we set
\phi=0
.

Definition 5.4
.

For a graph
G
with
\ell
loops, and any positive integer
i
, we have smooth closed differential forms on the open simplex
\sigma_{G}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})
, of degrees
\ell
and
4i+1
, denoted

\phi_{G}\coloneqq\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}\in\Omega^{\ell}(\sigma_{G})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\omega_{G}^{4i+1}\coloneqq\beta_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}^{4i+1}\in\Omega^{4i+1}(\sigma_{G}).

Since
\beta^{4i+1}_{X}
is
\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R})
invariant, it follows from (
5.3
) that
\omega_{G}^{4i+1}
is independent of the chosen cycle basis
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell})
. The Pfaffian
\phi_{G}
is only determined up to a sign: for another cycle basis
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,C_{\ell}^{\prime})P
with
P\in\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{Z})
,
lemma

2.2
(iii) shows that

\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}=\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}\cdot\det P=\pm\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}

(5.4)

where the ratio
\det P=C_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\ldots\wedge C^{\prime}_{\ell}/C_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge C_{\ell}=\pm 1
in
\det H_{1}(G)
compares the orientations of both cycle bases. This sign will be fixed later and is in fact crucial to define integrals associated to
oriented
graphs, i.e. elements of
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
, in
definition

6.1
.

It follows from (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) that
\phi_{G}
and
\omega_{G}^{4i+1}
are polynomial forms in
x_{e}
and
\mathrm{d}x_{e}
, divided by
\Psi^{(\ell+1)/2}
and
\Psi^{i+1}
, respectively.

As an example, for all odd-fold multiedges (even loop order dipole graphs) we obtain:

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
i\geq 1
, consider the dipole graph
D_{2i+1}
with edges
\{1,\ldots,2i+1\}
directed from vertex 0 to vertex 1. For the cycle basis
\mathcal{C}=(e_{1}-e_{2i+1},\ldots,e_{2i}-e_{2i+1})
,

\phi_{D_{2i+1}}=(-1)^{i}\frac{(2i)!}{2^{i}\cdot i!}\frac{(x_{1}\cdots x_{2i+1})^{i-1}\Omega_{2i+1}}{\Psi^{i+1/2}}

(5.5)

with the
2i
-form
\Omega_{2i+1}
from (
5.1
) and the Symanzik polynomial
\Psi=\sum_{a=1}^{2i+1}\prod_{b\neq a}x_{b}
.

Proof.

As any two cycles in this basis intersect only in edge
2i+1
, the dual Laplacian
2i\times 2i
matrix
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
has entries
\Lambda_{a,b}=x_{2i+1}+x_{a}\delta_{a,b}
. Apply
lemma

2.4
.

\blacksquare

Combining the Pfaffian and canonical forms, we obtain a host of closed smooth differential forms
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
on simplices.
As the Pfaffian vanishes for odd-dimensional matrices, these forms are only interesting for graphs
G
with even loop number
\ell
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Given a graph
G
and any canonical form
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
, fix any cycle basis
\mathcal{C}
and let
\phi_{G}=\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\omega_{G}=\omega_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}
denote the corresponding forms.
We have the properties:

(i)

Automorphism.
Let
\alpha
be any automorphism of
\,G
and denote by
\alpha^{*}
the pullback under the corresponding edge permutation:
\alpha^{*}(x_{e})=x_{\alpha(e)}
. Then there exists a matrix
P\in\mathsf{GL}_{\ell}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\alpha^{*}\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}=P^{\intercal}\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}P
, and with such a matrix, we have

\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}=\det{P}\cdot\alpha^{*}\left(\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}\right).

(5.6)

(ii)

Contraction.
Let
e
be an edge that is not a self-loop. Then
H_{1}(G)\cong H_{1}(G/e)
are canonically isomorphic, so
\mathcal{C}
determines a cycle basis
\mathcal{C}/e
of
G/e
. Set
\phi_{G/e}=\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}/e}}
. Then restriction to the hyperplane
\{x_{e}=0\}
can be identified with edge contraction:

\left(\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}\right)\big|_{x_{e}=0}=\phi_{G/e}\wedge\omega_{G/e}.

(iii)

Series.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
G
by subdividing an edge
e
(with a new two-valent vertex) into edges
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
.
Denote
s_{e}\colon\mathbb{R}^{E^{\prime}}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{E}_{+}
the map
y=s_{e}(x)
such that
y_{e}=x_{e^{\prime}}+x_{e^{\prime\prime}}
and
y_{i}=x_{i}
for
i\neq e
. Replace each occurrence of
e
in
\mathcal{C}
by the path
e^{\prime}e^{\prime\prime}
, to obtain a cycle basis
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of
G^{\prime}
. Set
\phi_{G^{\prime}}=\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
. Then

\phi_{G^{\prime}}\wedge\omega_{G^{\prime}}=s_{e}^{*}\left(\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}\right).

(iv)

Components.
Suppose that the edges of
G
can be partitioned into two subgraphs
G_{1},G_{2}
such that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
have at most one vertex in common.
Let
\Delta\omega=\sum_{(\omega)}\omega^{(1)}\otimes\omega^{(2)}
denote the coproduct from
section

2.3
.
Then

\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}=\sum_{(\omega)}\left(\phi_{G_{1}}\wedge\omega^{(1)}_{G_{1}}\right)\wedge\left(\phi_{G_{2}}\wedge\omega^{(2)}_{G_{2}}\right).

(5.7)

Proof.

By
[
22
, §6.2,6.3]
, the canonical forms
\omega_{G}
themselves satisfy
\omega_{G}=\alpha^{*}(\omega_{G})
,
\omega_{G}|_{x_{e}=0}=\omega_{G/e}
,
s_{e}^{*}(\omega_{G})=\omega_{G^{\prime}}
, and, for primitive forms,
\omega_{G}^{4i+1}=\omega_{G_{1}}^{4i+1}+\omega_{G_{2}}^{4i+1}
.
It remains only to establish the corresponding relations for the Pfaffian form
\phi_{G}
:

Every automorphism of
G
induces an automorphism of the cycle space
H_{1}(G)
, hence the matrix
P
exists. Then (
5.6
) follows from (
5.4
).

Cycles of
G
become cycles of
G/e
by removing all occurrences of
e
, thus
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}/e}=\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}|_{x_{e}=0}
and therefore
\phi_{G}|_{x_{e}=0}=\phi_{G/e}
.

Subdividing an edge yields a canonical isomorphism
H_{1}(G)\cong H_{1}(G^{\prime})
, replacing
e
by the path
e^{\prime}e^{\prime\prime}
. Thus for the induced cycle basis,
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}=s^{*}_{e}\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
and we get
\phi_{G^{\prime}}=s^{*}_{e}\phi_{G}
.

When two graphs
G_{1},G_{2}
share at most one vertex, then the cycle space of
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
is canonically a direct sum
H_{1}(G)=H_{1}(G_{1})\oplus H_{1}(G_{2})
. We can choose cycle bases
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of
G_{i}
such that
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})
defines the same orientation of
H_{1}(G)
as
\mathcal{C}
. Then
P^{\intercal}\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}P=\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\oplus\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
for a matrix
P
with
\det P=1
.
The identity
\omega_{G}^{4i+1}=\omega_{G_{1}}^{4i+1}+\omega_{G_{2}}^{4i+1}
generalizes to polynomials in primitive forms by the very definition of the coproduct (see
section

2.3
) as

\omega_{G}=\sum_{(\omega)}\omega^{(1)}_{G_{1}}\wedge\omega^{(2)}_{G_{2}}.

Since
lemma

2.2
(v) implies
\phi_{G}=\phi_{G_{1}}\wedge\phi_{G_{2}}
, the claimed relation follows.

\blacksquare

A connected graph is biconnected if every pair of distinct edges is contained in a cycle without repeated vertices. A
biconnected component
is an edge-maximal biconnected subgraph. Each edge belongs to exactly one biconnected component. Any two biconnected components have at most one vertex in common (such a vertex is called a
cut vertex
).

Corollary 5.7
.

If
G
has a biconnected component with odd loop number, then
\phi_{G}=0
.

Proof.

Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{c}
denote the biconnected components of
G
. Then
\phi_{G}=\phi_{G_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\phi_{G_{c}}
by (
5.7
) and induction. If any
G_{i}
has odd loop number, then
\phi_{G_{i}}=0
and so
\phi_{G}=0
.

\blacksquare

5.3
Whitney flip

Two graphs may give rise to the same dual Laplacian, and hence the same Pfaffian (and canonical) forms, even if they are not isomorphic. It suffices that they are
2-isomorphic
, that is, their edges can be identified in such a way that cycles are mapped to cycles and vice versa.
20
20

20

In matroid terms, two graphs are 2-isomorphic precisely when their cycle matroids are isomorphic.
For example, a graph
G=G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
with two connected components is 2-isomorphic to any graph
G^{\prime}
obtained from
G
by identifying a vertex in
G_{1}
with a vertex in
G_{2}
. In this case, the cycle spaces of
G
and
G^{\prime}
are both canonically identified with
H_{1}(G_{1})\oplus H_{1}(G_{2})
. This was exploited in
lemma

5.6
(iv).

G=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/flip1labels}}}\qquad G^{\prime}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.0}]{graphs/flip2labels}}}

Figure 2:
A Whitney flip of a graph.

Whitney showed in
[
63
]
that
all
2-isomorphisms can be explained with the help of only one further transformation of graphs. This
Whitney flip
or
Whitney switch
is illustrated in
fig.

2
:
Suppose that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
is a union of two subgraphs which have only 2 vertices
\{v,w\}=V(G_{1})\cap V(G_{2})
in common. Then the Whitney flip of
G
with respect to this bipartition
E(G)=E(G_{1})\sqcup E(G_{2})
is the graph
G^{\prime}
obtained from
G
by re-attaching all half-edges in
G_{2}
at
v
to
w
and vice-versa.

Since the edges and in fact the half-edges of
G
and
G^{\prime}
are by construction in natural bijection, their 1-chains
C_{1}(G)=C_{1}(G_{1})\oplus C_{1}(G_{2})=C_{1}(G^{\prime})
are canonically identified. This identification itself does not constitute a 2-isomorphism. For example, a cycle
C=P_{1}+P_{2}\in H_{1}(G)
consisting of a path
P_{1}
in
G_{1}
from
v
to
w
, and a path
P_{2}
in
G_{2}
from
w
to
v
, becomes a sum of two paths from
v
to
w
in
G^{\prime}
so that
C\notin H_{1}(G^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be the Whitney flip of
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
. Define a group homomorphism
\varphi\colon C_{1}(G)\rightarrow C_{1}(G^{\prime})
as the identity on
C_{1}(G_{1})
and minus the identity on
C_{1}(G_{2})
, so that

C_{1}(G)\supset\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}\ni e^{\prime}\wedge e^{\prime\prime}\mapsto\begin{cases}e^{\prime}\wedge e^{\prime\prime}&\text{if $e$ belongs to $G_{1}$,}\\
e^{\prime\prime}\wedge e^{\prime}&\text{if $e$ belongs to $G_{2}$.}\end{cases}

Then
\varphi
is a 2-isomorphism and thus maps cycle bases
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell})
of
G
to cycle bases
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\varphi(C_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(C_{\ell}))
of
G^{\prime}
. The respective Pfaffian forms
\phi_{G}=\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\phi_{G^{\prime}}=\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}}
are equal, and for any canonical form
\omega
, we have

\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}=\phi_{G^{\prime}}\wedge\omega_{G^{\prime}}.

(5.8)

Proof.

Consider a cycle basis
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},P_{1}+P_{2})
of
G
consisting of a cycle basis
\mathcal{C}_{1}
of
G_{1}
, a cycle basis
\mathcal{C}_{2}
of
G_{2}
and a cycle
P_{1}+P_{2}
built from a path
P_{1}
from
v
to
w
in
G_{1}
and a path
P_{2}
from
w
to
v
in
G_{2}
. Then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},-\mathcal{C}_{2},P_{1}-P_{2})
, where
-\mathcal{C}_{2}
traverses the cycles in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
in reverse, and
P_{1}-P_{2}
is indeed a cycle of
G^{\prime}
. Hence
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a cycle basis of
G^{\prime}
, which shows that
\varphi
is a 2-isomorphism.

Now choose any edge directions to identify
C_{1}(G)=C_{1}(G^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{E(G_{1})}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{E(G_{2})}
. Then
\varphi
is represented by the diagonal matrix
P=I_{m_{1}}\oplus-I_{m_{2}}
where
I_{m_{i}}
is an identity matrix block of size
m_{i}=|E(G_{i})|
. Therefore
\mathcal{C^{\prime}}=P\mathcal{C}
and the dual Laplacians

\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{C}^{\intercal}P^{\intercal}\cdot\mathcal{D}\cdot P\mathcal{C}=\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}

are identical, since the diagonal matrices
\mathcal{D}
and
P=P^{\intercal}=P^{-1}
commute.

\blacksquare

By definition, any 2-isomorphism
\varphi
of graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
induces isomorphisms
E(G)\cong E(G^{\prime})
and
H_{1}(G)\cong H_{1}(G^{\prime})
. It therefore also determines an identification

\varphi_{*}\colon\det\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)}\otimes\det H_{1}(G)\cong\det\mathbb{Z}^{E(G^{\prime})}\otimes\det H_{1}(G^{\prime})

such that
e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{m}\otimes C_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge C_{\ell}\mapsto\varphi(e_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(e_{m})\otimes\varphi(C_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(C_{\ell})
.
Following
section

4.4
, this means we can lift 2-isomorphisms to oriented graphs.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
(G,o)
be a connected oriented graph with even loop number. Let
G^{\prime}
denote a Whitney flip of
G
with respect to some edge-disjoint decomposition
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
.

The
oriented Whitney flip
of
(G,o)
with respect to
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
is the oriented graph
(G^{\prime},o^{\prime})
, where
o^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}o
for the 2-isomorphism
\varphi
from
lemma

5.8
.

Remark 5.10
.

The Whitney flip is not exactly symmetric under
G_{1}\leftrightarrow G_{2}
. Let
G^{\prime\prime}
denote the Whitney flip with the roles of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
swapped, and call
\psi\colon C_{1}(G)\cong C_{1}(G^{\prime\prime})
the corresponding 2-isomorphism: the identity on
C_{1}(G_{2})
and minus the identity on
C_{1}(G_{1})
. Then
G^{\prime}\cong G^{\prime\prime}
are not identical but only isomorphic (need to swap vertices
v\leftrightarrow w
). Since
\psi=-\varphi
, note
\psi_{*}=(-1)^{\ell(G)}\varphi_{*}
. An even loop number therefore ensures that the oriented Whitney flips
(G^{\prime},\varphi_{*}o)\sim(G^{\prime\prime},\psi_{*}o)
are equivalent in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
via the automorphism
\psi\circ\varphi^{-1}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

If we write the orientation
o=o_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{e}o_{e}
in terms of a vertex order
o_{V}=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{n}\in\det\mathbb{Z}^{V(G)}
and edge directions
o_{e}\in\det\mathbb{Z}^{H(e)}
as in
definition

4.1
, then the orientation of the Whitney flip is
o^{\prime}=(-1)^{\ell(G_{2})+1}o_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{e}o_{e}
.

Proof.

Consider a cycle basis
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},P_{1}+P_{2})
of
G
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},-\mathcal{C}_{2},P_{1}-P_{2})
for
G^{\prime}
as in the proof of
lemma

5.8
.
Let
o_{V}=v\wedge w\wedge o_{V_{1}}\wedge o_{V_{2}}
where
o_{V_{i}}
is any ordering of the vertices of
G_{i}\setminus\{v,w\}
. Then by
section

4.4
,
o_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{e}o_{e}=(\det A)\cdot o
where

A=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{C}_{1}&\mathcal{C}_{2}&P_{1}+P_{2}&P_{w}&\mathcal{Q}_{1}&\mathcal{Q}_{2}\end{pmatrix}

for any path
P_{w}
from
v
to
w
and
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
denoting matrices of paths from
v
to the vertices in
G_{i}\setminus\{v,w\}
, in the order of
o_{V_{i}}
.
We are free to choose
P_{w}=P_{1}
and we may assume that the paths in
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
are entirely contained in
G_{i}
.

For
o^{\prime}
the isomorphism from
section

4.4
gives
o_{V}\otimes\bigotimes_{e}o_{e}=(\det{A^{\prime}})\cdot o^{\prime}
for the matrix

A^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{C}_{1}&-\mathcal{C}_{2}&P_{1}-P_{2}&P_{1}&\mathcal{Q}_{1}&\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

where
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{\prime}
denotes the matrix obtained by adding
P_{1}
to every column of
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
(in
G^{\prime}
, paths from
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
originate at vertex
w
, not
v
). We conclude that
\det{A^{\prime}}=(-1)^{\ell(G_{2})+1}(\det{A})
.

\blacksquare

G=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc244}}}\qquad\qquad G^{\prime}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcWF244}}}

Figure 3:
A graph
G
that is isomorphic to its Whitney flip
G^{\prime}
along the cut
\{1,2\}
.

Example 5.12
.

The graph
G
denoted
G_{244}
in
table

3
is illustrated in
fig.

3
, together with its Whitney flip
G^{\prime}
taking
G_{2}
to be the subgraph induced by vertices
\{0,1,2\}
. Since
\ell(G_{2})+1=2
,
lemma

5.11
shows that with the orientations (edge directions and vertex ordering) as drawn,
G^{\prime}
is the oriented Whitney flip of
G
.

In this example, the flip
G^{\prime}
happens to be isomorphic to
G
itself, by swapping the vertices
1\leftrightarrow 2
and
4\leftrightarrow 5
. This isomorphism reverses the edge
4\rightarrow 5
, so that
G^{\prime}=-G
in
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
.
We thus say that
G
has an odd Whitney flip to itself.

5.4
Vanishing
