The above graph-theoretic identities imply that under certain conditions, forms
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
on the simplex
\sigma_{G}
that have top degree (equal to
\dim\sigma_{G}=m-1
) necessarily vanish:

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
G
be a graph with
m
edges, even loop number
\ell
, and no isolated vertices.
Let
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{m-\ell-1}
.
Then
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}=0
if any of the following hold:

(i)

G
has a self-loop

(ii)

G
is disconnected, or
G
has a vertex whose deletion disconnects
G

(iii)

G
has a 2-valent vertex

(iv)

G
has a 2-edge cut (two edges whose deletion disconnects
G
)

(v)

G
has degree
k=\left|G\right|>-3
and is not the single edge (
G\not\cong D_{1}
)

Note the second part of (ii) includes graphs
G\not\cong D_{1}
with bridges and 1-valent vertices.

Proof.

First note that the form
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
has degree
m-1
.

Case (i) is immediate from
corollary

5.7
(every self-loop is a biconnected component).

For case (ii), suppose that
G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}
is the union of two subgraphs sharing at most one vertex. Then
\ell(G)=\ell(G_{1})+\ell(G_{2})
, and the edge partition gives
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
when
G_{i}
has
m_{i}
edges.
Expand
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
as in (
5.7
) and let
d_{i}
denote the degrees of
\omega^{(i)}_{G_{i}}\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
.
Since
\phi_{G_{i}}\wedge\omega^{(i)}_{G_{i}}
is a projective holomorphic form of degree
\ell(G_{i})+d_{i}
in
m_{i}
variables, we must have
d_{i}\leq m_{i}-\ell(G_{i})-1
or else this form is trivially zero. But since
d_{1}+d_{2}=|\omega|=m-\ell-1
, both inequalities cannot hold simultaneously, and therefore every summand of
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
in (
5.7
) is zero.

For case (iii), let
G^{\prime}
be the graph such that
G
is obtained from
G^{\prime}
by subdividing the edge
e
.
By
lemma

5.6
(iii),
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}=s^{*}_{e}\left(\phi_{G^{\prime}}\wedge\omega_{G^{\prime}}\right)
is the pullback of the projective form
\phi_{G^{\prime}}\wedge\omega_{G^{\prime}}
of degree
m-1
in
m-1
variables, which must thus be zero.

For case (iv), suppose
G
has a 2-edge cut given by edges
\{e,f\}
.
Pick an endpoint of
e
and an endpoint of
f
, say vertices
\{v,w\}
, such that they lie in different connected components of
G\setminus\{e,f\}
.
In particular
\{v,w\}
is a 2-vertex cut of
G
and taking a Whitney flip with respect to this cut gives a graph
G^{\prime}
with a two-valent vertex adjacent to
\{e,f\}
.
By
lemma

5.8
,
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}=\phi_{G^{\prime}}\wedge\omega_{G^{\prime}}
, which then vanishes by (iii).

For (v) recall that
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
is supported in degrees
\leq-3
, so
G
must be disconnected or have some vertex
v
with degree less than three. By (ii) and (iii) we may assume that
G
is connected and free of
2
-valent vertices, so
v
has degree one. If the neighbour
w
of
v
has degree larger than one, then its removal disconnects
G
and we conclude by (ii). The only remaining case is that
w
has degree one, which by connectedness implies
G\cong D_{1}
.
21
21

21

By convention,
\phi_{G}=1
for graphs
G
with
\ell=0
.

\blacksquare

Remark 5.14
.

It was shown in
[
28
]
that the quotient of
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
by graphs with 1-vertex cuts is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
.
22
22

22

Dually,
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
is quasi-isomorphic to the subcomplex of
\mathsf{GC}_{3}
spanned by biconnected graphs
[
65
, Appendix F]
, i.e. graphs without cut-vertices (also called “one-vertex irreducible” (1VI) graphs).

Property (ii) above ensures that canonical integrals factor through this quotient. Hence, in order to compute the pairing between a cohomology class represented by a canonical integral, and a homology class, it suffices to know a representative of the homology class only modulo graphs with cut-vertices.

6
Canonical integrals on the odd graph complex

In this section we integrate Pfaffian forms to define a bilinear pairing

\mathcal{GC}_{3}\otimes\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad(X,\omega)\mapsto I_{X}(\omega).

For each canonical form
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
, we can interpret the linear function

I(\omega)\colon\mathcal{GC}_{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad X\mapsto I_{X}(\omega)

(6.1)

as an element of the cochain complex
\mathsf{GC}_{3}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\mathbb{R}=\Hom(\mathcal{GC}_{3},\mathbb{R})
via (
4.2
). In suitable loop orders, these cochains are closed, due to Stokes’ theorem, hence we obtain cohomology classes of the odd graph complex. We prove the convergence (
Theorem

1.3
) and Stokes’ relations (
Theorem

1.4
) in
section

6.1
and
section

6.2
, respectively. The vanishing (
lemma

1.5
) and cocycles (
corollary

1.6
) are discussed in
section

6.3
, and the proof of the non-triviality of the class defined by
I(\beta^{5})
(
Theorem

1.7
) is given in
section

6.4
.

Definition 6.1
.

Given an oriented graph
(G,o)\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
with edges
E=\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
\ell
loops, pick any cycle basis
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell})
such that
o=e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{m}\otimes C_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge C_{\ell}
in
\det\mathbb{Z}^{E}\otimes\det H_{1}(G)
. Then for any canonical form
\omega
, we set (with
\phi_{G}=\phi_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}
)

I_{(G,o)}(\omega)\coloneqq\frac{1}{(-2\pi)^{\ell/2}}\int_{\sigma_{G}}\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}.

(6.2)

To justify this definition, we will show in
Theorem

6.7
that the integrals (
6.2
) are absolutely convergent. Furthermore, note that if
\alpha
is an automorphism of
G
, then by pulling back the integration variables under the corresponding edge permutation, we have

I_{(G,o^{\prime})}(\omega)=\int_{(\sgn\alpha)\sigma_{G}}(\det P)\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}=\frac{o^{\prime}}{o}I_{(G,o)}(\omega)

due to (
5.2
) and (
5.6
). Here
\sgn\alpha
and
\det P
denote the action of
\alpha
on the orientations of edges
\det\mathbb{Z}^{E}
, and cycles
\det H_{1}(G)
, respectively, so that
o^{\prime}=o\cdot(\sgn\alpha)\cdot\det P
. Therefore,
I(\omega)
is indeed well-defined as a function on the graph complex
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
.

In the simplest case
\omega=1
, we can compute the integrals for all graphs explicitly:

Example 6.2
.

For dipole graphs
D_{2i+1}
with even loop number (oriented as in
example

4.18
), the integral of the Pfaffian form is absolutely convergent and evaluates to

I_{D_{2i+1}}(1)=\frac{1}{(-2\pi)^{i}}\int_{\sigma_{D_{2i+1}}}\phi_{D_{2i+1}}=1.

(6.3)

Proof.

By
example

4.18
, the dipole orientation is represented by the edge ordering
e_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge e_{2i+1}
and the cycle basis from
lemma

5.5
. Hence, apart from the factor
(-1)^{i}(2i)!/(2^{i}\cdot i!)=(-1)^{i}\cdot 1\cdot 3\cdots(2i-1)
in front of (
5.5
), the integral is

\int_{\sigma_{2i+1}}\frac{(x_{1}\cdots x_{2i+1})^{i-1}\Omega_{2i+1}}{\Psi^{i+1/2}}=\int_{\sigma_{2i+1}}\frac{(x_{1}\cdots x_{2i+1})^{-1/2}\Omega_{2i+1}}{(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{2i+1})^{i+1/2}}

by changing variables
x_{e}\mapsto 1/x_{e}
. In the chart
x_{1}+\ldots+x_{2i+1}=1
we get
\Omega_{2i+1}=\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{2i}
and the integral becomes the beta function of several variables,

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1},\ldots,x_{2i}>0\\
x_{1}+\ldots+x_{2i}<1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{2i}}{\sqrt{x_{1}\cdots x_{2i}(1-x_{1}-\ldots-x_{2i})}}=\frac{\Gamma(1/2)^{2i+1}}{\Gamma(i+1/2)}=\frac{(2\pi)^{i}}{1\cdot 3\cdots(2i-1)}

which combines with the factor
(-1)^{i}\cdot 1\cdot 3\cdots(2i-1)
as claimed.

\blacksquare

Corollary 6.3
.

For every oriented graph
G
, the integral
I_{G}(1)
is absolutely convergent. For all
X\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
, we have
I_{X}(1)=\left\langle X,\mathfrak{m}\right\rangle
, where
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}
denotes the cochain

\mathfrak{m}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{D_{2i+1}}{2\cdot(2i+1)!}.

(6.4)

In other words,
I_{G}(1)=0
unless
G
is isomorphic to a dipole with even loop number, in which case
I_{G}(1)=\pm 1
depending on the orientation of
G
.

Proof.

The degree of
\phi_{G}
is the loop number
\ell
, and the dimension of
\sigma_{G}
is one less than the edge number
m
. Thus the integral is trivially zero if
m\neq\ell+1
or if
\ell
is odd. The integrand also vanishes by
lemma

5.13
if
G
is disconnected or has a self-loop. The only remaining graphs have
m-\ell+1=2
vertices and are thus dipoles (with even loop number). The normalization is given by
example

6.2
and
\lvert\Aut(D_{2i+1})\rvert=2\cdot(2i+1)!
.

\blacksquare

More generally, for a canonical form
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{d}
of degree
d
and a connected graph
G
with
n
vertices,
m
edges and
\ell=m-n+1
loops, the integrand
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
has degree
\ell+d
. This is equal to the dimension
m-1
of the simplex
\sigma_{G}
only if
n=d+2
.

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
G
be an oriented graph with
\ell
loops and
n
vertices, and let
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{d}
be a canonical form of degree
d
. Then
I_{G}(\omega)=0
if
n\neq d+2
, or if
G
satisfies any of the conditions in
lemma

5.13
, or if
G
has an odd automorphism.

Remark 6.5
.

By
lemma

5.8
we also have
I_{G}(\omega)=0
whenever
G
is isomorphic to an odd Whitney flip of itself, or when
G
has a Whitney flip to a graph with an odd automorphism.

The vanishing of these integrals does not imply the vanishing of the integrands
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
.

Indeed, for
G=G_{244}
from
example

5.12
, the odd flip to itself explains why
\tau_{1}(G_{244})=I_{G}(\beta^{5})=0
in
table

3
. The integrand
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}^{5}
of
\tau_{1}(G_{244})
, however, is non-zero. In the form (
A.1
), it has numerator
Q_{G}=10(x_{1}+x_{2})(x_{9}-x_{8})(x_{10}+x_{11})
.

Similarly, the graph
G_{97}
in
table

3
has a Whitney flip to a graph with an odd automorphism, which explains why
\tau_{1}(G_{97})=0
. Its integrand, however, again is non-zero.

Corollary 6.6
.

For a homogeneous form
\omega
, the cochain
I(\omega)
is supported on graphs whose
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
-degree is related to the loop number by
\left|G\right|=1+\lvert\omega\rvert-2\ell(G)
. Therefore, the relative signs in the Lie bracket of two such cochains are determined by the forms alone:

\left[I(\omega^{\prime}),I(\omega^{\prime\prime})\right]=I(\omega^{\prime})\circ I(\omega^{\prime\prime})-(-1)^{(1+|\omega^{\prime}|)(1+|\omega^{\prime\prime}|)}I(\omega^{\prime\prime})\circ I(\omega^{\prime}).

(6.5)

6.1
Compactification

The forms
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
are smooth on the open simplex
\sigma_{G}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})
, but they can diverge on its boundary
\partial\overline{\sigma}_{G}=\{x_{1}\cdots x_{m}=0\}
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})
, because this boundary intersects the polar locus where
\Psi_{G}=0
: For any subset of edges
\gamma\subset E
, the linear subspace

L_{\gamma}\coloneqq\bigcap_{e\in\gamma}\{x_{e}=0\}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})

is entirely contained in
\{\Psi_{G}=0\}
whenever
\ell(\gamma)>0
, i.e. when the subgraph
\gamma
contains a loop. Therefore, the forms
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}
do not extend to smooth forms on the closed simplex
\overline{\sigma}_{G}
, and hence the convergence of the integrals (
6.2
) is not obvious.

Following
[
8
]
, we associate to a graph
G
with edges
E
an iterated blowup

\pi\colon P^{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})

of the arrangement of linear subspaces
L_{\gamma}
indexed by the bridgeless subgraphs
\gamma\subset E
. Bridgeless means that
\ell(\gamma\setminus\{e\})<\ell(\gamma)
for every edge
e\in\gamma
; in other words, every edge of
\gamma
lies in a cycle of
\gamma
. Note that bridgeless subgraphs are not necessarily connected.

The exceptional divisors
D_{\gamma}\subset P^{G}
of these blowups, together with the strict transforms
D_{e}
of the coordinate hyperplanes
\{x_{e}=0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{E-1}
, form a divisor
D\subset P^{G}
:

D=\bigcup_{\gamma\ \text{bridgeless}}D_{\gamma}\quad\cup\quad\bigcup_{e\in E}D_{e}.

This divisor has simple normal crossings. There are canonical isomorphisms

D_{\gamma}\cong P^{\gamma}\times P^{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}

where
G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma
denotes the graph obtained from
G
by contracting each connected component of
\gamma
to a single vertex (so if
\gamma
is a connected graph, then
G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma=G/\gamma)
. This also applies to single edges
\gamma=\{e\}
, in which case
P^{\gamma}
is a point, so that
D_{e}\cong P^{G/e}
.

The closure of the preimage of the simplex is a compact manifold with corners

\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}=\overline{\pi^{-1}(\sigma_{G})}\subset P^{G},

(6.6)

called the
Feynman polytope
in
[
19
]
. The facets of its boundary
\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\subset D
are in bijection with the irreducible components of
D
. The facet in
D^{\gamma}\cong P^{\gamma}\times P^{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}
is

\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{\gamma}=\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{\gamma}\cong\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma}\times\widetilde{\sigma}_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}

(6.7)

for every
\gamma
that is bridgeless. This identity also holds for single edges
\gamma=\{e\}
, since then the factor
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma}
is a point and indeed we have a canonical isomorphism
\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{e}=\widetilde{\sigma}_{G/e}
.

Theorem 6.7
.

The integral (
6.2
) converges absolutely for any graph
G
and any
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
.

Proof.

It was shown in
[
22
, §7.3]
that the pullback
\pi^{*}(\omega_{G})
of a canonical form extends smoothly over the boundary of the polytope
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
. However, this is not the case for
\pi^{*}(\phi_{G})
, which develops (integrable) square root singularities on
\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
.

We thus change coordinates to the square roots of edge lengths, by the covering map

q\colon\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{m}),\quad[x_{1}:\cdots:x_{m}]\mapsto[x_{1}^{2}:\cdots:x_{m}^{2}].

Let
\widetilde{\phi}_{G}=\pi^{*}(q^{*}\phi_{G})
and
\widetilde{\omega}_{G}=\pi^{*}(q^{*}\omega_{G})
denote the pullbacks to the blowup
P^{G}
. We claim that they extend smoothly over the boundary of the compact polytope
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
. This implies the absolute convergence of
\int_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}}\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G}
, which thus equals the improper integrals

\int_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\setminus\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}}\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G}=\int_{\sigma_{G}}\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}

since the change of coordinates
q\circ\pi
restricts to a diffeomorphism of the interior of the polytope with the open simplex:
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\setminus\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cong\sigma_{G}
.

To establish smoothness near a facet
\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{\gamma}
, consider coordinates on
P^{G}
given by

x_{e}=\begin{cases}\lambda y_{e}&\text{if $e\in\gamma$,}\\
z_{e}&\text{else,}\end{cases}

(6.8)

such that
D_{\gamma}=\{\lambda=0\}
. By the exact sequence
0\rightarrow H_{1}(\gamma)\rightarrow H_{1}(G)\rightarrow H_{1}(G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma)\rightarrow 0
, every cycle basis
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})
of
G
that begins with a cycle basis
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of
\gamma
determines a cycle basis of
G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma
, which we denote
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma
, by forgetting from
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
all rows corresponding to edges in
\gamma
.
In such a basis
\mathcal{C}
, the dual Laplacian of
G
has the block form

\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda A&\lambda B\\
\lambda B^{\intercal}&C\\
\end{pmatrix}

where
A=\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(y)
is a dual Laplacian of
\gamma
, and
C\equiv\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}(z)\mod\lambda
becomes the dual Laplacian of the quotient graph
G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma
on
D_{\gamma}
. Note that
example

2.6
shows that
\pi^{*}\phi_{G}
can indeed have singularities of the form
(\mathrm{d}\lambda)/\sqrt{\lambda}=2\mathrm{d}(\sqrt{\lambda})
on
D_{\gamma}
.

These singularities disappear by considering the blowup
P^{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{E})
not on the space of edge lengths
x_{e}
, but rather on the space of their square roots. Indeed, by
lemma

2.7
,

q^{*}\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{2}q^{*}A&\lambda^{2}q^{*}B\\
\lambda^{2}q^{*}B^{\intercal}&q^{*}C\\
\end{pmatrix}

is smooth at
\lambda=0
. Thus
q^{*}\phi_{G}
extends smoothly to the interior of the facet
\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{\gamma}
. Since
D
has normal crossings, this already implies that
q^{*}\phi_{G}
extends smoothly over the entire boundary of
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
, i.e. also over faces of higher codimension. Here we use that
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
does not meet the strict transform of the polar locus
\{\Psi_{G}=0\}
, see
[
19
, Theorem 6.7]
.

In more detail, for any chain
\emptyset\neq\gamma_{1}\subsetneq\ldots\subsetneq\gamma_{r}\subsetneq\gamma_{r+1}=G
of bridgeless subgraphs,
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
has a face of codimension
r
, contained in
D_{\gamma_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap D_{\gamma_{r}}\cong P^{\gamma_{1}}\times P^{\gamma_{2}\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma_{1}}\times\cdots\times P^{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma_{r}}
. This face is visible in the chart of the iterated blowup
P^{G}
defined by

x_{e}=\lambda_{i}\cdots\lambda_{r}y_{e}\quad\text{for}\quad e\in\gamma_{i}\setminus\gamma_{i-1}.

In this chart,
D_{\gamma_{i}}=\{\lambda_{i}=0\}
for each
1\leq i\leq r
. The denominator of
q^{*}\phi_{G}
becomes

q^{*}\sqrt{\Psi_{G}}=\lambda_{1}^{\ell(\gamma_{1})}\cdots\lambda_{r}^{\ell(\gamma_{r})}\cdot q^{*}\sqrt{\Psi_{\gamma_{1}}(y)\Psi_{\gamma_{2}\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma_{1}}(y)\cdots\Psi_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma_{r}}(y)+R(\lambda,y)}

where the polynomial
R
has positive coefficients (recall
definition

5.2
) and vanishes if any
\lambda_{i}=0
. Singularities of
q^{*}\phi_{G}
on the interior (all
y_{e}>0
) of the face
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{\gamma_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap D_{\gamma_{r}}
could therefore only arise from poles in
\lambda_{i}
. The earlier discussion of facets
shows that such poles are absent for each
i
.

For the smoothness of
\widetilde{\omega}_{G}
, it suffices to consider the primitive generators
\omega=\beta^{4i+1}
of the algebra
\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
. The smoothness of
\pi^{*}(q^{*}\beta_{G}^{4i+1})
on
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
then follows by the same argument as before, with
lemma

2.8
standing in for
lemma

2.7
.

\blacksquare

6.2
Stokes’ relations

Theorem 6.8
.

In Sweedler notation
\sum_{(\omega)}\omega^{\prime}\otimes\omega^{\prime\prime}=\Delta\omega-1\otimes\omega-\omega\otimes 1
, we have for every canonical form
\omega
the relation (
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}
is defined in
corollary

6.3
)

0=\delta I(\omega)+[I(\omega),\mathfrak{m}]+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{|\omega^{\prime}|}\left[I(\omega^{\prime\prime}),I(\omega^{\prime})\right].

(6.9)

Proof.

Above we showed that
\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G}=\pi^{*}(q^{*}(\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}))
is a smooth form on the Feynman polytope
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
. Recall that
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
has two types of facets: those labelled by edges (coming from the boundary facets
\partial\overline{\sigma}_{G}
of the original projective simplex), and those coming from blowups, labelled by bridgeless subgraphs.

Since
\phi_{G}
and
\omega_{G}
are closed, Stokes’ theorem applied to
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
thus says

0=\int_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}}\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G})=\sum_{e}\int_{\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{e}}(\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G})\Big|_{D_{e}}+\sum_{\gamma\ \text{bridgeless}}\int_{\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{\gamma}}(\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G})\Big|_{D_{\gamma}}.

Topologically, the facet of
\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}
in
D_{\gamma}
is the product
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma}\times\widetilde{\sigma}_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}
, see (
6.7
). To determine its orientation, write
m_{\gamma}=|E(\gamma)|
for the number of edges in
\gamma
, and suppose that they come first in the edge ordering of
G
. Then in the chart (
6.8
), the volume form (
5.1
) becomes
23
23

23

As a polynomial form,
\Omega_{G}(x)
also has a term
\lambda^{m_{\gamma}}\Omega_{\gamma}(y)\bigwedge_{e\notin\gamma}\mathrm{d}z_{e}
, but this is zero since the chart (
6.8
) is understood with
y_{i}=1
and
z_{j}=1
for some choice of
i
and
j
. That is,
y
and
z
are affine charts on
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{E(\gamma)}\big)
and
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{E(G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma)}\big)
, respectively. Thus,
\mathrm{d}z_{m_{\gamma}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}z_{m}=0
.

\Omega_{G}(x)=\lambda^{m_{\gamma}-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda\wedge\Omega_{\gamma}(y)\wedge(-1)^{m_{\gamma}}\Omega_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}(z).

Contracting with the outward normal vector field
-\partial/\partial\lambda
, the induced orientation of the facet (which is implied in Stokes’ theorem) is represented by the volume form
(-1)^{m_{\gamma}-1}\Omega_{\gamma}(y)\wedge\Omega_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}(z)
. So in terms of fundamental classes of oriented manifolds,

\big[\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{\gamma}\big]=(-1)^{m_{\gamma}-1}\cdot\big[\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma}\big]\times\big[\widetilde{\sigma}_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}\big].

(6.10)

This identity holds for Feynman polytopes oriented according to any edge orderings
o\in(\det\mathbb{Z}^{E(G)})^{\times}
,
o^{\prime}\in(\det\mathbb{Z}^{E(\gamma)})^{\times}
, and
o^{\prime\prime}\in(\det\mathbb{Z}^{E(G/\gamma)})^{\times}
, provided that
o=o^{\prime}\wedge o^{\prime\prime}
.

Restricting to
D_{e}
amounts to setting
x_{e}=0
. So for any cycle basis
\mathcal{C}
of
G
,

\left.\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\right|_{x_{e}=0}=\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}/e}

in terms of the cycle basis
\mathcal{C}/e
of
G/e
induced by
H_{1}(G)\cong H_{1}(G/e)
. Here we may assume
H_{1}(\{e\})=0
(
G
has no self-loops), as otherwise
\widetilde{\phi}_{G}=0
. We conclude that

\int_{\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{e}}(\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G})\Big|_{D_{e}}=\int_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{G/e}}\widetilde{\phi}_{G/e}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G/e}=I_{G/e}(\omega).

The sum over edge-type boundaries
\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{e}
in Stokes’ theorem thus produces the first term

(\delta I(\omega))(G)=I_{\partial G}(\omega)=\sum_{e}I_{G/e}(\omega)

in (
6.9
).

Restricting to
D_{\gamma}
for a bridgeless subgraph
\gamma
, it follows from
lemmas

2.8
and
2.7
(see the proof of
Theorem

6.7
) that

\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\Big|_{D_{\gamma}}=\widetilde{\phi}_{\gamma}\wedge\widetilde{\phi}_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\beta}^{4i+1}_{G}\Big|_{D_{\gamma}}=\widetilde{\beta}_{\gamma}^{4i+1}+\widetilde{\beta}_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}^{4i+1},

provided that
\phi_{G}
,
\phi_{\gamma}
, and
\phi_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}
are computed with respect to cycle bases
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma
respectively. The restriction of
\beta_{G}^{4i+1}
implies for arbitrary polynomials
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{\bullet}
in primitive canonical forms, that

\widetilde{\omega}_{G}\Big|_{D_{\gamma}}=\widetilde{\omega}_{\gamma}+\widetilde{\omega}_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}+\sum_{(\omega)}\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime\prime}_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}.

Using (
6.10
), we can therefore identify the terms from facets
D_{\gamma}
in Stokes’ formula as

\int_{\partial\widetilde{\sigma}_{G}\cap D_{\gamma}}(\widetilde{\phi}_{G}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{G})\Big|_{D_{\gamma}}=\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{m_{\gamma}-1}I_{\gamma}(\omega_{(1)})I_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}(\omega_{(2)})

where
\sum_{(\omega)}\omega_{(1)}\otimes\omega_{(2)}=1\otimes\omega+\omega\otimes 1+\sum_{(\omega)}\omega^{\prime}\otimes\omega^{\prime\prime}
denotes the full coproduct. Now recall that
I_{\gamma}(\omega^{\prime})=0
if the dimension
m_{\gamma}-1
of
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma}
differs from the degree
\ell(\gamma)+|\omega^{\prime}|
of the integrand. Also,
I_{\gamma}(\omega^{\prime})
vanishes if
\ell(\gamma)
is odd, hence we can replace
(-1)^{m_{\gamma}-1}
by
(-1)^{|\omega^{\prime}|}
.
Summing over
\gamma
, the contributions from all
D_{\gamma}
boundaries to Stokes’ formula is

\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{|\omega_{(1)}|}\sum_{\gamma\ \text{bridgeless}}I_{\gamma}(\omega_{(1)})I_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}(\omega_{(2)})=\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{|\omega_{(1)}|}\Big(I(\omega_{(2)})\circ I(\omega_{(1)})\Big)(G).

(
\ast
)

Here we exploit that
I_{\gamma}(\omega_{(1)})=0
for subgraphs that have a bridge or are disconnected, hence we may equally sum over
all
(not just bridgeless) subgraphs
\gamma
, and may replace
G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma=G/\gamma
as if
\gamma
were always connected, to invoke
Theorem

4.14
. In fact, we defined the orientations of
\gamma
,
G/\gamma
and
G
on the left-hand side of (

∗

‣
6.2
) in terms of edges and cycle bases (subgraph
\gamma
comes first), whereas on the right-hand side the orientation of the insertion
G/\gamma\circ\gamma
is defined according to
section

4.2
by keeping edge directions and ordering the vertices of
\gamma
to come first (and the insertion vertex
v_{*}
is first in
G/\gamma
). Thus tacitly we have used in (

∗

‣
6.2
) that these two ways to relate orientations on
G
with pairs of orientations of
\gamma
and
G/\gamma
are equivalent under our identification from
section

4.4
.

To see this, let
n^{\prime}
denote the number of vertices in
\gamma
. Pick paths
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=(P_{2},\ldots,P_{n^{\prime}})
from the first vertex
v_{1}
in
\gamma
to the others vertices of
\gamma
, such that each of these paths is contained in
\gamma
(recall we may assume
\gamma
connected). Let
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}=(P_{n^{\prime}+1},\ldots,P_{n})
denote paths in
G
from
v_{1}
to the vertices not in
\gamma
. We get an induced list
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}/\gamma
of paths in
G/\gamma
starting at
v_{*}
, by skipping from the paths all edges that belong to
\gamma
. Then with respect to an edge ordering of
G
where the edges of
\gamma
come first, the determinant

\det\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}&\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}&\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}&\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}=(-1)^{(n^{\prime}-1)\ell(G/\gamma)}\det\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{C^{\prime}}&\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}/\gamma&\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}/\gamma\end{pmatrix}

factorizes because the matrix
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\ \mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\ \mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\ \mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime})
is upper block triangular. Since
I_{G\mathord{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\gamma}(\omega_{(2)})=0
whenever
G/\gamma
has odd loop order, the factor
(-1)^{(n^{\prime}-1)\ell(G/\gamma)}
relating the orientations on the left- and right-hand sides of (

∗

‣
6.2
) can be omitted.

Exploiting the graded cocommutativity (
2.10
), we can rewrite (

∗

‣
6.2
) with (
6.5
) as

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{|\omega_{(1)}|}I(\omega_{(2)})\circ I(\omega_{(1)})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{|\omega_{(1)}|\cdot|\omega_{(2)}|}(-1)^{|\omega_{(2)}|}I(\omega_{(1)})\circ I(\omega_{(2)})\\
=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(\omega)}(-1)^{|\omega_{(1)}|}\underbrace{\left(I(\omega_{(2)})\circ I(\omega_{(1)})-(-1)^{(1+|\omega_{(1)}|)(1+|\omega_{(2)}|)}I(\omega_{(1)})\circ I(\omega_{(2)})\right)}_{\left[I(\omega_{(2)}),I(\omega_{(1)})\right]}.

This provides the Lie bracket terms in (
6.9
); it remains only to identify the primitive terms
1\otimes\omega+\omega\otimes 1
in the coproduct using

\frac{1}{2}\left[I(\omega),I(1)\right]+\frac{(-1)^{|\omega|}}{2}\left[I(1),I(\omega)\right]=\left[I(\omega),I(1)\right].

To conclude, recall from
corollary

6.3
that the cochain defined by
I(1)
is equal to the dipole sum
\mathfrak{m}
.

\blacksquare

Example 6.9
.

For
\omega=1
in
Theorem

6.8
, the coproduct is
\Delta 1=1\otimes 1
and thus
\sum_{(\omega)}\omega^{\prime}\otimes\omega^{\prime\prime}=-1\otimes 1
. Stokes’ relation hence amounts to the Maurer-Cartan equation

0=\delta I(1)+\frac{1}{2}[I(1),\mathfrak{m}]=\delta\mathfrak{m}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}]

for the dipole sum
\mathfrak{m}
from (
6.4
). This equation was obtained combinatorially in
[
40
]
, and used there to define the
twisted differential

\delta+[\cdot,\mathfrak{m}]
.
This twist adds
(2i+1)
-valent vertices and inserts
(2i+1)
-fold multiedges, in all possible ways.

Conversely, without advance knowledge of
corollary

6.3
, Stokes’ relation for the bare Pfaffian form says
\delta I(1)=-[I(1),I(1)]/2=-I(1)\circ I(1)
. Applied to the triangle graph

G=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcTri12k}}}\quad\text{with boundary}\quad\partial G=D_{2i+1}

and subgraph
\gamma\cong D_{2i-1}
with quotient
G/\gamma\cong-D_{3}
(see
example

4.7
), we find
I_{D_{2i+1}}(1)=I_{D_{3}}(1)I_{D_{2i-1}}(1)=(I_{D_{3}})^{i}
by induction. Proving
example

6.2
then simplifies to computing a single elementary integral:
I_{D_{3}}(1)=1
(the integrand is
\phi_{D_{3}}
from
section

1.2
).

Corollary 6.10
.

For any integer
i\geq 1
, the cochain
I(\beta^{4i+1})
is closed with respect to the twisted differential:
\delta I(\beta^{4i+1})=-[I(\beta^{4i+1}),\mathfrak{m}]
.

6.3
Classes in graph cohomology

We now show
lemma

1.5
from the introduction. We write
\lceil x\rceil
for the smallest integer larger than or equal to
x
.

Lemma 6.11
.

For a homogeneous canonical form
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{d}
of degree
d
, the integrals
I_{G}(\omega)=0
vanish for all graphs with loop order
\ell(G)<2+2\lceil d/4\rceil
.

Proof.

Suppose
I_{G}(\omega)\neq 0
and let
\ell=\ell(G)
. Then
G
must have
m=1+\ell+d
edges (
corollary

6.4
). By
lemma

5.13
,
G
must be connected, and therefore
G
has
n=m-\ell+1=2+d
vertices, and furthermore each vertex must have degree at least 3, giving
m\geq 3n/2
. This amounts to
\ell\geq 2+d/2
. Since
\ell
must be even (otherwise
\phi_{G}=0
), we conclude
\ell=2\lceil\ell/2\rceil\geq 2\lceil 1+d/4\rceil
.

\blacksquare

We denote the restriction of a cochain
I(\omega)
to graphs with loop order
\ell
as

\gr_{\ell}I(\omega)\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathbb{R},\qquad G\mapsto\begin{cases}I_{G}(\omega)&\text{if $\ell(G)=\ell$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Since
\gr_{\ell}I_{G}(\omega)
is supported in a fixed loop order and fixed degree
k=1+|\omega|-2\ell
(for homogeneous
\omega
), there are only finitely many isomorphism classes of such graphs. Thus
\gr_{\ell}I(\omega)\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}
can be written with an ordinary (not completed) tensor product.

Corollary 6.12
.

For any
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{d}
,
the restriction of
I(\omega)
to graphs with
2+2\lceil d/4\rceil
loops defines a cocycle in
\mathsf{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}
in degree
k\in\{-6,\ldots,-3\}
with
k\equiv d+1\mod 4
.

Proof.

Define
c=2\lceil|\omega|/4\rceil
and likewise
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}
with
\omega
replaced by
\omega^{\prime}
,
\omega^{\prime\prime}
.
The Lie bracket
\gr_{\ell}[I(\omega^{\prime\prime}),I(\omega^{\prime})]=\sum_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}}\left[\gr_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}I(\omega^{\prime\prime}),\gr_{\ell^{\prime}}I(\omega^{\prime})\right]
preserves loop order. Non-vanishing of a summand requires, by
lemma

6.11
, that

\ell^{\prime}\geq 2+c^{\prime}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}\geq 2+c^{\prime\prime}

which imply
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}\geq 4+c
. Hence, applying
\gr_{2+c}
to the Stokes’ relation (
6.9
) kills all Lie brackets, leaving only
0=\delta\gr_{2+c}I(\omega)
. Since
\ell=2+c=2+2\lceil d/4\rceil
, the degree
k=1+d-2\ell
is equal to
-6+((d+3)\mod 4)
, where
d+3\mod 4\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

\blacksquare

Definition 6.13
.

For every integer
i\geq 1
, let
\tau_{i}\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}
denote the cocycle in degree
-6
given by

\tau_{i}\coloneqq\gr_{4+2i}I(\beta^{4i+1})
.

In
section

6.4
we demonstrate that
\tau_{1}
is not exact, so its class spans
\gr_{6}H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\otimes\mathbb{R}
.

Question 6.14
.

Are the classes
[\tau_{i}]\in\gr_{4+2i}H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\otimes\mathbb{R}
non-zero for all
i>0
?

The first such class is a bracket
[\tau_{1}]=[[Y_{3}],[D_{3}]]
of the classes
[D_{3}],[Y_{3}]\in H^{-3}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
of the dipole and prism graph (see
section

6.4
).
In fact, it follows from
[
40
, Theorem 2]
that
\tau_{k}=\delta c_{k}+[c_{k},\mathfrak{m}]
for some (not necessarily closed) cochains
c_{k}\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}
.
24
24

24

Explicit
c_{k}
should be constructible from integrals of primitives
[
7
]
of the Pfaffian forms
\phi_{G}
.
This suggests that perhaps
all
classes
[\tau_{k}]
are brackets with
[D_{3}]
.
If true, this and
[[D_{3}],[D_{3}]]=0
would give another explanation of the following observation.

Lemma 6.15
.

For all integers
i>0
, we have
\Big[[\tau_{i}],[D_{3}]\Big]=0\in H^{-9}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
.

Proof.

Restrict Stokes’ relation from
corollary

6.10
to loop order
\ell=6+2i
. This yields

\delta\Big(\gr_{6+2i}I(\beta^{4i+1})\Big)=-\left[\gr_{4+2i}I(\beta^{4i+1}),\tfrac{1}{12}D_{3}\right]=-\tfrac{1}{12}\left[\tau_{i},D_{3}\right]

where
D_{3}/12=\gr_{2}\mathfrak{m}
. So, indeed,
[[\tau_{i}],[D_{3}]]
is zero in cohomology.

\blacksquare

To illustrate an example where
\omega
is not primitive, consider
\omega=\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}
. The corresponding cocycle
\gr_{10}I(\omega)
from
corollary

6.12
lies in degree
-5
and is trivial, since
\gr_{10}H^{-5}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})=0
(
table

2
). So there is a cochain
c\in\mathsf{GC}_{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}
such that
\gr_{10}I(\omega)=\delta c
. Restricting Stokes’ relation to
\ell=12
loops (instead of
\ell=10
), we find

0=\delta\big(\gr_{12}I(\omega)\big)+\big[\gr_{10}I(\omega),D_{3}/12\big]=\delta\eta\quad\text{where}\quad\eta\coloneqq\gr_{12}I(\omega)+[c,D_{3}/12].

Therefore, we have constructed a cocycle in degree
-9
with
\ell=12
loops (this is in the unknown region of
table

2
). Its cohomology class does not depend on the choice of
c
.

Question 6.16
.

Is the class
[\eta]\in H^{-9}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\otimes\mathbb{R}
non-zero?

Examining Stokes’ relation for
\omega=\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}
at
\ell=14
loops, we learn that

0=\delta\big(\gr_{14}I(\omega)\big)+[\gr_{12}I(\omega),D_{3}^{\vee}]+[\gr_{10}I(\omega),D_{5}^{\vee}]+[\tau_{2},\tau_{1}]

where
D_{3}^{\vee}\coloneqq D_{3}/12=\gr_{2}\mathfrak{m}
and
D_{5}^{\vee}\coloneqq D_{5}/240=\gr_{4}\mathfrak{m}
.
By the (graded) Jacobi identity and
\delta D_{5}^{\vee}=-[D_{3}^{\vee},D_{3}^{\vee}]/2
from the Maurer-Cartan equation for
\mathfrak{m}
, we can replace

[\gr_{10}I(\omega),D_{5}^{\vee}]=\delta[c,D_{5}^{\vee}]-[c,\delta D_{5}^{\vee}]=\delta[c,D_{5}^{\vee}]+[[c,D_{3}^{\vee}],D_{3}^{\vee}].

Therefore, the class
[\eta]
constructed from
\beta^{5}\wedge\beta^{9}
is related to the classes
[\tau_{1}]
and
[\tau_{2}]
constructed from
\beta^{5}
and
\beta^{9}
through the Lie algebra structure on graph cohomology as

\Big[[\tau_{1}],[\tau_{2}]\Big]=\tfrac{1}{12}\Big[[\eta],[D_{3}]\Big]\quad\in H^{-12}(\mathsf{GC}_{3}).

(6.11)

6.4
A class in degree
-6

Let
X\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
denote the chain shown in
fig.

4
.
25
25

25

In terms of the graphs defined in
table

3
and the
ancillary files that accompany this paper
, this element is (row by row, and within each row from left to right)

X=G_{266}+G_{103}+G_{106}-2G_{107}-G_{109}+G_{99}+G_{101}+G_{112}+G_{236}-G_{195}-\tfrac{1}{3}G_{198}-G_{199}-G_{234}+G_{87}-\tfrac{1}{3}G_{288}+G_{35}+G_{175}+G_{247}-2G_{7}-G_{22}
.

\displaystyle+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc266}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc103}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc106}}}-2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc107}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc109}}}

\displaystyle+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc99}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc101}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc112}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc236}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc195}}}

Figure 4:
A graph cycle representing the homology class
\gr_{6}H_{-6}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})\cong\mathbb{Q}
.

With a computer program we verified that
X
is a cycle (
\partial X=0
) and that it spans the one-dimensional 6-loop part
\gr_{6}H_{-6}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})=\mathbb{Q}\cdot[X]
of the homology group in degree
-6
.

This cycle pairs non-trivially with the cocycle
[Y_{3},D_{3}]
obtained in
example

4.8
from the prism and theta graphs. Only two graphs are shared between the cocycle and
X
, so
26
26

26

The factors
2
and
6
are sizes of automorphism groups (
2
from the double-edge) in (
4.2
).

\displaystyle\left\langle[Y_{3},D_{3}],X\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle[Y_{3},D_{3}],\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/gc112}}}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{3}\left\langle[Y_{3},D_{3}],\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/gc288}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-1\cdot 2\cdot 72-\frac{1}{3}\cdot 6\cdot 24=-192.

(6.12)

Therefore, the homology class
[X]\in H_{-6}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})
and the cohomology class
[[Y_{3}],[D_{3}]]\in H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
are clearly non-zero. With the knowledge of the ranks from
table

2
, we conclude that the bracket with
D_{3}
restricts to an isomorphism
\gr_{4}H^{-3}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\cong\gr_{6}H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
, as predicted by the spectral sequence from
[
40
, Table 2, second arrow from the left]
.

Remark 6.17
.

This cohomology class is also representable by
[K_{4},K_{4}]=2K_{4}\circ K_{4}
, the bracket of the complete graph
K_{4}
with itself. Explicitly, these are

K_{4}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gcK4}}}\quad\text{and}\quad[K_{4},K_{4}]=-192\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc288}}}-288\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc287}}}

and one reads off that

\left\langle[K_{4},K_{4}],X\right\rangle=(1/3)\cdot 6\cdot 192=384
. Thus, in the cohomology group
H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})
, there is a relation

[[K_{4}],[K_{4}]]=-2[[Y_{3}],[D_{3}]]
.

We computed the canonical integrals
\tau_{1}(G)
for all 288 graphs that define non-zero elements of
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
at
6
loops and degree
-6
(that is,
12
edges). The calculations are detailed in
appendix

A
; for each graph,
\tau_{1}(G)
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combination of the numbers

1,\ \pi^{2},\ \zeta(3),\ \pi^{2}\ln 2,\ \pi^{4},\ (\ln 2)^{4}+24\operatorname{Li}_{4}(1/2),\ \text{and}\ \pi^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{2}(\mathrm{i})+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i}).

(6.13)

Our results show in particular that the cocycle
\tau_{1}
does detect the homology class
[X]
:

Theorem 6.18
.

For
X\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
shown in
fig.

4
, the canonical integral is

\tau_{1}(X)=I_{X}(\beta^{5})=40\cdot\left(13\zeta(3)-2\pi^{2}\ln 2\right).

(6.14)

Comparing with (
6.12
), we can formulate this result also as follows: The cohomology class defined by the cocycle
\tau_{1}=\gr_{6}I(\beta^{5})\colon\mathcal{GC}_{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is equal to

[\tau_{1}]=\frac{5}{24}\left(2\pi^{2}\ln 2-13\zeta(3)\right)\cdot\Big[[Y_{3}],[D_{3}]\Big]\in H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\otimes\mathbb{R}.

Written in terms of
[K_{4},K_{4}]
as in
remark

6.17
, we get the form stated in the introduction.

It is striking to observe the simplicity of
\tau_{1}(X)
as compared to the individual integrals
\tau_{1}(G)
. Most polylogarithms cancel, and only an alternating sum of weight 3 remains:

2\pi^{2}\ln 2-13\zeta(3)=8\operatorname{Li}_{1,2}(-1,-1)=8\sum_{0<m<n}\frac{(-1)^{n+m}}{mn^{2}}.

Assuming that the 7 numbers in (
6.13
) are linearly independent over
\mathbb{Q}
, the fact that
\tau_{1}
is a cocycle (with values in
\mathbb{R}
) implies that each of the 7 coefficient functions
\lambda_{i}\colon\mathcal{GC}_{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
in (
A.3
) is a cocycle as well. Indeed, we verified that each
\lambda_{i}
as given in
table

3
is a cocycle. The simplification in (
6.14
) thus amounts to the exactness of each
\lambda_{i}
with
i\in\{1,2,5,6,7\}
, as well as the exactness of the combination
\lambda_{3}-(4/3)\lambda_{4}
from rewriting

\lambda_{3}\zeta(3)+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{6}\Big(2\pi^{2}\ln 2-21\zeta(3)\Big)=\Big(\lambda_{3}-\frac{4}{3}\lambda_{4}\Big)\zeta(3)+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{6}\Big(2\pi^{2}\ln 2-13\zeta(3)\Big)

in order to adapt our basis from (
A.3
) to include the particular combination of
\pi^{2}\ln 2
and
\zeta(3)
that survives in the integral of the cycle
X
.

Remark 6.19
.

Similarly, each cocycle
\tau_{k}(G)=\sum_{i}\lambda_{k,i}(G)p_{k,i}
takes values in some finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space of real numbers that are periods in the sense of
[
41
]
. The coefficient functions in any basis
\{p_{k,i}\}
provide rational cocycles
\lambda_{k,i}
. A
single
cocycle
\tau_{k}
might thereby supply
several
rational cohomology classes
[\lambda_{k,i}]
. For example, these coefficients of
[\tau_{2}]
might span a subspace of dimension
0
,
1
, or
2
in
\gr_{8}H^{-6}(\mathsf{GC}_{3})\cong\mathbb{Q}^{2}
.

Technically, this can be implemented (avoiding transcendence conjectures) by promoting the cocycles to taking values in the ring of motivic periods, similar to
[
22
, §9]
.

Remark 6.20
.

Any element
h\in\mathcal{GC}_{3}
has a unique decomposition
h=h_{s}+h_{m}
into a simple part
h_{s}
(no multiedges) and the part
h_{m}
from graphs with one or more multiedges. The latter span a subcomplex that is almost acyclic
[
64
, Theorem 2]
; its homology is spanned by the theta graph
D_{3}
. For
\ell>2
loops, the class
[h]\in H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{GC}_{3})
is therefore determined by
h_{s}
. In the case of
h=X
the cycle from
fig.

4
, this simple part is

X_{s}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc266}}}-\frac{1}{3}\times\!\!\!\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc288}}}.

However, canonical integrals of graphs with multiedges can be non-zero (see
appendix

A
). Hence to obtain
[\tau_{1}]([X])
, it was not enough to only compute the integrals of the two graphs in
X_{s}
; instead, we also needed the multiedge part
X_{m}=X-X_{s}
. In contrast, for the acyclic subcomplex of graphs with cut vertices the situation is better, see
remark

5.14
.

Appendix A
Integrals at 6 loops and 12 edges

This appendix lists the integrals
\tau_{1}(G)=I_{G}(\beta^{5})
for all connected graphs
G
with
6
loops,
12
edges, and at least 3 edges incident at any vertex. The number of isomorphism classes of such graphs which do not have any odd automorphisms, i.e. the dimension of
\mathcal{GC}_{3}
in this bidegree
(\ell,k)=(6,-6)
, is
288
by
[
3
, Table 3]
. We confirmed this number using Sage
[
32
]
and
nauty

[
49
]
, and we computed the corresponding Pfaffians and traces with
Maple
. For all 288 graphs, we find that

\phi_{G}\wedge\omega^{5}_{G}=\frac{Q_{G}\Omega_{12}}{\Psi_{G}^{5/2}}

(A.1)

for a homogeneous polynomial
Q_{G}\in\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{12}]
of degree
3
. Thus for these graphs, the bound from
Theorem

2.10
is not optimal: we observe the cancellation of an additional power of
\Psi_{G}
.

For most graphs, the product
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega^{5}_{G}
is in fact zero, and therefore
\tau_{1}(G)=0
. Most of these zeroes are explained by the presence of a 2-edge cut, according to
lemma

5.13
.

Only 45 graphs give a non-zero integrand
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega^{5}_{G}
. These graphs are listed in
table

3
. We write their adjacency lists as
B_{0}|\cdots|B_{6}
, with each block
B_{i}
encoding the edges pointing away from vertex
i
, represented by the vertex they are pointing towards. For example, the graph
G_{199}
with adjacency list

445|446|556|456|||
has the edges

0\rightarrow 4,0\rightarrow 4,0\rightarrow 5,1\rightarrow 4,1\rightarrow 4,1\rightarrow 6,2\rightarrow 5,2\rightarrow 5,2\rightarrow 6,3\rightarrow 4,3\rightarrow 5,3\rightarrow 6.

Whitney flips relate several of these graphs and such pairs have identical
\tau_{1}(G)
—up to sign—by (
5.8
). We furthermore exploit Stokes’ relations
\tau_{1}(\partial H)=0
. For example, we have
\tau_{1}(G_{267})=-\tau_{1}(G_{112}+G_{185}+G_{259}+G_{264}+G_{266})
from the boundary

\displaystyle\partial\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gcImg185}}}

\displaystyle=-2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc112}}}-2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc185}}}-2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc259}}}

(A.2)

\displaystyle-2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc264}}}-2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc266}}}-2\times\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.7}]{graphs/gc267}}}.

Combining all these relations, the 45 values
\tau_{1}(G)
can be expressed in terms of only 14 remaining integrals.
To compute these integrals, we used a range of methods for Feynman integrals, which we illustrate in this appendix. The results are summarized in
table

3
.

For all 45 graphs, there are integer vectors
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{7}
such that

\displaystyle\tau_{1}(G)

\displaystyle=10\cdot\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{1}}{3}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{9}\pi^{2}+\lambda_{3}\zeta(3)+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{6}\Big(2\pi^{2}\ln 2-21\zeta(3)\Big)+\frac{\lambda_{5}}{180}\pi^{4}

(A.3)

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda_{6}}{3}\Big((\ln 2)^{4}+24\operatorname{Li}_{4}(1/2)\Big)+\frac{\lambda_{7}}{9}\Big(\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})\Big)\bigg)

is a linear combination of special values of polylogarithms.
27
27

27

We combine
\pi^{2}\ln 2
with
-21\zeta(3)/2
for two reasons: 1) this sets
\lambda_{3}=0
for many graphs; 2) this particular linear combination is a motivic Galois conjugate of
(\ln 2)^{4}+24\operatorname{Li}_{4}(1/2)
and thus makes it more easily apparent that
table

3
supports a generalisation of the coaction principle
[
52
]
.
Here
\zeta(3)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}1/n^{3}\approx\numprint{1.202057}
is Apéry’s constant and
\mathcal{C}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{2}(\mathrm{i})
denotes Catalan’s constant, thus

\mathcal{C}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n+1)^{2}}\approx\numprint{0.915966}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n+1)^{4}}\approx\numprint{0.988945}.

In addition to the derivations explained below, all results in
table

3
, with the exception of the graphs
G_{266}
and
G_{267}
, have furthermore been confirmed independently within a precision of roughly 5 digits by numeric integration of (
A.1
) using pySecDec
[
13
]
.

table

), the canonical forms (the numerator polynomials
Q_{G}
of (
A.1
)) and the program used to compute them, are available and documented in plain text files (in a format compatible with Maple) deposited at

https://dx.doi.org/10.5287/ora-ngnborbr4

Table 3:
The canonical Pfaffian integrals
\tau_{1}(G)
of graphs
G
with

). The vanishing for

and

.

A.1
Momentum space propagators

Canonical integrals can be interpreted as linear combinations of propagator Feynman integrals:
since the Symanzik polynomial
\Psi_{G}=x_{e}\Psi_{G\setminus e}+\Psi_{G/e}
is linear with respect to any variable, the integral over a single variable in a graph with
N
edges gives

\int_{\sigma_{N}}\frac{\Omega_{N}}{\Psi_{G}^{D/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{n_{i}-1}=\frac{\Gamma(n_{e})\Gamma(D/2-n_{e})}{\Gamma(D/2)}\int_{\sigma_{N-1}}\frac{\Omega_{N-1}}{\Psi_{G\setminus e}^{n_{e}\phantom{/}}\Psi_{G/e}^{D/2-n_{e}}}\prod_{i\neq e}x_{i}^{n_{i}-1}

for any monomial
\prod_{i}x_{i}^{n_{i}-1}
in the numerator. The right-hand side can be identified with a massless Feynman integral of the graph
G\setminus e
, considered with two external legs attached at the endpoints of the deleted edge
e
, carrying a momentum
p
with norm
\lVert p\rVert=1

[
59
,
51
]
. Such integrals are heavily used in field theory calculations, for an overview of techniques see
[
44
]
. They admit a momentum space representation

\mathcal{I}(G,n,D)=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(G)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}q_{i}}{\pi^{D/2}}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{E(G)}\frac{1}{\lVert k_{j}\rVert^{2n_{j}}}

(A.4)

where the integral is over vectors
q_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
that parametrize the loop space
H_{1}(G)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{D}
. The vectors
k_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
are assigned to edges and denote a linear combination of loop momenta
q_{i}
and the external momentum
p
. For example, the “bubble” integral is

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/bubble}}},n_{1},n_{2},D\right)

\displaystyle=\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}q_{1}}{\pi^{D/2}}\frac{1}{\lVert q_{1}\rVert^{2n_{1}}\lVert q_{1}-p\rVert^{2n_{2}}}\right|_{\lVert p\rVert=1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(D/2-n_{1})\Gamma(D/2-n_{2})\Gamma(n_{1}+n_{2}-D/2)}{\Gamma(n_{1})\Gamma(n_{2})\Gamma(D-n_{1}-n_{2})}

(A.5)

Accounting for all prefactors, the precise relation between a canonical integral and a propagator Feynman integral of the form (
A.4
) is

\int_{\sigma_{N}}\frac{\Omega_{N}}{\Psi_{G}^{D/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{n_{i}-1}=\mathcal{I}(G\setminus e,n,D)\cdot\frac{1}{\Gamma(D/2)}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma(n_{i}).

(A.6)

For example, the integral of the invariant Pfaffian form for the 3-edge dipole graph is

\int_{\sigma_{3}}\phi_{D_{3}}=-\int_{\sigma_{3}}\frac{\Omega_{3}}{\Psi_{D_{3}}^{3/2}}=-\frac{\Gamma(1)^{3}}{\Gamma(3/2)}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/bubble}}},1,1,3\right)=-\frac{\Gamma(1/2)^{3}}{\Gamma(3/2)}=-2\pi

in agreement with
example

6.2
.

Consider now the integral (
A.1
) for the graph
G_{199}
with adjacency list
445|446|556|456|||
.
The numerator of
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega^{5}_{G}
is in this case
Q_{G}=10(x_{1}+x_{2})(x_{4}+x_{5})(x_{7}+x_{8})
. Since
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
correspond to parallel edges
\{0,4\}
, swapping
x_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{2}
leaves
\Psi_{G}
invariant, and similarly for
x_{4}\leftrightarrow x_{5}
and
x_{7}\leftrightarrow x_{8}
. All 8 monomials obtained by expanding
Q_{G}
therefore yield the same integral and can be combined into

\tau_{1}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc199}}}\right)=\frac{10}{(-2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\sigma_{12}}\frac{8x_{1}x_{4}x_{7}\Omega_{12}}{\Psi_{G}^{5/2}}=\frac{80}{(-2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{\Gamma(5/2)}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/pf199p}}},5\right).

Here we chose to delete the edge
\{2,6\}
, and we adopt the convention that
n_{i}=1
for all edges, unless a different value is indicated explicitly next to an edge.
We may replace

•

a sequential pair

of edges with a single edge of index
n_{1}+n_{2}
, and

•

a parallel pair

with a single edge of index
n_{1}+n_{2}-D/2
and a factor (
A.5
).

Applying these series-parallel reductions, the example simplifies to

\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/pf199p}}},5\right)=\left[\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/bubble12}}},5\right)\right]^{3}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/pf199pred}}},5\right).

The bubble integrals on the right-hand side each evaluate to
\pi^{3/2}/2
via (
A.5
). We have therefore expressed the canonical integral of the graph
G_{199}
as a 2-loop propagator,

\tau_{1}(G_{199})=-10\cdot\frac{\pi}{6}\cdot\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/pf199pred}}},5\right).

A.2
Hypergeometric functions

The 2-loop propagator integral above can be expressed in terms of hypergeometric functions. One such formula was derived in
[
16
]
, which in this case shows that

\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/pf199pred}}},5-2\varepsilon\right)=-8\pi+\frac{16}{\pi\varepsilon}\left(\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2}+{}_{3}F_{2}\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\tfrac{1}{2},1,-\tfrac{1}{2}+3\dimeps}{\tfrac{3}{2}+\dimeps,\tfrac{1}{2}+2\dimeps};1\right]\right)+O(\varepsilon).

(A.7)

In order to take the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, we expand the
{}_{3}F_{2}
function in
\varepsilon
using the algorithms from
[
38
]
. Their implementation
HypExp 2
readily calculates that

{}_{3}F_{2}\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\tfrac{1}{2},1,-\tfrac{1}{2}+3\dimeps}{\tfrac{3}{2}+\dimeps,\tfrac{1}{2}+2\dimeps};1\right]=\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon\left(2+\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\left(4-\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}-\frac{21\zeta(3)}{2}\right)+O(\varepsilon^{3})

and therefore the propagator integral (
A.7
) evaluates at
\varepsilon=0
to
48/\pi-4\pi
. In conclusion, the canonical integral of
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega^{5}_{G}/(-2\pi)^{3}
for the graph
G_{199}
takes the value

\tau_{1}(G_{199})=10\cdot\left(\frac{2}{3}\pi^{2}-8\right)\approx-\numprint{14.202637}.

In the same way we can reduce another canonical integral to a 2-loop propagator. The graph
G_{100}
with adjacency list
335|466|566|46|5||
has numerator
Q_{G}=-10(x_{1}+x_{2})(x_{5}+x_{6})(x_{8}+x_{9})
which again raises the index to
n_{i}=2
for one edge in each of the 3 pairs of double-edges. Therefore,

\tau_{1}(G_{100})=\tau_{1}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc100}}}\right)=10\cdot\frac{\pi}{6}\cdot\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/pf100p}}},5\right).

Using again the formula from
[
16
]
, we can express the propagator integral in terms of hypergeometric functions, after introducing a regulator
\varepsilon
. This gives a linear combination

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/pf100peps}}},5-2\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=A_{1}(\varepsilon)+A_{2}(\varepsilon)\cdot{}_{3}F_{2}\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}+2\dimeps}{\tfrac{3}{2},1+2\dimeps};1\right]

\displaystyle\quad+A_{3}(\varepsilon)\cdot{}_{3}F_{2}\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,-\dimeps,-\tfrac{1}{2}+2\dimeps}{\tfrac{3}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}+\dimeps};1\right]

where
A_{i}(\varepsilon)
are expressions in terms of
\Gamma
-functions that are easily expanded in
\varepsilon
and which have triple poles at
\varepsilon=0
. We thus need to expand these
{}_{3}F_{2}
functions to order
\varepsilon^{3}
. Unfortunately the implementation
HypExp 2
does not cover these functions, which belong to the case referred to as
3^{1}_{2}
in
[
38
, §B.5]
.
We therefore apply a different strategy: use a standard integral representation of hypergeometric functions, concretely

{}_{3}F_{2}\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{1}{2}+2\dimeps}{\tfrac{3}{2},1+2\dimeps};1\right]=\frac{\Gamma(1+2\varepsilon)H(\varepsilon)}{2\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon)\sqrt{\pi}},\quad H(\varepsilon)=\int_{0}^{1}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t_{1}}{\sqrt{1-t_{1}}}\int_{0}^{1}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t_{2}}{\sqrt{t_{2}}}\left(\frac{1-t_{1}t_{2}}{1-t_{2}}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon},

and expand the integrand in
\varepsilon
to integrate term-by-term. These integrands are polynomials in
\log(1-t_{2})
and
\log(1-t_{1}t_{2})
, with coefficients that are rational functions of
t_{1},t_{2}
and the square roots
\sqrt{t_{2}}
,
\sqrt{1-t_{1}}
,
\sqrt{1-t_{2}}
, and
\sqrt{1-t_{1}t_{2}}
. We parametrize

t_{1}=1-\left(\frac{(1-u^{2})v}{(1-v^{2})u}\right)^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad t_{2}=\left(\frac{2u}{1+u^{2}}\right)^{2}

(A.8)

so that all four square roots become rational functions of the coordinates
u
and
v
. After this change of variables, we obtain

H(\varepsilon)=8\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u}\int_{0}^{u}\!\mathrm{d}v\frac{(1+v^{2})^{2-4\varepsilon}(1-u^{2})^{2}}{(1-v^{2})^{3-4\varepsilon}(1+u^{2})^{2}}=8\int_{0}^{1}\!\mathrm{d}v\frac{(1+v^{2})^{2-4\varepsilon}}{(1-v^{2})^{3-4\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{v^{2}-1}{1+v^{2}}-\ln v\right).

We used
HyperInt

[
53
]
to express the
\varepsilon
-expansion of this integral in terms of hyperlogarithms (iterated integrals) with letters in
\{0,\pm 1,\pm\mathrm{i}\}\subset\mathbb{C}
. This produces a large expression, but such iterated integrals with 4th roots of unity are well-understood
[
31
]
and fulfil many linear relations. We used the implementation in
HyperlogProcedures

[
57
]
of the decomposition algorithm
[
18
]
into the
parity
f
-alphabet

[
52
]
to simplify the expressions.

In a similar way, we can compute the
\varepsilon
-expansion of the other
{}_{3}F_{2}
function

{}_{3}F_{2}\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{1,-\dimeps,-\tfrac{1}{2}+2\dimeps}{\tfrac{3}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}+\dimeps};1\right]=\frac{\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)}{2\Gamma(-\varepsilon)\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon)}\int_{0}^{1}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t_{1}}{\sqrt{1-t_{1}}}\int_{0}^{1}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}t_{2}}{t_{2}^{1+\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1-t_{1}t_{2}}{1-t_{2}}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon}.

An additional complication here is caused by the divergence of the integral over
t_{2}
when
\varepsilon=0
. This translates into a singularity near
u=0
in the parametrization (
A.8
). To expand in
\varepsilon
, we add
0=u-\int_{0}^{u}\mathrm{d}v
to split the integral as

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{2^{-\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}u}{u^{2+2\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{u}\!\mathrm{d}v\frac{(1+v^{2})^{2-4\varepsilon}(1-u^{2})^{2}}{(1-v^{2})^{3-4\varepsilon}(1+u^{2})^{1-2\varepsilon}}\\
=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{2^{-\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}u}{u^{2+2\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{u}\!\mathrm{d}v\left(\frac{(1+v^{2})^{2-4\varepsilon}(1-u^{2})^{2}}{(1-v^{2})^{3-4\varepsilon}(1+u^{2})^{1-2\varepsilon}}-1\right)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon 2^{1+\varepsilon}}.

The integral in the second row can be expanded in
\varepsilon
on the integrand level, since the inner
\mathrm{d}v
-integral vanishes with order
u^{2}
for small
u
, cancelling the singularity of the outer
\mathrm{d}u
-integration. Applying the same steps as before, we can thus calculate the
\varepsilon
-expansion in terms of hyperlogarithms at 4th roots of unity.

Putting everything together, we obtain a surprisingly simple result, namely

\tau_{1}(G_{100})=10\cdot\left(-\frac{8}{3}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}+\frac{2}{9}\left[\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})\right]\right)\approx\numprint{13.267735}.

(A.9)

The appearance of the specific combination
\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})
in Feynman integrals in odd dimensions was observed already in
[
37
]
, using very high precision numerics.

A.3
Integration by parts

Consider the graph
G_{110}
with edge list
556|445|356|46|6||
. It has two double-edges,
\{0,5\}
(variables
x_{1},x_{2}
) and
\{1,4\}
(variables
x_{4},x_{5}
). The numerator is
Q=10x_{11}(x_{1}+x_{2})(x_{4}+x_{5})
, where the Schwinger parameter
x_{11}
corresponds to the edge
\{3,6\}
.
Proceeding as in
section

A.1
, we find

\tau_{1}(G_{110})=\tau_{1}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc110}}}\right)=\frac{10\cdot 4}{(-2\pi)^{3}\Gamma(\tfrac{5}{2})}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf110p}}},5\right)=\frac{-5}{3\sqrt{\pi}}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf110pred}}},5\right)

where we chose to delete edge
\{1,5\}
and integrated out the bubbles using (
A.5
). To compute this 3-loop propagator integral, we apply the triangle rule
[
27
, §4.3]
. It gives

\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf110pred}}},D\right)=\frac{2\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.4}]{graphs/2buba}}},D\right)-2\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.4}]{graphs/2bubd}}},D\right)+\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.4}]{graphs/2bubb}}},D\right)-\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.4}]{graphs/2bubc}}},D\right)}{5-D}

which we expand in
D=5-2\varepsilon
. Integrating out the parallel edges (bubbles) with (
A.5
), this expression simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf110pred}}},5\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon=0}\bigg[\frac{\pi^{3/2}}{4}\Big(-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\gamma_{\text{E}}-\ln 4+2\Big)\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.45}]{graphs/2bubared}}},5-2\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}-\gamma_{\text{E}}-\ln 4+4\Big)\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.45}]{graphs/2bubbred}}},5-2\varepsilon\right)\bigg]-\pi^{3/2}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.45}]{graphs/pf100p}}},5\right)

where
\gamma_{\text{E}}\approx\numprint{0.577216}
denotes the Euler-Mascheroni constant. The last integral is (
A.9
) divided by
5\pi/3
, and the
\varepsilon
-expansions of the two integrals inside the limit can be computed with
HypExp 2
as explained in
section

A.2
for (
A.7
). The final result is

\tau_{1}(G_{110})=10\cdot\left(\frac{8}{3}-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3}-7\zeta(3)+\frac{2}{3}\pi^{2}\ln 2+\frac{2}{9}\left[\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})\right]\right)\approx-\numprint{4.834340}.

The same strategy applies to the graph
G_{103}
with edge list
566|344|456|56|6||
. The result turns out to be exactly the same, up to the sign:
\tau_{1}(G_{103})=-\tau_{1}(G_{110})
.

The graph
G_{234}
with edge list
112|46|56|456|6|6|
has numerator
Q=10x_{11}x_{12}(x_{1}+x_{2})
. This amounts to doubled propagators
n_{11}=n_{12}=2
on the edges
\{4,6\}
and
\{5,6\}
. After cutting edge
\{0,2\}
and integrating out the bubble of parallel edges
\{0,1\}
, the corresponding Feynman integral is

\tau_{1}(G_{234})=\tau_{1}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc234}}}\right)=-\frac{5}{3\pi^{2}}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf234p}}},5\right).

This can be reduced to 2-loop propagators by applying the triangle rule twice, and those remaining propagators can again be expanded in
\varepsilon
as in
section

A.2
. The result reads

\displaystyle\tau_{1}(G_{234})

\displaystyle=10\cdot\bigg(-8+\frac{14\pi^{2}}{9}+21\zeta(3)-2\pi^{2}\ln 2-\frac{2\pi^{4}}{15}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad-\frac{4}{9}\left[\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})\right]+\frac{2}{3}\left[(\ln 2)^{4}+24\operatorname{Li}_{4}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\right]\bigg)\approx-\numprint{2.072043}.

Consider now the graph
G_{227}
with edge list
355|455|346|6|6|6|
. Its numerator polynomial is
Q=10x_{9}(x_{2}+x_{3})(x_{5}+x_{6})
. Cut edge
\{1,4\}
and integrate out both bubbles, to get

\tau_{1}(G_{227})=\tau_{1}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc227}}}\right)=-\frac{5}{3\sqrt{\pi}}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p}}},5\right).

Applying the triangle rule to this graph directly does not help, since one of the 4 terms it produces still has the full set of edges.
28
28

28

The indices change only by integers, hence the edge with a half-integer index can never be contracted.

Instead, we can apply the method of uniqueness
[
39
,
36
]
to replace trivalent vertices whose indices sum up to
n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=5/2
by triangles (denoted
Y\mapsto\Delta
) with indices
5/2-n_{i}
(summing up to
5
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p}}},5\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p2}}},5\right)\underset{Y\mapsto\Delta}{=}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p3}}},5\right)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p4}}},5\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p5}}},5\right)\underset{\Delta\mapsto Y}{=}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p6}}},5\right)\underset{Y\mapsto\Delta}{=}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4}\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p7}}},5\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/pf227p8}}},5\right).

In the last graph, all 3 edges whose indices get reduced by the triangle rule have integer indices, hence by applying the triangle rule twice, this integral can again be reduced to 2-loop propagators and thus computed as before. The final result is

\tau_{1}(G_{227})=10\cdot\left(-8-\frac{8}{9}\pi^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\pi^{4}\right)\approx-\numprint{5.381332}.

With the techniques illustrated above we also computed the canonical integrals of the graphs
G_{243}
and
G_{246}
(see
table

3
for their definition and results).

For the remaining integrals, we use more involved integration-by-parts relations than just the triangle rule. Assume that
G
has 6 loops and a pair of parallel edges, then:

(i)

Following
section

A.1
, cut an edge and integrate out the parallel pair to write
\tau_{1}(G)
as a linear combination of 4-loop propagators
\mathcal{I}(G^{\prime},n,5)
.

(ii)

Introduce dimensional regularization:
\mathcal{I}(G^{\prime},n,5)=\lim_{\varepsilon=0}\mathcal{I}(G^{\prime},n,5-2\varepsilon)
.

(iii)

Use
LiteRed

[
47
]
to reduce the propagators
\mathcal{I}(G^{\prime},n,5-2\varepsilon)
to the basis of 4-loop master integrals
M_{i}(5-2\varepsilon)
defined in
[
2
, Fig. 2]
.

(iv)

Use
LiteRed
’s dimension shift to replace the master integrals
M_{i}(5-2\varepsilon)
near 5 dimensions by the same integrals in
3-2\varepsilon
dimensions.

(v)

Substitute the
\varepsilon
-expansions of the master integrals
M_{i}(3-2\varepsilon)
near 3 dimensions computed in
[
46
]
.

To illustrate this process, consider again the graph
G_{100}
from
section

A.1
: Integrating out only one of the 3 bubbles, we have

\tau_{1}(G_{100})=10\cdot\frac{\pi}{6}\cdot\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/pf100p}}},5\right)=10\cdot\frac{2}{3\pi^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.6}]{graphs/M21b}}},5\right).

Applying
LiteRed
to this 4-loop propagator
29
29

29

The concrete function call is
IBPReduce[LoweringDRR[p4,0,0,0,0,0,2,2,2,1,0,1,0,2,1]]
.
produces the reduction

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/M21b}}},D+2\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(D-4)(7D-18)}{4(D-1)(3D-10)}M_{13}(D)-\frac{D-4}{4D-4}M_{21}(D)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(2D-5)(5D-12)(9D^{2}-50D+72)}{4(D-3)(D-1)(3D-10)(3D-8)}M_{01}(D)

in terms of 3 master integrals defined by

\displaystyle M_{01}(D)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/M01}}},D\right)=\frac{\Gamma(D/2-1)^{5}\Gamma(5-2D)}{\Gamma(5D/2-5)}

\displaystyle M_{13}(D)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/M13}}},D\right)=\frac{\Gamma(D/2-1)^{6}\Gamma(2-D/2)\Gamma(3-D)\Gamma(2D-5)\Gamma(6-2D)}{\Gamma(D-2)\Gamma(3D/2-3)\Gamma(5-3D/2)\Gamma(5D/2-6)},

\displaystyle M_{21}(D)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{graphs/M21}}},D\right).

The above expressions for
M_{01}
and
M_{13}
in terms of
\Gamma
-functions follow from series-parallel reductions. Expanding in
D=3-2\varepsilon
, these calculations result in

\tau_{1}(G_{100})=10\cdot\left(-\frac{8}{3}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}+\frac{M_{21}(3)}{12\pi^{2}}\right).

Similarly, we can express all other canonical integrals (of graphs that have at least one pair of parallel edges) in terms of 4-loop master integrals in
3-2\varepsilon
dimensions. The latter
\varepsilon
-expansions were computed in
[
46
]
.
30
30

30

In that paper the
\varepsilon
-expansions are computed numerically to 55 significant digits and then fitted to a basis of the expected transcendental numbers. Our calculation only uses very few of those coefficients, for example the expansion of
M_{36}
up to the finite part, which was computed exactly in
[
30
]
.

Remark A.1
.

In the above example, we can reverse the logic, and infer the value of
M_{21}(3)
from our calculation of
\tau_{1}(G_{100})
. Our result (
A.9
) amounts to

\begin{split}M_{21}(3-2\varepsilon)&=\frac{8\pi^{2}}{3}\big[\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})\big]+O(\varepsilon)\\
&=M_{01}(3-2\varepsilon)\cdot\left(-8\varepsilon\big[\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})\big]+O(\varepsilon^{2})\right)\end{split}

and therefore identifies the numeric constant
M_{21}^{(1)}\approx\numprint{-262.199098}
from
[
46
, eq. (A.3)]
with
-8[\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})]
. This identity was pointed out explicitly in
[
54
, eq. (8)]
, and in fact observed already implicitly in
[
37
]
.

A.4
Gegenbauer polynomials

The only remaining graphs not amenable to the above techniques are the simple graphs. The corresponding 5-loop propagators and their master integrals are not known in 5 dimensions, and without any parallel edges, we cannot simply reduce them to 4-loop propagators as we did for all other graphs.

Instead of momentum space, we now use the position space representation

\int_{\sigma_{N}}\frac{\Omega_{N}}{\Psi_{G}^{D/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{n_{i}-1}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(D/2)}\left(\prod_{v\neq v_{0},v_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D}z_{v}}{\pi^{D/2}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(n_{i}^{\star})}{\lVert z_{a(i)}-z_{b(i)}\rVert^{2n_{i}^{\star}}}

(A.10)

where we set
n_{i}^{\star}\coloneqq D/2-n_{i}
. The integral on the right-hand side is over variables
z_{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
associated to each vertex, except for two arbitrary vertices
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
which are fixed at the origin
z_{v_{0}}=0\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
and a unit vector
z_{v_{1}}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
. With
a(i)
and
b(i)
we denote the two endpoints (vertices) of edge
i
. We write
z_{i}=r_{i}\hat{z}_{i}
in polar coordinates

r_{i}\coloneqq\lVert z_{i}\rVert\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{z}_{i}\coloneqq z_{i}/r_{i}.

The method
[
26
]
is based on expanding all propagators into the series

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lVert z_{i}-z_{j}\rVert^{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}C^{\alpha}_{n}(\hat{z}_{i}\cdot\hat{z}_{j})R^{\alpha}_{n}(r_{i},r_{j})\quad\text{with}\quad R^{\alpha}_{n}(s,t)=\begin{cases}\frac{s^{n}}{t^{n+2\alpha}}&\text{if $s<t$,}\\
\frac{t^{n}}{s^{n+2\alpha}}&\text{if $t<s$.}\\
\end{cases}

(A.11)

This form separates the dependence on the lengths of the vectors
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
from the dependence on the enclosed angle
\phi
(the scalar product
\hat{z}_{i}\cdot\hat{z}_{j}=\cos\phi
). The Gegenbauer polynomials
C^{\alpha}_{n}
are defined by the generating series

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}C_{n}^{\alpha}(u)t^{n}=(1-2ut+t^{2})^{-\alpha}.

The spherical angles in the measure
\mathrm{d}^{D}z_{i}=r_{i}^{D-1}\mathrm{d}r_{i}\,\mathrm{d}^{D-1}\hat{z}_{i}
can be integrated out, thanks to the orthogonality relation

\int_{\lVert z_{i}\rVert=1}C_{n}^{\alpha}(\hat{z}_{i}\cdot\hat{z}_{j})C_{m}^{\alpha}(\hat{z}_{i}\cdot\hat{z}_{k})\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D-1}\hat{z}_{i}}{\pi^{D/2}}=\frac{2\alpha}{\Gamma(D/2)}\frac{C^{\alpha}_{n}(\hat{z}_{j}\cdot\hat{z}_{k})}{\alpha+n}\,\delta_{n,m}

(A.12)

and further identities, whereas the integrals over the lengths
r_{i}
produce rational functions of the summation indices
n
in (
A.11
). The end result is a formula for the integral (
A.10
) as a multiple sum, which can be evaluated numerically to high precision.

G_{241}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc241}}}\qquad G_{266}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc266}}}\qquad G_{267}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.8}]{graphs/gc267}}}

Figure 5:
The simple graphs with non-zero integrand
\phi_{G}\wedge\omega_{G}^{5}
.

Consider for example the graph
G_{266}
from
fig.

5
with edge list
456|346|356|6|5|6|
; the variables
x_{2},x_{10},x_{12}
thus correspond to the edges
\{0,5\},\{3,6\},\{5,6\}
, respectively. The numerator is
Q=10\big[x_{10}x_{12}(x_{2}-x_{11})-x_{2}x_{6}x_{9}\big]
and we choose
v_{0}=6
and
v_{1}=5
. For the first of the 3 numerator monomials,
x_{2}x_{10}x_{12}
, the integral (
A.10
) then reads

\frac{\Gamma(3/2)^{9}\Gamma(1/2)^{3}}{\Gamma(5/2)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{4}}{\pi^{5/2}}\frac{1}{\lVert z_{4}-z_{5}\rVert^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{0}}{\pi^{5/2}}\frac{1}{r_{0}^{3}\lVert z_{0}-z_{4}\rVert^{3}\lVert z_{0}-z_{5}\rVert}\\
\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{1}}{\pi^{5/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{2}}{\pi^{5/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{5}z_{3}}{\pi^{5/2}}\frac{1}{r_{1}^{3}r_{2}^{3}r_{3}\lVert z_{5}-z_{2}\rVert^{3}\lVert z_{2}-z_{3}\rVert^{3}\lVert z_{3}-z_{1}\rVert^{3}\lVert z_{1}-z_{4}\rVert^{3}}.

The angular integrals over vertices
1,2,3
are straightforward with (
A.12
):

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}\hat{z}_{1}}{\pi^{5/2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}\hat{z}_{2}}{\pi^{5/2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}\hat{z}_{3}}{\pi^{5/2}}\frac{1}{\lVert z_{5}-z_{2}\rVert^{3}\lVert z_{2}-z_{3}\rVert^{3}\lVert z_{3}-z_{1}\rVert^{3}\lVert z_{1}-z_{4}\rVert^{3}}\\
=\frac{64}{\pi^{3/2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{C^{3/2}_{n}(\hat{z}_{4}\cdot\hat{z}_{5})}{(n+3/2)^{3}}R_{n}^{3/2}(1,r_{2})R_{n}^{3/2}(r_{2},r_{3})R_{n}^{3/2}(r_{3},r_{1})R_{n}^{3/2}(r_{1},r_{4})

(A.13)

where the prefactor is
(4/\sqrt{\pi})^{3}=(3/\Gamma(5/2))^{3}
.
To compute the angular integral over
\hat{z}_{0}
, we replace
C^{1/2}_{n}(\hat{z}_{0}\cdot\hat{z}_{5})
in the expansion (
A.11
) of
1/\lVert z_{0}-z_{5}\rVert
by the identity

C^{1/2}_{n}=\frac{C^{3/2}_{n}-C^{3/2}_{n-2}}{2n+1}

so that we can apply the orthogonality relation (
A.12
) with the polynomials
C^{3/2}_{n}(\hat{z}_{0}\cdot\hat{z}_{4})
from the expansion of
1/\lVert z_{0}-z_{4}\rVert^{3}
. We therefore obtain that

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}\hat{z}_{0}}{\pi^{5/2}}\frac{1}{\lVert z_{0}-z_{4}\rVert^{3}\lVert z_{0}-z_{5}\rVert}\\
=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{C^{3/2}_{m}(\hat{z}_{4}\cdot\hat{z}_{5})}{m+3/2}R_{m}^{3/2}(r_{0},r_{4})\left[\frac{R_{m}^{1/2}(1,r_{0})}{2m+1}-\frac{R_{m+2}^{1/2}(1,r_{0})}{2m+5}\right].

(A.14)

The final angular integral over
\mathrm{d}^{4}\hat{z}_{4}
involves the product of three Gegenbauer polynomials: one from the expansion of
1/\lVert z_{4}-z_{5}\rVert^{3}
, one from (
A.13
), and one from (
A.14
). This

\int C^{3/2}_{n}(\hat{z}_{i}\cdot\hat{z}_{j})C^{3/2}_{m}(\hat{z}_{i}\cdot\hat{z}_{j})C^{3/2}_{l}(\hat{z}_{i}\cdot\hat{z}_{j})\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}\hat{z}_{i}}{\pi^{5/2}}=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Delta(n,m,l)

can be computed as follows
[
26
, Appendix A]
: If
k=(n+m+l)/2
is not an integer, or if any of the triangle inequalities
n\leq m+l
,
m\leq n+l
,
l\leq n+m
is violated, then we have
\Delta(n,m,l)=0
. Otherwise,

\Delta(n,m,l)=\frac{2\Gamma(k+3)}{\pi\Gamma(k+5/2)}\frac{\Gamma(k-n+3/2)}{\Gamma(k-n+1)}\frac{\Gamma(k-m+3/2)}{\Gamma(k-m+1)}\frac{\Gamma(k-l+3/2)}{\Gamma(k-l+1)}.

In conclusion, we obtain a triple sum

\int_{\sigma_{12}}\frac{x_{2}x_{10}x_{12}\Omega_{12}}{\Psi_{G_{266}}^{5/2}}=\frac{(2\pi)^{3}}{3}\sum_{n,m,l=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Delta(n,m,l)F(n,m,l)}{(n+3/2)^{3}(m+3/2)}

where
F(n,m,l)
is a rational function defined by the radial integrals:

F(n,m,l)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!r_{4}^{4}\mathrm{d}r_{4}\;R_{l}^{3/2}(r_{4},1)\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!r_{0}\mathrm{d}r_{0}\;R_{m}^{3/2}(r_{0},r_{4})\bigg[\frac{R_{m}^{1/2}(1,r_{0})}{2m+1}-\frac{R_{m+2}^{1/2}(1,r_{0})}{2m+5}\bigg]\\
\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!r_{1}\mathrm{d}r_{1}\;\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!r_{2}\mathrm{d}r_{2}\;\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!r_{3}^{3}\mathrm{d}r_{3}\;R_{n}^{3/2}(1,r_{2})R_{n}^{3/2}(r_{2},r_{3})R_{n}^{3/2}(r_{3},r_{1})R_{n}^{3/2}(r_{1},r_{4}).

These integrals are easily computed in closed form by subdividing the integration domain into
6!
regions according to the total order of
\{r_{0},r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4},1\}
, since in each such region, all functions
R_{k}^{\alpha}(r_{i},r_{j})
are given by a fixed monomial.

Treating the remaining terms analogously, we obtain a triple sum for
\tau_{1}(G_{266})
. With a
Maple
program we compute the truncations
S(M)=\sum_{n,m\leq M}\sum_{l=\lvert n-m\rvert}^{n+m}(\text{summand})
of these sums up to
M=700
. These truncations exhibit a power law remainder term,

S(M)=\tau_{1}(G_{266})+\frac{1}{M^{3}}\left(c+O\left(\frac{1}{M}\right)\right)

and thus the precision improves only very slowly with growing
M
. With methods to accelerate convergence
31
31

31

We used
[
17
, eq. (21)]
with
C=3
for
G_{266}
, and with
C=5
for
G_{241}
.
we can nevertheless reliably identify 41 significant figures,

\tau_{1}(G_{266})\approx\numprint{0.76007971277105870470418001694287511301749}.

This is ample precision to confirm the relation (found with PSLQ) that

\tau_{1}(G_{266})=10\cdot\bigg(-\frac{8}{3}-\frac{2}{9}\pi^{2}-\frac{4}{3}\pi^{2}\ln(2)-2\zeta(3)-\frac{13}{60}\pi^{4}\\
+\frac{2}{3}\Big(\ln^{4}(2)+24\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\tfrac{1}{2})\Big)+\frac{8}{9}\Big(\pi^{2}\mathcal{C}+24\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\mathrm{i})\Big)\bigg).

(A.15)

With the same method we obtain 41 digits for
\tau_{1}(G_{241})
from another triple sum (choosing again to fix the vertices
v_{0}=6
and
v_{1}=5
). This approximation fits the expression

\tau_{1}(G_{241})=10\cdot\left(-\frac{8}{3}-2\pi^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\pi^{2}\ln(2)-7\zeta(3)+\frac{16}{45}\pi^{4}-\frac{2}{3}(\ln^{4}(2)+24\operatorname{Li}_{4}(\tfrac{1}{2}))\right).

(A.16)

These identifications of
\tau_{1}(G_{241})
and
\tau_{1}(G_{266})
are further confirmed by a Stokes relation, which relates their sum to previously computed integrals:

\tau_{1}(G_{241}+G_{266})=\tau_{1}(G_{238}-G_{236}-G_{265})

We checked that the values from (
A.15
) and (
A.16
) fulfil this identity.

The integral of the 3rd simple graph with non-vanishing integrand,
G_{267}
, is determined by another Stokes relation, namely the boundary given in (
A.2
).
